Jeremy Levy


Abstract




From Qubit to Qubit

A Graduate Course in Quantum Mechanics

Jeremy Levy

Department of Physics and Astronomy

University of Pittsburgh

Illustration by Natalia Valderrama.

Preface

This book is the textbook for a two-semester graduate course in quantum mechanics taught at the University of Pittsburgh (PHYS 2565 and PHYS 2566). The notes have evolved over several years of teaching, and the present arrangement is the one I have settled on.

What distinguishes this treatment from other graduate texts is the order of presentation. The first two chapters develop quantum mechanics within the spin-1/2 system, with the Bloch cube, a six-sided physical object whose faces are the cardinal states of a single qubit, used as a concrete companion to the algebraic notation. Rotations of the cube enact unitary operations; pairs of cubes make entangled states visible; many of the basic calculations can be performed with a cube in hand. Quantum fields are introduced early on, in chapter 3, where the lattice version of a quantum field theory is the natural object once one has many qubits in a row. Three-dimensional angular momentum, the hydrogen atom, and perturbation theory occupy the middle chapters and are developed in the standard way. Bell’s theorem, the Dirac equation, and the renormalization group complete the year. The book closes by returning to the qubit, now as a logical qubit protected by quantum error correction; the threshold for fault-tolerant computation appears as an unstable fixed point of the renormalization group, which is what the title points at.

The discrete-to-continuous transition is a recurring theme: in Hilbert space, when finite- dimensional qubits give way to infinite-dimensional state spaces; in real space, when lattice models give way to wave mechanics; in time, when discrete unitary steps give way to the Schrödinger equation; and finally in scale, when block-spin renormalization group transformations give way to continuous rescaling and to continuous variation in the number of spatial dimensions.

Viewed this way, the book differs from a standard graduate text in its global topology rather than its local content. Each section taken individually is conventional and could appear in any other textbook; this is by design. The order, however, closes into a loop: the qubit at the start identifies with the qubit at the end, the way coordinates on a circle wrap back to themselves rather than running off to infinity on a line. The path between them, through lattice fields to wave mechanics to relativistic dynamics to scaling, is the rearrangement, and many of the connections that this rearrangement makes visible are harder to see when the same material is presented linearly.

The treatment is largely self-contained. A reader with a working command of linear algebra, complex analysis, multivariable calculus, and the standard undergraduate sequence of mechanics and electromagnetism should be able to follow it. Many sections are accessible to advanced undergraduates; the later material assumes more mathematical maturity. Researchers in quantum information, condensed matter, or atomic physics may find the integrated treatment useful as a single reference for material that is usually scattered.

Each chapter uses a small set of pedagogical elements. Key Idea boxes mark the central conceptual moves. Examples work calculations end to end. Bloch Cube Activities are short physical exercises meant to be done with a cube in hand. Mathematical Asides give technical derivations that can be skipped on a first reading. Physical Insights connect the formalism to experiment.

I am grateful to the students whose questions and frustrations have shaped this presentation, and to colleagues who have read earlier drafts. Special thanks are due to Chandralekha Singh for the collaborative development of the pedagogical approach, and to the American Journal of Physics for publishing our paper on the dot-vector formalism and the Bloch cube.

Chapter 1  Foundations via Spin-1/2 Systems

1.1  Introduction: Quantum Foundations via Two-State Systems

Key Idea: The Power of Two-State Systems

The mathematical structure of quantum mechanics reveals itself most clearly through the study of two-state systems. These systems, despite their apparent simplicity, encode all the essential features of quantum theory: superposition, measurement, collapse, and unitary evolution. By developing a thorough understanding of two-state quantum mechanics, we establish a foundation that extends naturally to infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

This course takes a pedagogically distinct approach to graduate quantum mechanics. Rather than beginning with wave mechanics and the Schrödinger equation, we start with finite-dimensional systems where the mathematical structure is transparent and the physical interpretation is unambiguous. One important pedagogical tool will be the “Bloch cube,” a physical representation of quantum states that makes abstract mathematical operations tangible and intuitive.

Figure 1.1
:
Two Bloch Cubes.

The Bloch cube is a six-sided object, similar to a die, where each face represents a distinct quantum state of a two-level system. Through physical manipulation of these cubes, students can perform quantum operations, visualize superposition, and understand measurement in a concrete way before abstracting to the mathematical formalism. This approach bridges the gap between classical intuition and quantum formalism through what we call “dot-vec notation,” a mathematical framework that connects familiar vector operations to the Dirac notation of quantum mechanics.

The power of this approach lies in its ability to make the abstract concrete. When we say that quantum states can be "superposed," what does this really mean? When we claim that measurement "collapses" a quantum state, how can we visualize this process? The Bloch cube provides immediate, tactile answers to these questions. By the end of this chapter, you will understand quantum mechanics not just as a mathematical formalism, but as a physical theory with clear operational meaning.

1.2  Mathematical Preliminaries: Vectors and Dot-Vectors

1.2.1  Review of Two-Dimensional Vector Spaces

We begin with the familiar setting of two-dimensional Euclidean space. Consider orthonormal basis vectors
\hat{x}
and
\hat{y}
satisfying the standard inner product relations:
\hat{x}\cdot\hat{x}=1
,
\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}=0
,
\hat{y}\cdot\hat{x}=0
, and
\hat{y}\cdot\hat{y}=1
. Any vector in this space can be expressed as a linear combination
\vec{V}=V_{x}\hat{x}+V_{y}\hat{y}
, where the components are extracted through the inner product:
V_{x}=\hat{x}\cdot\vec{V}
and
V_{y}=\hat{y}\cdot\vec{V}
.

This elementary construction forms the basis for our approach to quantum mechanics. The key insight is that quantum states behave mathematically like vectors, while measurement processes behave like inner products. To make this connection explicit, we introduce a notational innovation that clarifies the dual roles played by vectors in quantum theory.

Let us explore this vector space more deeply. Consider a general vector
\vec{V}=3\hat{x}+4\hat{y}
. Its magnitude is
|\vec{V}|=\sqrt{3^{2}+4^{2}}=5
, and we can normalize it to create a unit vector:
\hat{V}=\frac{3}{5}\hat{x}+\frac{4}{5}\hat{y}
. The angle this vector makes with the x-axis is
\theta=\arctan(4/3)\approx 53.1
degrees. These familiar calculations from classical mechanics will have direct quantum mechanical analogs.

1.2.2  The Dot-Vector Construction

We now introduce a formal distinction between vectors and what we term “dot-vectors.” For each basis vector, we define a corresponding dot-vector by symbolically attaching the dot operation:
\hat{x}\rightarrow\hat{x}\cdot
and
\hat{y}\rightarrow\hat{y}\cdot
. While this may appear to be merely a notational trick, it serves a profound pedagogical purpose by making explicit the dual nature of vectors in quantum mechanics.

A general dot-vector takes the form
\vec{W}\cdot=W_{x}\hat{x}\cdot+W_{y}\hat{y}\cdot
. The action of a dot-vector on a regular vector produces a scalar through the natural extension of the inner product:
\vec{W}\cdot\vec{V}=W_{x}V_{x}+W_{y}V_{y}
. This operation mirrors the action of a bra on a ket in quantum mechanics, providing students with a concrete mathematical bridge to the more abstract Dirac notation.

To illustrate the power of this notation, consider the dot-vector
\vec{W}\cdot=2\hat{x}\cdot+3\hat{y}\cdot
acting on our previous vector
\vec{V}=3\hat{x}+4\hat{y}
:

\displaystyle\vec{W}\cdot\vec{V}

\displaystyle=(2\hat{x}\cdot+3\hat{y}\cdot)(3\hat{x}+4\hat{y})

(1.1)

\displaystyle=2\cdot 3+3\cdot 4

(1.2)

\displaystyle=6+12=18

(1.3)

This calculation, while elementary, establishes the pattern for all quantum mechanical calculations involving state amplitudes and measurement probabilities.

1.2.3  Operator Construction from Vectors and Dot-Vectors

The true power of the dot-vector notation emerges when we consider products in the reverse order. The expression
\hat{x}\hat{x}\cdot
represents an operator that acts on vectors. To understand its action, consider its effect on an arbitrary vector
\vec{V}
:

(\hat{x}\hat{x}\cdot)\vec{V}=\hat{x}(\hat{x}\cdot\vec{V})=\hat{x}V_{x}=V_{x}\hat{x}

(1.4)

This operator extracts the
x
-component of the vector and returns a vector pointing in the
x
-direction with that magnitude. Similarly,
\hat{y}\hat{y}\cdot
projects onto the
y
-direction.

Let’s examine these projection operators more carefully. Consider their action on our example vector
\vec{V}=3\hat{x}+4\hat{y}
:

\displaystyle(\hat{x}\hat{x}\cdot)\vec{V}

\displaystyle=\hat{x}(\hat{x}\cdot(3\hat{x}+4\hat{y}))=\hat{x}(3)=3\hat{x}

(1.5)

\displaystyle(\hat{y}\hat{y}\cdot)\vec{V}

\displaystyle=\hat{y}(\hat{y}\cdot(3\hat{x}+4\hat{y}))=\hat{y}(4)=4\hat{y}

(1.6)

The sum of these projections recovers the original vector:

[(\hat{x}\hat{x}\cdot)+(\hat{y}\hat{y}\cdot)]\vec{V}=3\hat{x}+4\hat{y}=\vec{V}

(1.7)

This demonstrates that the sum of projection operators yields the identity operator:

\hat{I}=\hat{x}\hat{x}\cdot+\hat{y}\hat{y}\cdot

(1.8)

This decomposition of the identity will later correspond to the completeness relation in quantum mechanics. More generally, we can construct operators that transform between different directions. For instance, the operator
\hat{y}\hat{x}\cdot
maps the
x
-direction to the
y
-direction:
(\hat{y}\hat{x}\cdot)\hat{x}=\hat{y}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{x})=\hat{y}
.

1.3  The Quantum Correspondence

1.3.1  From Dot-Vec to Dirac Notation

We now establish the formal correspondence between our dot-vector notation and the Dirac notation of quantum mechanics. This mapping transforms our concrete two-dimensional vector space into the abstract two-dimensional Hilbert space of a quantum two-state system:

Dot-Vec Notation

Dirac Notation

Physical Meaning

\hat{x}

|+z\rangle

Spin up along z-axis

\hat{y}

|-z\rangle

Spin down along z-axis

\hat{x}\cdot

\langle+z|

Measurement of spin up

\hat{y}\cdot

\langle-z|

Measurement of spin down

\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}

\langle+z|-z\rangle

Transition amplitude

\hat{x}\hat{y}\cdot

\outerproduct{+z}{-z}

Spin flip operator

This correspondence is not merely formal—it captures the deep mathematical structure shared by classical vectors and quantum states. The orthonormality of our basis vectors translates directly to the orthogonality of quantum basis states:
\langle+z|+z\rangle=1
,
\langle-z|-z\rangle=1
, and
\langle+z|-z\rangle=\langle-z|+z\rangle=0
.

1.3.2  Quantum Superposition

The principle of superposition, fundamental to quantum mechanics, emerges naturally in our framework. Just as any two-dimensional vector can be expressed as a linear combination of basis vectors, any quantum state in our two-dimensional Hilbert space can be written as:

|\psi\rangle=\alpha|+z\rangle+\beta|-z\rangle

(1.9)

Here,
\alpha
and
\beta
are complex numbers satisfying the normalization condition
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
. This condition ensures that the total probability of finding the system in some state equals unity. The complex nature of these coefficients, absent in our classical vector analogy, introduces the uniquely quantum phenomenon of phase.

To understand the role of complex coefficients, consider the state:

|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|+z\rangle+\frac{e^{i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{2}}|-z\rangle

(1.10)

The magnitudes
|\alpha|=|\beta|=1/\sqrt{2}
determine measurement probabilities, while the relative phase
e^{i\pi/4}
affects interference between the components. This phase has no classical analog in our dot-vector notation—it represents genuinely quantum behavior.

The coefficients can be extracted using the inner product, just as in the classical case:
\alpha=\langle+z|\psi\rangle
and
\beta=\langle-z|\psi\rangle
. These probability amplitudes encode all information about the quantum state and determine the probabilities of measurement outcomes through the Born rule.

1.4  The Bloch Cube: A Tangible Quantum System

1.4.1  Construction and Concept

We now introduce the central pedagogical tool of our approach: the Bloch cube. This physical object serves as a concrete representation of a quantum two-state system, making abstract quantum concepts tangible and manipulable. The Bloch cube is a regular cube (such as a die) where each of the six faces represents a specific quantum state.

The full Bloch sphere, which represents all possible states of a two-level quantum system, forms a sphere in three-dimensional space. The general state
|\psi\rangle=\cos(\theta/2)|+z\rangle+e^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2)|-z\rangle
corresponds to the point with spherical coordinates
(\theta,\phi)
on this sphere. While this continuum of states is mathematically elegant, it presents pedagogical challenges for beginning students.

The Bloch cube simplifies this picture by restricting attention to six special states that form the vertices of a regular octahedron inscribed within the Bloch sphere. These states correspond to the intersections of the sphere with the three coordinate axes. By limiting ourselves to these six states initially, we can perform all fundamental quantum operations while maintaining a clear physical picture.

1.4.2  The Six Fundamental States

The six faces of the Bloch cube represent the following quantum states:

Along the z-axis, we have
|+z\rangle
and
|-z\rangle
, which serve as our computational basis states. These represent definite values of the z-component of spin for a spin-1/2 particle.

Along the x-axis, we find
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)
and
|-x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)
. These states represent equal superpositions of the z-basis states, differing only in their relative phase.

Finally, along the y-axis, we have
|+y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)
and
|-y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)
. The presence of the imaginary unit
i
in these superpositions introduces a phase difference of
\pi/2
between the components.

1.4.3  Complete Basis Representations

A crucial tool for quantum calculations is the ability to express any state in any basis. Here we provide the complete representation of all six Bloch cube states in all three bases:

Bloch Cube States in All Bases

Z-basis representations:

\displaystyle|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=|+z\rangle

(1.11)

\displaystyle|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=|-z\rangle

(1.12)

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)

(1.13)

\displaystyle|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)

(1.14)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)

(1.15)

\displaystyle|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)

(1.16)

X-basis representations:

\displaystyle|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+x\rangle+|-x\rangle)

(1.17)

\displaystyle|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+x\rangle-|-x\rangle)

(1.18)

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle

(1.19)

\displaystyle|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=|-x\rangle

(1.20)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+i)|+x\rangle+(1-i)|-x\rangle}{2}

(1.21)

\displaystyle|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-i)|+x\rangle+(1+i)|-x\rangle}{2}

(1.22)

Y-basis representations:

\displaystyle|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+y\rangle+|-y\rangle)

(1.23)

\displaystyle|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-i|+y\rangle+i|-y\rangle)

(1.24)

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-i)|+y\rangle+(1+i)|-y\rangle}{2}

(1.25)

\displaystyle|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+i)|+y\rangle+(1-i)|-y\rangle}{2}

(1.26)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=|+y\rangle

(1.27)

\displaystyle|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=|-y\rangle

(1.28)

These representations are essential for calculating transition probabilities between different bases and understanding how the same quantum state appears different when viewed from different measurement perspectives.

1.4.4  Using Cross-Basis Representations

Let’s work through an example that demonstrates the utility of these cross-basis representations. Suppose we want to calculate
\langle+x|+y\rangle
directly using the Z-basis.

Example 1: Cross-Basis Inner Product

Calculate
\langle+x|+y\rangle
using Z-basis representations:

From our table:

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)

(1.29)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)

(1.30)

Therefore:

\displaystyle\langle+x|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\langle+z|+\langle-z|)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)

(1.31)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\langle+z|+z\rangle+i\langle+z|-z\rangle+\langle-z|+z\rangle+i\langle-z|-z\rangle]

(1.32)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[1+0+0+i]

(1.33)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+i)

(1.34)

We can verify this is correct since
|\langle+x|+y\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}
, as expected for adjacent cube faces.

1.4.5  Orthogonality and the Cube Geometry

The geometric structure of the cube encodes the orthogonality relations between quantum states. States on opposite faces of the cube are orthogonal, meaning their inner product vanishes. This can be verified through direct calculation:
\langle+z|-z\rangle=0
,
\langle+x|-x\rangle=0
, and
\langle+y|-y\rangle=0
. This orthogonality has profound physical meaning—orthogonal states can be perfectly distinguished by appropriate measurements.

States on adjacent faces of the cube are not orthogonal. The inner products between adjacent states all have magnitude
1/\sqrt{2}
:

\displaystyle|\langle+z|+x\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle+z|+y\rangle|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.35)

\displaystyle|\langle+x|+y\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle+x|-y\rangle|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.36)

\displaystyle|\langle+y|+z\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle+y|-z\rangle|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.37)

This uniform overlap between adjacent states reflects the high symmetry of the cube arrangement on the Bloch sphere.

Bloch Cube Activity: 1.1: Constructing and Exploring the Bloch Cube

To begin your hands-on exploration of quantum mechanics, first construct or obtain a Bloch cube. You can use a standard six-sided die or create a cube with labeled faces. Label the six faces according to the states described above:
|+z\rangle
,
|-z\rangle
,
|+x\rangle
,
|-x\rangle
,
|+y\rangle
, and
|-y\rangle
. Once you have your Bloch cube, verify the following properties through direct manipulation:

(a)

Opposite faces represent orthogonal states

(b)

Each state can be expressed as a superposition of any pair of orthogonal states

(c)

The cube can be oriented to place any face “up,” representing the current quantum state

(d)

Rotations of the cube correspond to unitary transformations of the quantum state

(e)

Use the basis representation table to verify that
|+x\rangle
is indeed an equal superposition of
|+z\rangle
and
|-z\rangle

1.5  Measurement Theory and the Born Rule

1.5.1  Measurement as Fundamental Process

Measurement in quantum mechanics differs fundamentally from classical measurement. While classical measurements merely reveal pre-existing properties, quantum measurements actively disturb the system and, in general, change its state. Our Bloch cube framework provides a concrete model for understanding this process.

A quantum measurement on our two-state system corresponds to asking a yes-no question about the state. For the Bloch cube, we have three natural measurement types, corresponding to the three spatial axes. The Z-measurement asks whether the system is in state
|+z\rangle
or
|-z\rangle
. Similarly, X-measurements distinguish between
|+x\rangle
and
|-x\rangle
, while Y-measurements distinguish between
|+y\rangle
and
|-y\rangle
.

To understand how measurement procedures translate into mathematical operators, let’s construct them step by step from our concrete measurement outcomes. We’ve already encountered the concept of eigenstates and eigenvalues in the context of rotations—here, the eigenvalues will correspond to the possible measurement outcomes and eigenstates correspond to the states that yield definite results.

Consider the Z-measurement procedure: we ask whether the system is in state
|+z\rangle
or
|-z\rangle
. If we assign numerical scores to these outcomes—say +1 for finding
|+z\rangle
and -1 for finding
|-z\rangle
—then we can construct a mathematical operator that encodes this measurement:

\hat{\sigma}_{z}=(+1)\outerproduct{+z}{+z}+(-1)\outerproduct{-z}{-z}=\outerproduct{+z}{+z}-\outerproduct{-z}{-z}

(1.38)

This operator has the property that when it acts on the measurement eigenstates, it returns the corresponding measurement outcome:
\hat{\sigma}_{z}|+z\rangle=(+1)|+z\rangle
and
\hat{\sigma}_{z}|-z\rangle=(-1)|-z\rangle
.

Similarly, we can construct operators for X and Y measurements:

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{x}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+x}{+x}-\outerproduct{-x}{-x}

(1.39)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{y}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+y}{+y}-\outerproduct{-y}{-y}

(1.40)

These measurement operators are known as the Pauli operators, which form the foundation of two-level quantum mechanics. Notice that the six Bloch cube states we introduced earlier are precisely the eigenstates of these three operators:
|\pm z\rangle
are eigenstates of
\sigma_{z}
,
|\pm x\rangle
are eigenstates of
\sigma_{x}
, and
|\pm y\rangle
are eigenstates of
\sigma_{y}
.

Observe the pattern in our construction: each measurement operator was built by assigning real numerical values (
\pm 1
) to the possible outcomes and weighting the corresponding projection operators. This systematic construction ensures that the eigenvalues—which represent the measurement outcomes we can observe—are always real numbers. This is a general feature of all quantum measurements.

1.5.2  The Born Rule and Probability

The Born rule provides the fundamental connection between the mathematical formalism of quantum mechanics and experimental predictions. According to this rule, if a system is in state
|\psi\rangle
and we measure an observable with eigenstates
|\phi_{i}\rangle
, the probability of obtaining the outcome corresponding to
|\phi_{i}\rangle
is:

P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|\phi_{i}\rangle)=|\langle\phi_{i}|\psi\rangle|^{2}

(1.41)

This formula encodes one of the most profound aspects of quantum mechanics: even with complete knowledge of the quantum state, we can only predict probabilities of measurement outcomes, not the outcomes themselves.

Let’s explore several examples to understand the Born rule deeply.

Example 2: Measurement Probabilities for
|+x\rangle

Consider the state
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)
. Let’s calculate the probabilities for all three measurement types.

Z-measurement:

\displaystyle P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+z\rangle)

\displaystyle=|\langle+z|+x\rangle|^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}

(1.42)

\displaystyle P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-z\rangle)

\displaystyle=|\langle-z|+x\rangle|^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}

(1.43)

X-measurement:

\displaystyle P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+x\rangle)

\displaystyle=|\langle+x|+x\rangle|^{2}=|1|^{2}=1

(1.44)

\displaystyle P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-x\rangle)

\displaystyle=|\langle-x|+x\rangle|^{2}=|0|^{2}=0

(1.45)

Y-measurement:

Using
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+y\rangle+|-y\rangle)
from our basis table:

\displaystyle P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+y\rangle)

\displaystyle=|\langle+y|+x\rangle|^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}

(1.46)

\displaystyle P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-y\rangle)

\displaystyle=|\langle-y|+x\rangle|^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}

(1.47)

1.5.3  State Collapse and Sequential Measurements

Following a measurement, the quantum state “collapses” to the eigenstate corresponding to the observed outcome. This collapse is instantaneous and irreversible within the standard quantum formalism. If we measure
|+x\rangle
along the z-axis and the system collapses to
|+z\rangle
, the post-measurement state is
|+z\rangle
, not
|+x\rangle
.

This collapse leads to one of the most striking features of quantum measurement: the order of measurements matters. Consider performing X and Z measurements in sequence. If we start with
|+y\rangle
, measure X (causing the system to collapse to, say,
|+x\rangle
), then measure Z, we get random Z results. However, if we first measure Z, then X, we get random X results. The non-commutativity of measurements reflects the non-commutativity of the corresponding operators.

Example 3: Sequential Measurements

Start with
|+y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)
.

Path 1: Measure Z, then X

(a)

Initial state:
|+y\rangle

(b)

Measure Z: The system collapses to
|+z\rangle
with probability
1/2
or
|-z\rangle
with probability
1/2

(c)

Suppose the system collapses to
|+z\rangle

(d)

Now measure X on
|+z\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+x\rangle+|-x\rangle)

(e)

The system collapses to
|+x\rangle
or
|-x\rangle
each with probability
1/2

Path 2: Measure X, then Z

(a)

Initial state:
|+y\rangle=\frac{(1+i)|+x\rangle+(1-i)|-x\rangle}{2}

(b)

Measure X: The system collapses to
|+x\rangle
with probability
1/2
or
|-x\rangle
with probability
1/2

(c)

Suppose the system collapses to
|+x\rangle

(d)

Now measure Z on
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)

(e)

The system collapses to
|+z\rangle
or
|-z\rangle
each with probability
1/2

The final probability distributions are identical, but the intermediate states differ!

Bloch Cube Activity: 1.2: Simulating Quantum Measurement

Using your Bloch cube, simulate the measurement process:

(a)

Set the cube to show
|+x\rangle
(facing up)

(b)

To simulate a Z-measurement, calculate the probabilities for collapsing to
|\pm z\rangle
states

(c)

Use a random process (coin flip) to determine the outcome

(d)

Rotate the cube to show the post-measurement state

(e)

Repeat multiple times to verify the statistical predictions

(f)

Try starting from
|+y\rangle
and perform measurements in different orders

1.5.4  Measurement Outcomes and Expectation Values

So far, we have discussed measurements as producing states
|\pm z\rangle
,
|\pm x\rangle
, or
|\pm y\rangle
. To connect with observable physical quantities, we assign numerical values to these outcomes. We adopt a standard “scoring” system:

•

Measuring
|+w\rangle
yields outcome
+1

•

Measuring
|-w\rangle
yields outcome
-1

where
W\in\{X,Y,Z\}
labels the measurement type. This assignment is not arbitrary—it corresponds to measuring the component of spin angular momentum along the specified axis (in units of
\hbar/2
).

With this scoring system, each measurement operator can be written as:

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{z}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+z}{+z}-\outerproduct{-z}{-z}

(1.48)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{x}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+x}{+x}-\outerproduct{-x}{-x}

(1.49)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{y}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+y}{+y}-\outerproduct{-y}{-y}

(1.50)

These are the Pauli operators. Notice something important: unlike the rotation operators whose eigenvalues were complex phases (
1
and
i
for
\hat{Z}
), the Pauli operators have real eigenvalues (
\pm 1
) that represent the possible measurement outcomes. This is not a coincidence—measurement outcomes must be real numbers that we can observe in experiments.

This pattern—that measurement operators constructed from real-valued outcomes always have real eigenvalues—holds generally in quantum mechanics. Operators with this property are called
Hermitian
operators. The requirement that measurement outcomes be real numbers forces measurement operators to be Hermitian, while the requirement that rotations preserve probability forces rotation operators to be
unitary
(ensuring eigenvalues with magnitude 1).

Interestingly, there’s a simple relationship between the rotation and Pauli operators:
\hat{\sigma}_{W}=\hat{W}^{2}
for
W\in\{X,Y,Z\}
. That is, the Pauli operators correspond to 180
∘
rotations about their respective axes. This makes sense geometrically—a 180
∘
rotation takes each Bloch cube face to its opposite face, which is precisely what the Pauli operators encode through their
\pm 1
eigenvalues.

For a system in state
|\psi\rangle
, the
expectation value
of a measurement is the average outcome we would obtain if we performed the measurement on many identically prepared systems:

\langle\hat{\sigma}_{W}\rangle=P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|+W\rangle)\cdot(+1)+P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|-W\rangle)\cdot(-1)=P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|+W\rangle)-P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|-W\rangle)

(1.51)

where
P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|\pm W\rangle)
are the probabilities calculated using the Born rule.

Example 4: Expectation Values for
|+x\rangle

Calculate the expectation values of all three Pauli operators for the state
|+x\rangle
.

From Example 1.2, we found:

•

Z-measurement:
P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+z\rangle)=P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-z\rangle)=1/2

•

X-measurement:
P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+x\rangle)=1
,
P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-x\rangle)=0

•

Y-measurement:
P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+y\rangle)=P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-y\rangle)=1/2

Therefore:

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\sigma}_{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+z\rangle)-P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-z\rangle)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=0

(1.52)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\sigma}_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+x\rangle)-P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-x\rangle)=1-0=1

(1.53)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\sigma}_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|+y\rangle)-P(|+x\rangle\rightarrow|-y\rangle)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=0

(1.54)

The state
|+x\rangle
has definite value
+1
for X-measurements but gives random (zero average) results for Y and Z measurements.

Expectation values can also be calculated directly using the inner product:

\langle\hat{A}\rangle=\langle\psi|\hat{A}|\psi\rangle

(1.55)

This formula, fundamental to quantum mechanics, connects the abstract mathematical formalism to measurable physical quantities.

Bloch Cube Activity: 1.3: Measuring Expectation Values with Bloch Cubes

Using your Bloch cube:

(a)

Prepare the state
|+y\rangle

(b)

Perform 20 simulated Z-measurements, recording
+1
for
|+z\rangle
and
-1
for
|-z\rangle

(c)

Calculate the average of your results

(d)

Compare with the theoretical expectation value
\langle\hat{\sigma}_{z}\rangle=0

(e)

Repeat for X and Y measurements

(f)

Discuss how many measurements would be needed to distinguish
\langle\hat{\sigma}_{z}\rangle=0
from
\langle\hat{\sigma}_{z}\rangle=0.1

1.6  Unitary Evolution and Quantum Dynamics

1.6.1  Discrete Rotations as Quantum Gates

The time evolution of isolated quantum systems is governed by unitary transformations. For our Bloch cube, these transformations are implemented as physical rotations of the cube. This provides an immediate, tactile understanding of quantum dynamics that complements the mathematical formalism.

Consider rotations by 90 degrees about the three coordinate axes. These discrete rotations form a group and can be represented by unitary operators. The rotation by 90 degrees clockwise about the z-axis (when viewed from the positive z-direction) is represented by the operator:

\hat{Z}=\outerproduct{+z}{+z}+i\outerproduct{-z}{-z}

(1.56)

Let’s trace through how this operator acts on all six Bloch cube states:

Example 5: Action of
\hat{Z}
on All Cube States

\displaystyle\hat{Z}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=|+z\rangle

(1.57)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=i|-z\rangle

(1.58)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{Z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)=|+y\rangle

(1.59)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{Z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)=|-y\rangle

(1.60)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{Z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)=|-x\rangle

(1.61)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{Z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)=|+x\rangle

(1.62)

This confirms that
\hat{Z}
implements a 90
∘
rotation:
+x\rightarrow+y\rightarrow-x\rightarrow-y\rightarrow+x
.

Notice something remarkable in the calculations above: when
\hat{Z}
acts on
|+z\rangle
and
|-z\rangle
, these states are only multiplied by phase factors (1 and
i
respectively). This illustrates a fundamental concept in quantum mechanics:
eigenstates
and
eigenvalues
.

Key Idea: Eigenstates and Eigenvalues

A state
|\psi\rangle
is called an eigenstate of an operator
\hat{A}
if the operator’s action on that state merely multiplies it by a complex number:

\hat{A}|\psi\rangle=\lambda|\psi\rangle

(1.63)

The complex number
\lambda
is called the eigenvalue. For our rotation operator
\hat{Z}
:

•

|+z\rangle
is an eigenstate with eigenvalue
\lambda=1

•

|-z\rangle
is an eigenstate with eigenvalue
\lambda=i

Physically, eigenstates represent states that are “stable” under the operation—they don’t change direction, only acquire a phase. For rotations about the z-axis, it makes intuitive sense that states aligned with the z-axis (
|\pm z\rangle
) would be special in this way.

The eigenvalues of rotation operators are always complex numbers with magnitude 1 (i.e., pure phases), which ensures that the operators preserve the normalization of quantum states. This is a consequence of unitarity. For measurement operators, as we’ve seen earlier, the eigenvalues will be real numbers corresponding to the possible measurement outcomes.

Similarly, we can define the X and Y rotation operators:

\displaystyle\hat{X}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+x}{+x}+i\outerproduct{-x}{-x}

(1.64)

\displaystyle\hat{Y}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+y}{+y}+i\outerproduct{-y}{-y}

(1.65)

1.6.2  The Non-Commutativity of Rotations

A fundamental feature of quantum mechanics is that operations generally do not commute. This non-commutativity can be directly experienced through Bloch cube manipulations. Consider the unitary operators
\hat{X}
and
\hat{Y}
, representing 90-degree rotations about the x and y axes respectively.

To understand this rigorously, we need to determine how these operators act on the state
|+z\rangle
. Using the operator definitions:

\displaystyle\hat{X}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+x}{+x}+i\outerproduct{-x}{-x}

(1.66)

\displaystyle\hat{Y}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+y}{+y}+i\outerproduct{-y}{-y}

(1.67)

Example 6: Rigorous Derivation of Rotation Actions

Finding
\hat{X}|+z\rangle
:

\displaystyle\hat{X}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=(\outerproduct{+x}{+x}+i\outerproduct{-x}{-x})|+z\rangle

(1.68)

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle\langle+x|+z\rangle+i|-x\rangle\langle-x|+z\rangle

(1.69)

Using our basis representations:
\langle+x|+z\rangle=\langle-x|+z\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle\hat{X}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}+i|-x\rangle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.70)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+x\rangle+i|-x\rangle)

(1.71)

Converting to Z-basis using
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)
and
|-x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)
:

\displaystyle\hat{X}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)+i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)\right]

(1.72)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[(1+i)|+z\rangle+(1-i)|-z\rangle]

(1.73)

Factoring out the phase:
(1+i)=\sqrt{2}e^{i\pi/4}
, so:

\hat{X}|+z\rangle=e^{i\pi/4}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)=e^{i\pi/4}|-y\rangle

(1.74)

Finding
\hat{Y}|+z\rangle
:

Similarly:

\displaystyle\hat{Y}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=(\outerproduct{+y}{+y}+i\outerproduct{-y}{-y})|+z\rangle

(1.75)

\displaystyle=|+y\rangle\langle+y|+z\rangle+i|-y\rangle\langle-y|+z\rangle

(1.76)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+y\rangle+i|-y\rangle)

(1.77)

Converting to Z-basis and following similar algebra:

\hat{Y}|+z\rangle=e^{i\pi/4}|+x\rangle

(1.78)

Now let’s trace through both possible orderings with the correct phase factors:

Order 1:
\hat{X}
then
\hat{Y}

|+z\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{X}}e^{i\pi/4}|-y\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{Y}}?

To find
\hat{Y}|-y\rangle
:

\displaystyle\hat{Y}|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=(\outerproduct{+y}{+y}+i\outerproduct{-y}{-y})|-y\rangle

(1.79)

\displaystyle=|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+y|-y\rangle}_{=0}+i|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-y|-y\rangle}_{=1}

(1.80)

\displaystyle=i|-y\rangle

(1.81)

Therefore:
|+z\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{X}}e^{i\pi/4}|-y\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{Y}}ie^{i\pi/4}|-y\rangle=e^{i3\pi/4}|-y\rangle

Order 2:
\hat{Y}
then
\hat{X}

|+z\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{Y}}e^{i\pi/4}|+x\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{X}}?

To find
\hat{X}|+x\rangle
:

\displaystyle\hat{X}|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=(\outerproduct{+x}{+x}+i\outerproduct{-x}{-x})|+x\rangle

(1.82)

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle\underbrace{\langle+x|+x\rangle}_{=1}+i|-x\rangle\underbrace{\langle-x|+x\rangle}_{=0}

(1.83)

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle

(1.84)

Therefore:
|+z\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{Y}}e^{i\pi/4}|+x\rangle\xrightarrow{\hat{X}}e^{i\pi/4}|+x\rangle

Comparison:

•

\hat{Y}\hat{X}|+z\rangle=e^{i3\pi/4}|-y\rangle

•

\hat{X}\hat{Y}|+z\rangle=e^{i\pi/4}|+x\rangle

The final states differ both in direction (
|-y\rangle
vs
|+x\rangle
) and global phase (
e^{i3\pi/4}
vs
e^{i\pi/4}
), demonstrating that
\hat{X}\hat{Y}\neq\hat{Y}\hat{X}
.

Key Idea: Phase Factors in Quantum Rotations

The 90
∘
rotation operators naturally introduce phase factors that are often omitted in simplified treatments. These phases are essential for:

•

Mathematical consistency with the operator definitions

•

Correct calculation of sequential transformations

•

Understanding the geometric structure of rotation groups

•

Proper treatment of commutation relations

While global phases don’t affect measurement probabilities, they are crucial for understanding how operators compose and for advanced topics like geometric phases.

This result can be verified through explicit matrix calculations using the matrix representations of these rotation operators. The non-trivial result provides a concrete demonstration that quantum rotations do not commute, with immediate physical consequences observable on the Bloch sphere.

Bloch Cube Activity: 1.4: Exploring Non-Commutativity

Using your Bloch cube:

(a)

Start with the cube showing
|+z\rangle

(b)

Perform a 90
∘
rotation about the X-axis, then about the Y-axis

(c)

Record the final state

(d)

Reset to
|+z\rangle
and perform the rotations in reverse order

(e)

Compare the final states

(f)

Repeat for different initial states and rotation sequences

(g)

Discuss the physical implications of non-commutativity

1.7  The Density Operator Formalism

1.7.1  Pure States and Statistical Mixtures

Thus far, we have represented quantum states as vectors in Hilbert space. This representation assumes maximal knowledge about the quantum system—what we call a pure state. However, in many practical situations, our knowledge is incomplete, necessitating a more general formalism.

The density operator provides this generalization. For a pure state
|\psi\rangle
, the density operator is simply:

\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}

(1.85)

For example, the density operator for
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)
is:

\displaystyle\rho_{+x}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{+x}{+x}

(1.86)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)(\langle+z|+\langle-z|)

(1.87)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\outerproduct{+z}{+z}+\outerproduct{+z}{-z}+\outerproduct{-z}{+z}+\outerproduct{-z}{-z})

(1.88)

This operator contains all information about the quantum state and can be used to calculate expectation values of observables:
\langle A\rangle=\text{Tr}(\rho A)
.

When our knowledge is incomplete, we have a statistical mixture of pure states. If the system is in state
|\psi_{i}\rangle
with classical probability
p_{i}
, the density operator becomes:

\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}\outerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_i}

(1.89)

1.7.2  Properties and Interpretation

The density operator formalism reveals important distinctions between quantum and classical probability. Consider two preparations:

(a)

A pure state
|\psi\rangle=\sqrt{0.6}|+z\rangle+\sqrt{0.4}|-z\rangle

(b)

A mixture with 60% probability of
|+z\rangle
and 40% probability of
|-z\rangle

For Z-measurements, both preparations yield identical statistics. However, they differ dramatically for X-measurements. The pure state shows quantum interference through the off-diagonal terms
\outerproduct{+z}{-z}
and
\outerproduct{-z}{+z}
in its density operator, while the mixture does not have these terms.

This difference is captured by the purity
\text{Tr}(\rho^{2})
. Pure states always have purity 1, while mixed states have purity less than 1.

Example 7: Calculating Expectation Values for a Mixed State Ensemble

Consider the mixed state
\rho=0.6\outerproduct{+z}{+z}+0.4\outerproduct{-z}{-z}
. We’ll calculate the expectation values
\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle
,
\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle
, and
\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle
using a Bloch cube ensemble.

Step 1: Create the ensemble

•

Take 5 Bloch cubes

•

Set 3 to show
|+z\rangle
and 2 to show
|-z\rangle

•

This ensemble represents our mixed state

Step 2: Use pairing to simplify

A key insight: opposite states contribute equally but with opposite signs to any measurement. Therefore, any pair of cubes showing
|+z\rangle
and
|-z\rangle
contributes zero to all expectation values.

•

Pair up 2
|+z\rangle
cubes with the 2
|-z\rangle
cubes

•

These 4 cubes (2 pairs) contribute 0 to all expectation values

•

We’re left with 1 unpaired
|+z\rangle
cube

Step 3: Calculate expectation values

For the ensemble, the expectation value is the average over all cubes:

Z-measurement:

•

4 paired cubes: contribute 0

•

1 unpaired
|+z\rangle
: contributes +1

•

Average over 5 cubes:
\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle=\frac{0+1}{5}=0.2

X-measurement:

•

For
|+z\rangle
: equal probability of
\pm 1
, so expectation is 0

•

All 5 cubes have expectation 0 for X-measurement

•

\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle=0

Y-measurement:

•

Same reasoning as X-measurement

•

\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle=0

Verification:
We can check this matches the theoretical calculation:

\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle=0.6(+1)+0.4(-1)=0.6-0.4=0.2

This example shows how the pairing trick simplifies calculations and provides physical intuition: mixed states with equal amounts of opposite states are "balanced" and give zero expectation values for all measurements.

Contrast with pure state:
The pure state
\sqrt{0.6}|+z\rangle+\sqrt{0.4}|-z\rangle
would also give
\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle=0.2
, but would give non-zero values for
\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle
and
\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle
due to quantum interference. This mixture cannot show such interference because it’s just a classical probability distribution over definite states.

Bloch Cube Activity: 1.5: The Pairing Principle for Mixed States

Explore the pairing principle with different mixed states:

(a)

Create an ensemble with 4 cubes: 2 showing
|+x\rangle
and 2 showing
|-x\rangle

(b)

Verify that all expectation values are zero by pairing opposite states

(c)

Now add a 5th cube showing
|+x\rangle
. Calculate
\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle
,
\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle
,
\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle

(d)

Create a more complex ensemble: 2
|+z\rangle
, 2
|-z\rangle
, 1
|+x\rangle
, 1
|-x\rangle

(e)

Use pairing to simplify the calculation of expectation values

(f)

Discuss: Can you always use pairing? What happens with ensembles that have states from all three axes?

1.8  Advanced Topics: Beyond the Cube

1.8.1  The Full Bloch Sphere

While the Bloch cube restricts us to six special states, the full Bloch sphere encompasses all possible states of a two-level system. Any point on the unit sphere corresponds to a valid quantum state:

|\psi(\theta,\phi)\rangle=\cos(\theta/2)|+z\rangle+e^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2)|-z\rangle

(1.90)

The Bloch vector
\vec{r}=(r_{x},r_{y},r_{z})
provides an alternative parameterization:

\rho=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{I}+\vec{r}\cdot\vec{\sigma})

(1.91)

For pure states,
|\vec{r}|=1
(on the sphere surface). For mixed states,
|\vec{r}|<1
(inside the sphere). The completely mixed state
\rho=\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}
sits at the origin with
\vec{r}=0
.

1.8.2  Quantum Process Tomography

Given an unknown quantum state, how can we determine it experimentally? This is the problem of quantum state tomography. For a two-level system, we need to measure in three different bases to fully characterize the state.

If we measure many copies of the state in the X, Y, and Z bases, obtaining expectation values:

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}(\rho\sigma_{x})=r_{x}

(1.92)

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}(\rho\sigma_{y})=r_{y}

(1.93)

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}(\rho\sigma_{z})=r_{z}

(1.94)

We can reconstruct the density operator from these Bloch vector components. This provides a complete experimental characterization of the quantum state.

1.9  The Postulates of Quantum Mechanics

Having developed the mathematical and physical tools, we can now state the fundamental postulates of quantum mechanics in a form particularly suited to our Bloch cube approach:

Postulate 1 (State Space):
The state of a quantum system is represented by a normalized vector
|\psi\rangle
in a complex Hilbert space. For our two-state system, this space is two-dimensional.

Postulate 2 (Observables):
Physical quantities are represented by operators constructed from measurement procedures. The eigenvalues of these operators correspond to possible measurement outcomes. Since measurement outcomes must be real numbers, these operators are necessarily Hermitian.

Postulate 3 (Born Rule):
Upon measuring an observable
\hat{A}
for a system in state
|\psi\rangle
, the probability of obtaining eigenvalue
a_{i}
is
P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|a_{i}\rangle)=|\langle a_{i}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
, where
|a_{i}\rangle
is the corresponding eigenstate.

Postulate 4 (State Collapse):
After obtaining measurement result
a_{i}
, the system’s state becomes
|a_{i}\rangle
.

Postulate 5 (Unitary Evolution):
The time evolution of an isolated quantum system is governed by the unitary operator
\hat{U}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}
, where
\hat{H}
is the Hamiltonian.

These postulates, while abstractly stated, become concrete through Bloch cube manipulations. Each postulate corresponds to physical operations: preparation, measurement, and rotation of the cube.

Bloch Cube Activity: 1.6: Verifying the Postulates

Using your Bloch cube, verify each postulate:

(a)

State Space
: Show that any face can be expressed as a normalized superposition

(b)

Observables
: Identify the three measurement types with cube orientations

(c)

Born Rule
: Prepare
|+x\rangle
and measure Z many times to verify 50-50 statistics

(d)

Collapse
: After measuring Z on
|+x\rangle
, verify the state is now
|\pm z\rangle

(e)

Evolution
: Show that rotations preserve normalization and orthogonality

1.10  Matrix Representations

In the computational basis, quantum states are represented as column vectors and operators as matrices. This section formally introduces the matrix representations that complement our Bloch cube approach.

1.10.1  Single-Qubit Matrix Representations

For a single qubit, the computational (Z) basis states have the matrix representations:

|+z\rangle=|0\rangle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad|-z\rangle=|1\rangle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.95)

The projection operators from earlier in this chapter can now be written explicitly as matrices. For example:

\displaystyle\outerproduct{0}{0}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.96)

\displaystyle\outerproduct{1}{1}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

(1.97)

\displaystyle\outerproduct{0}{1}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.98)

\displaystyle\outerproduct{1}{0}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.99)

The Pauli matrices, which represent measurements along the three coordinate axes, have the explicit forms:

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.100)

\displaystyle\sigma_{y}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.101)

\displaystyle\sigma_{z}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}

(1.102)

The rotation operators from earlier also have matrix representations. For example:

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{z}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\theta\sigma_{z}/2}\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta/2}&0\\
0&e^{i\theta/2}\end{pmatrix}

(1.103)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}

\displaystyle=\hat{R}_{z}(\pi/2)\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&i\end{pmatrix}

(1.104)

Similarly, the discrete rotation operators have the matrix forms:

\displaystyle\hat{X}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+i&1-i\\
1-i&1+i\end{pmatrix}

(1.105)

\displaystyle\hat{Y}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+i&-1-i\\
1+i&1+i\end{pmatrix}

(1.106)

1.11  Comprehensive Basis Representations

Before moving on to more advanced topics, we establish a comprehensive reference for the six Bloch cube states expressed in all three orthonormal bases. This reference will prove invaluable throughout our study of quantum mechanics, as it allows us to quickly read off the representation of any state in any basis.

1.11.1  The Six Bloch Cube States in All Bases

Master Reference: All Bloch Cube States in All Bases

Z-basis representations:

\{|+z\rangle,|-z\rangle\}

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.107)

\displaystyle|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\end{pmatrix}

(1.108)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
i\end{pmatrix}

(1.109)

\displaystyle|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\end{pmatrix}

(1.110)

\displaystyle|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=|+z\rangle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(1.111)

\displaystyle|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=|-z\rangle\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.112)

X-basis representations:

\{|+x\rangle,|-x\rangle\}

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle\underset{x}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(1.113)

\displaystyle|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=|-x\rangle\underset{x}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.114)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+i)|+x\rangle+(1-i)|-x\rangle}{2}\underset{x}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+i\\
1-i\end{pmatrix}

(1.115)

\displaystyle|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-i)|+x\rangle+(1+i)|-x\rangle}{2}\underset{x}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1-i\\
1+i\end{pmatrix}

(1.116)

\displaystyle|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+x\rangle+|-x\rangle)\underset{x}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.117)

\displaystyle|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+x\rangle-|-x\rangle)\underset{x}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\end{pmatrix}

(1.118)

Y-basis representations:

\{|+y\rangle,|-y\rangle\}

\displaystyle|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-i)|+y\rangle+(1+i)|-y\rangle}{2}\underset{y}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1-i\\
1+i\end{pmatrix}

(1.119)

\displaystyle|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+i)|+y\rangle+(1-i)|-y\rangle}{2}\underset{y}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+i\\
1-i\end{pmatrix}

(1.120)

\displaystyle|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=|+y\rangle\underset{y}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(1.121)

\displaystyle|-y\rangle

\displaystyle=|-y\rangle\underset{y}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.122)

\displaystyle|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+y\rangle+|-y\rangle)\underset{y}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}

(1.123)

\displaystyle|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-i|+y\rangle+i|-y\rangle)\underset{y}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-i\\
i\end{pmatrix}

(1.124)

1.11.2  Using the Reference Table

This comprehensive table enables several powerful calculations:

Example 8: Cross-Basis Inner Products

Calculate
\langle+x|+y\rangle
using different basis representations.

Method 1: Using Z-basis

\displaystyle\langle+x|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\langle+z|+\langle-z|)\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)\right)

(1.125)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\langle+z|+z\rangle+i\langle+z|-z\rangle+\langle-z|+z\rangle+i\langle-z|-z\rangle)

(1.126)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+0+0+i)=\frac{1+i}{2}

(1.127)

Method 2: Using X-basis

From the table:
|+y\rangle=\frac{(1+i)|+x\rangle+(1-i)|-x\rangle}{2}

\displaystyle\langle+x|+y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+x|\left(\frac{(1+i)|+x\rangle+(1-i)|-x\rangle}{2}\right)

(1.128)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[(1+i)\langle+x|+x\rangle+(1-i)\langle+x|-x\rangle]

(1.129)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[(1+i)\cdot 1+(1-i)\cdot 0]

(1.130)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+i}{2}

(1.131)

Both methods give the same result, confirming our calculation.

Magnitude:

|\langle+x|+y\rangle|=\left|\frac{1+i}{2}\right|=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

Phase:

\arg(\langle+x|+y\rangle)=\arg(1+i)=\pi/4

1.11.3  Transformation Matrices Between Bases

The basis transformations are most clearly understood through their basis-independent operator form, which then yields matrix representations in any chosen basis.

Basis-Independent Form

The transformation operators map basis states from one representation to another:

Basis Transformation Operators

Z to X transformation:

U_{xz}=\outerproduct{+x}{+z}+\outerproduct{-x}{-z}

(1.132)

This operator maps:
|+z\rangle\mapsto|+x\rangle
and
|-z\rangle\mapsto|-x\rangle

Z to Y transformation:

U_{yz}=\outerproduct{+y}{+z}+\outerproduct{-y}{-z}

(1.133)

This operator maps:
|+z\rangle\mapsto|+y\rangle
and
|-z\rangle\mapsto|-y\rangle

X to Y transformation:

U_{yx}=\outerproduct{+y}{+x}+\outerproduct{-y}{-x}

(1.134)

This operator maps:
|+x\rangle\mapsto|+y\rangle
and
|-x\rangle\mapsto|-y\rangle

These operators transform states according to their definition. To find the matrix representation of any operator
U
in a given basis, we construct the matrix by computing all matrix elements in that basis.

Deriving Matrix Representations

To find the matrix representation of an operator
U
in any basis, we compute the matrix elements
\langle i|U|j\rangle
where
|i\rangle
and
|j\rangle
are the basis states. For example, in the Z-basis, the matrix elements are:

U\underset{z}{\doteq}\begin{pmatrix}\langle+z|U|+z\rangle&\langle+z|U|-z\rangle\\
\langle-z|U|+z\rangle&\langle-z|U|-z\rangle\end{pmatrix}

(1.135)

Example 9: Deriving
U_{xz}
in the Z-basis

The operator
U_{xz}=|+x\rangle\langle+z|+|-x\rangle\langle-z|
has the following matrix elements in the Z-basis:

\displaystyle\langle+z|U_{xz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+z|(|+x\rangle\langle+z|+|-x\rangle\langle-z|)|+z\rangle

(1.136)

\displaystyle=\langle+z|+x\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|+z\rangle}_{=1}+\langle+z|-x\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|+z\rangle}_{=0}

(1.137)

\displaystyle=\langle+z|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.138)

\displaystyle\langle+z|U_{xz}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+z|+x\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|-z\rangle}_{=0}+\langle+z|-x\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|-z\rangle}_{=1}

(1.139)

\displaystyle=\langle+z|-x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.140)

\displaystyle\langle-z|U_{xz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-z|+x\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|+z\rangle}_{=1}+\langle-z|-x\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|+z\rangle}_{=0}

(1.141)

\displaystyle=\langle-z|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.142)

\displaystyle\langle-z|U_{xz}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-z|+x\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|-z\rangle}_{=0}+\langle-z|-x\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|-z\rangle}_{=1}

(1.143)

\displaystyle=\langle-z|-x\rangle=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.144)

Therefore:

U_{xz}\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}

(1.145)

Verification:
This operator transforms the Z-basis states to X-basis states:

\displaystyle U_{xz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)

(1.146)

\displaystyle U_{xz}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=|-x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-|-z\rangle)

(1.147)

We can verify this using the matrix representation:

\displaystyle U_{xz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}=|+x\rangle\checkmark

(1.148)

Key Idea: Matrix Representations of Operators

The matrix representation of any operator
U
in a given basis is constructed from its matrix elements:

•

In the Z-basis:
(U)_{ij}=\langle i|U|j\rangle
where
i,j\in\{+z,-z\}

•

The operator equation
U|\psi\rangle=|\phi\rangle
becomes a matrix equation when expressed in any basis

•

Different bases give different matrix representations of the same operator

•

The physical content (eigenvalues, trace, determinant) remains invariant

Example 10: Deriving
U_{yz}
in the Z-basis

The operator
U_{yz}=|+y\rangle\langle+z|+|-y\rangle\langle-z|
has the following matrix elements in the Z-basis:

\displaystyle\langle+z|U_{yz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+z|(|+y\rangle\langle+z|+|-y\rangle\langle-z|)|+z\rangle

(1.149)

\displaystyle=\langle+z|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|+z\rangle}_{=1}+\langle+z|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|+z\rangle}_{=0}

(1.150)

\displaystyle=\langle+z|+y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.151)

\displaystyle\langle+z|U_{yz}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+z|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|-z\rangle}_{=0}+\langle+z|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|-z\rangle}_{=1}

(1.152)

\displaystyle=\langle+z|-y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.153)

\displaystyle\langle-z|U_{yz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-z|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|+z\rangle}_{=1}+\langle-z|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|+z\rangle}_{=0}

(1.154)

\displaystyle=\langle-z|+y\rangle=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.155)

\displaystyle\langle-z|U_{yz}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-z|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+z|-z\rangle}_{=0}+\langle-z|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-z|-z\rangle}_{=1}

(1.156)

\displaystyle=\langle-z|-y\rangle=\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.157)

Therefore:

U_{yz}\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
i&-i\end{pmatrix}

(1.158)

Verification:
This operator transforms:

\displaystyle U_{yz}|+z\rangle

\displaystyle=|+y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)

(1.159)

\displaystyle U_{yz}|-z\rangle

\displaystyle=|-y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle-i|-z\rangle)

(1.160)

Example 11: Deriving
U_{yx}
in the X-basis

The operator
U_{yx}=|+y\rangle\langle+x|+|-y\rangle\langle-x|
has the following matrix elements in the X-basis:

\displaystyle\langle+x|U_{yx}|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+x|(|+y\rangle\langle+x|+|-y\rangle\langle-x|)|+x\rangle

(1.161)

\displaystyle=\langle+x|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+x|+x\rangle}_{=1}+\langle+x|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-x|+x\rangle}_{=0}

(1.162)

\displaystyle=\langle+x|+y\rangle

(1.163)

From the correct expression
|+y\rangle=\frac{(1+i)|+x\rangle+(1-i)|-x\rangle}{2}
, we have
\langle+x|+y\rangle=\frac{1+i}{2}
, so:

\langle+x|U_{yx}|+x\rangle=\frac{1+i}{2}

(1.164)

Similarly:

\displaystyle\langle+x|U_{yx}|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle+x|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+x|-x\rangle}_{=0}+\langle+x|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-x|-x\rangle}_{=1}=\langle+x|-y\rangle=\frac{1-i}{2}

(1.165)

\displaystyle\langle-x|U_{yx}|+x\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-x|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+x|+x\rangle}_{=1}+\langle-x|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-x|+x\rangle}_{=0}=\langle-x|+y\rangle=\frac{1-i}{2}

(1.166)

\displaystyle\langle-x|U_{yx}|-x\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-x|+y\rangle\underbrace{\langle+x|-x\rangle}_{=0}+\langle-x|-y\rangle\underbrace{\langle-x|-x\rangle}_{=1}=\langle-x|-y\rangle=\frac{1+i}{2}

(1.167)

Therefore:

U_{yx}\underset{x}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+i&1-i\\
1-i&1+i\end{pmatrix}

(1.168)

Summary of Transformation Matrices

The basis transformation operators have the following matrix representations:

\displaystyle U_{xz}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{(transforms }|+z\rangle\to|+x\rangle,|-z\rangle\to|-x\rangle\text{)}

(1.169)

\displaystyle U_{yz}

\displaystyle\underset{z}{\doteq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
i&-i\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{(transforms }|+z\rangle\to|+y\rangle,|-z\rangle\to|-y\rangle\text{)}

(1.170)

\displaystyle U_{yx}

\displaystyle\underset{x}{\doteq}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+i&1-i\\
1-i&1+i\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{(transforms }|+x\rangle\to|+y\rangle,|-x\rangle\to|-y\rangle\text{)}

(1.171)

These matrices satisfy important properties:

•

Unitarity:
U^{\dagger}U=UU^{\dagger}=I

•

They implement the basis transformations
U_{xz}
,
U_{yz}
, and
U_{yx}
defined above

•

Each matrix is constructed from the matrix elements
\langle i|U|j\rangle
in the respective basis

1.12  Chapter Summary

This chapter has established the mathematical and conceptual foundations of quantum mechanics through the study of two-state systems. Beginning with the familiar mathematics of two-dimensional vectors, we developed the dot-vector notation as a bridge to Dirac’s formalism. The introduction of the Bloch cube provided a tangible representation of quantum states, making abstract concepts physically manipulable.

We explored the fundamental features of quantum mechanics: superposition of states, the probabilistic nature of measurement described by the Born rule, state collapse following measurement, and unitary evolution. The non-commutativity of quantum operations emerged naturally from the non-commutativity of rotations. We provided complete basis representations for all six Bloch cube states, enabling calculations in any basis. Finally, we introduced the density operator formalism to handle situations of incomplete knowledge.

This foundation, built on the simplest non-trivial quantum system, contains all the essential features of quantum mechanics. The complete basis representation table will serve as a crucial reference throughout the course. In subsequent chapters, we will extend these concepts to multi-qubit systems, continuous variables, and ultimately to the wave mechanics of particles in space. The physical intuition developed through Bloch cube manipulations will guide us through increasingly abstract mathematical territories.

(Dot-Vec Notation and Basic Operations)

and

Problem 1.4

(The Born Rule and Measurement Probabilities)

The Born rule states that for a system in state
|\psi\rangle
and measurement eigenstate
|\phi_{i}\rangle
, the probability is
P(|\psi\rangle\rightarrow|\phi_{i}\rangle)=|\langle\phi_{i}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
.

(a)

Consider the Bloch cube state
|+x\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|-z\rangle)
. Verify it is normalized, then use the Born rule to calculate the probabilities for obtaining
|+z\rangle
and
|-z\rangle
in a Z-measurement.

(b)

For the state
|+y\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+i|-z\rangle)
, calculate all six probabilities:
P(|+y\rangle\rightarrow|\pm z\rangle)
,
P(|+y\rangle\rightarrow|\pm x\rangle)
, and
P(|+y\rangle\rightarrow|\pm y\rangle)
.

(c)

Using your Bloch cube: Create an ensemble of 8 cubes to represent the mixed state
\rho=0.5\outerproduct{+x}{+x}+0.5\outerproduct{-x}{-x}
. How many cubes should show each face? Calculate
\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle
for this ensemble and compare to the pure state
|+y\rangle
.

(d)

Conceptual: Explain in your own words why the Born rule gives probabilities (not deterministic outcomes) even when we have complete knowledge of the quantum state. How does this differ from classical probability?

Problem 1.8

(Density Operator and Pure vs Mixed States)

The density operator
\hat{\rho}
fully characterizes a quantum state. For a pure state
|\psi\rangle
, we have
\hat{\rho}=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
with
\text{Tr}(\hat{\rho}^{2})=1
. Mixed states have
\text{Tr}(\hat{\rho}^{2})<1
.

(a)

Construct the density matrices for the six Bloch cube face states

(b)

For each, verify that
\text{Tr}(\hat{\rho})=1
and
\text{Tr}(\hat{\rho}^{2})=1
(confirming purity)

(c)

Consider the thermal state
\hat{\rho}_{T}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}
. Calculate
\text{Tr}(\hat{\rho}_{T}^{2})
and explain what this purity means physically

(d)

Show that for any density matrix, the eigenvalues
p_{i}
satisfy
0\leq p_{i}\leq 1
and
\sum_{i}p_{i}=1

(e)

Construct a mixed state as a 50-50 mixture of
|+z\rangle
and
|+x\rangle
:
\hat{\rho}=0.5|+z\rangle\langle+z|+0.5|+x\rangle\langle+x|
. Calculate its purity

Problem 1.14

(Projectors and Spectral Decomposition)

Projection operators
\hat{P}_{i}=|\phi_{i}\rangle\langle\phi_{i}|
extract components of quantum states:

|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+z\rangle+|+x\rangle)

and

|\psi\rangle
and interpret the results as measurement

(c)

\hat{P}_{+z}+\hat{P}_{-z}=\hat{I}
(completeness)

(d)

For a basis
\{|\phi_{i}\rangle\}
, the spectral decomposition of an observable is
\hat{A}=\sum_{i}a_{i}\hat{P}_{i}
. Write
\sigma_{z}
in this form using the Z-basis eigenstates

(e)

Express the same operator in the X-basis and verify that the spectrum (eigenvalues) is unchanged

Problem 1.15

(Operator Polar Decomposition)

Any operator can be decomposed as
\hat{A}=\hat{U}\hat{P}
where
\hat{U}
is unitary and
\hat{P}
is positive semidefinite:

(a)

Show that
\sigma_{x}
can be written in polar form (hint:
\sigma_{x}
is both unitary and Hermitian)

(b)

For the operator
\hat{A}=\hat{I}+i\sigma_{y}
(not Hermitian), find its polar decomposition

(c)

Construct the operator
\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}
and verify that it is positive semidefinite

(d)

For a general density matrix
\hat{\rho}
, explain why the polar decomposition naturally emerges from its spectral decomposition with
p_{i}\geq 0

(e)

Consider the operator
\hat{b}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\sigma_{x}+i\sigma_{y})=|-z\rangle\langle+z|
. Compute
\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}
and interpret as a projection

Problem 1.16

(Two-Level System Kinematics: State Space Geometry)

The space of all quantum states forms a geometric object—the Bloch ball:

(a)

Show that any single-qubit density matrix can be written as
\hat{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{I}+\vec{r}\cdot\vec{\sigma})
with
|\vec{r}|\leq 1

(b)

For the pure states (surface of the Bloch ball), what is the magnitude of
\vec{r}
?

(c)

For the completely mixed state
\hat{I}/2
(center of the Bloch ball), what is
\vec{r}
?

(d)

Show that the constraint
\text{Tr}(\hat{\rho}^{2})\leq 1
is equivalent to
|\vec{r}|\leq 1

(e)

For a state with
\vec{r}=(1/2,0,1/2)
(normalized), construct the density matrix and verify its purity
