\hat{V}_{I}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\hat{V}(t)e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}
is the interaction in the interaction picture. This formulation is essential for quantum field theory and many-body physics.

Key Takeaway
: Time ordering is not merely a mathematical technicality but a fundamental requirement when dealing with time-dependent quantum systems where the Hamiltonian at different times doesn’t commute. It ensures causality and provides the correct perturbative expansion for quantum evolution.

4.8.7  The Driven Two-Level System

A fundamental example is a two-level system with a static energy splitting driven by an oscillating field:

\hat{H}(t)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[\sigma_{x}\cos(\omega t)+\sigma_{y}\sin(\omega t)]

(4.121)

This describes numerous physical systems including an atom in a laser field, where
\omega_{0}
is the atomic transition frequency and
\Omega
is proportional to the electric field amplitude; a nuclear spin in an oscillating magnetic field (NMR), where
\omega_{0}
is the Larmor frequency and
\Omega
is the Rabi frequency; and a superconducting qubit driven by microwave pulses for quantum computation.

4.8.8  The Rotating Frame, Interaction Picture, and Resonance

The key insight for solving driven systems is to transform to the interaction picture (rotating frame). This is analogous to moving to a reference frame rotating at the drive frequency.

The Interaction Picture:
Recall from Section 4.7 that we have two equivalent formulations of quantum dynamics:

•

Schrödinger picture
: States evolve, operators are fixed:
|\psi_{S}(t)\rangle=e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}|\psi_{S}(0)\rangle

•

Heisenberg picture
: Operators evolve, states are fixed:
\hat{A}_{H}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{A}_{S}e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}

For time-dependent Hamiltonians, we introduce a third picture that combines features of both. When the Hamiltonian can be separated into a time-independent part
\hat{H}_{0}
and a time-dependent perturbation
\hat{V}(t)
:

\hat{H}(t)=\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{V}(t)

(4.122)

we define the
interaction picture
(also called the
Dirac picture
) as follows:

\displaystyle\textbf{Interaction picture states:}\quad|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}|\psi_{S}(t)\rangle

(4.123)

\displaystyle\textbf{Interaction picture operators:}\quad\hat{A}_{I}(t)

\displaystyle=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\hat{A}_{S}e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}

(4.124)

In this picture, both states and operators evolve with time, but the time evolution of operators is governed only by
\hat{H}_{0}
, while the time evolution of states is governed by the transformed perturbation
\hat{V}_{I}(t)
. The interaction picture Schrödinger equation becomes:

i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle=\hat{V}_{I}(t)|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

(4.125)

where
\hat{V}_{I}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\hat{V}(t)e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}
is the interaction picture perturbation.

Connection to the Dyson Series:
The interaction picture provides the natural setting for the Dyson series developed in Section 4.8.3. The full Schrödinger picture evolution operator can be written as:

\hat{U}_{S}(t,t_{0})=e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}(t-t_{0})/\hbar}\hat{U}_{I}(t,t_{0})

(4.126)

where the interaction picture evolution operator is given by the Dyson series applied to
\hat{V}_{I}(t)
:

\hat{U}_{I}(t,t_{0})=\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\right)

(4.127)

This separation is powerful because:

•

The evolution due to
\hat{H}_{0}
is trivial (simple exponential)

•

Only the perturbation
\hat{V}_{I}(t)
requires time-ordering via the Dyson series

•

When
\hat{V}_{I}
is small, the Dyson series converges rapidly, enabling perturbative calculations

For the driven two-level system below, when we work in the rotating frame and apply the rotating wave approximation, the interaction picture Hamiltonian becomes approximately time-independent, allowing us to avoid the Dyson series altogether and use a simple exponential.

Application to the Driven Two-Level System:
For our system, we separate:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}\quad\text{(static field)}

(4.128)

\displaystyle\hat{V}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[\sigma_{x}\cos(\omega t)+\sigma_{y}\sin(\omega t)]\quad\text{(rotating field)}

(4.129)

The transformation to the interaction picture is:

|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}|\psi_{S}(t)\rangle=e^{i\omega_{0}t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{S}(t)\rangle

(4.130)

Applying the Schrödinger equation in the interaction picture:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{I}(t)

\displaystyle=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\hat{V}(t)e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}

(4.131)

\displaystyle=e^{i\omega_{0}t\sigma_{z}/2}\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[\sigma_{x}\cos(\omega t)+\sigma_{y}\sin(\omega t)]e^{-i\omega_{0}t\sigma_{z}/2}

(4.132)

Using the rotation properties
e^{i\theta\sigma_{z}/2}\sigma_{x}e^{-i\theta\sigma_{z}/2}=\sigma_{x}\cos\theta+\sigma_{y}\sin\theta
and
e^{i\theta\sigma_{z}/2}\sigma_{y}e^{-i\theta\sigma_{z}/2}=-\sigma_{x}\sin\theta+\sigma_{y}\cos\theta
:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{I}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[(\sigma_{x}\cos\omega_{0}t+\sigma_{y}\sin\omega_{0}t)\cos\omega t

(4.133)

\displaystyle\quad+(-\sigma_{x}\sin\omega_{0}t+\sigma_{y}\cos\omega_{0}t)\sin\omega t]

(4.134)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[\sigma_{x}(\cos\omega_{0}t\cos\omega t-\sin\omega_{0}t\sin\omega t)

(4.135)

\displaystyle\quad+\sigma_{y}(\sin\omega_{0}t\cos\omega t+\cos\omega_{0}t\sin\omega t)]

(4.136)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[\sigma_{x}\cos((\omega_0 - \omega)t)+\sigma_{y}\sin((\omega_0 - \omega)t)]

(4.137)

This interaction picture Hamiltonian still has explicit time dependence, oscillating at the difference frequency
(\omega_{0}-\omega)
. When the driving frequency
\omega
is close to the natural frequency
\omega_{0}
, this oscillation is slow compared to the original oscillation at
\omega_{0}
.

Transforming to the Doubly Rotating Frame:
To further simplify, we make a second transformation to a frame rotating at the drive frequency
\omega
. Define:

|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle=e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

(4.138)

The Hamiltonian in this doubly rotating frame becomes:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\hat{H}_{I}(t)e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}

(4.139)

Derivation of the
-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}
term:
This term arises from the time derivative of the rotating frame transformation. When we define
|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle=e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle
, we need to find the equation of motion for
|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle
. Taking the time derivative:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left[e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left[e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\right]|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle+e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=i\frac{\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle+e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

(4.140)

The interaction picture Schrödinger equation is:

i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle=\hat{H}_{I}(t)|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

(4.141)

Substituting this into our expression:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=i\frac{\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle+e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{H}_{I}(t)|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=i\frac{\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle-\frac{i}{\hbar}e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\hat{H}_{I}(t)e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle

(4.142)

Multiplying both sides by
i\hbar
:

i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle=-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle+e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\hat{H}_{I}(t)e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}|\psi_{rot}(t)\rangle

(4.143)

This shows that the effective Hamiltonian in the rotating frame is:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\hat{H}_{I}(t)e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}

(4.144)

The
-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}
term represents the "fictitious" energy shift due to the rotating frame, analogous to fictitious forces in classical rotating reference frames. This is why the effective detuning becomes
\Delta\omega=\omega_{0}-\omega
in the rotating frame.

To evaluate the transformation, we express the driving field in terms of ladder operators. Starting from:

\displaystyle\hat{V}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}[\sigma_{x}\cos(\omega t)+\sigma_{y}\sin(\omega t)]

(4.145)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[(\sigma_{x}-i\sigma_{y})e^{i\omega t}+(\sigma_{x}+i\sigma_{y})e^{-i\omega t}]

(4.146)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}e^{-i\omega t}+\sigma_{-}e^{i\omega t}]

(4.147)

In the interaction picture with
\hat{H}_{0}=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}
:

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{I}(t)

\displaystyle=e^{i\omega_{0}t\sigma_{z}/2}\hat{V}(t)e^{-i\omega_{0}t\sigma_{z}/2}

(4.148)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}+\sigma_{-}e^{-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}]

(4.149)

In the doubly rotating frame, the complete Hamiltonian is:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\left(\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\hat{V}_{I}(t)\right)e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}

(4.150)

Since
e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\sigma_{z}e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}=\sigma_{z}
:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=\frac{\hbar(\omega_{0}-\omega)}{2}\sigma_{z}+e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\hat{V}_{I}(t)e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}

(4.151)

For the second term, we use the transformation properties:

\displaystyle e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\sigma_{+}e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{+}e^{i\omega t}

(4.152)

\displaystyle e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\sigma_{-}e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{-}e^{-i\omega t}

(4.153)

Therefore:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=\frac{\hbar(\omega_{0}-\omega)}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}e^{i\omega t}+\sigma_{-}e^{-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}e^{-i\omega t}]

(4.154)

Defining the detuning
\Delta\omega=\omega_{0}-\omega
and expanding the exponentials:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=\frac{\hbar\Delta\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}e^{i\Delta\omega t}e^{i\omega t}+\sigma_{-}e^{-i\Delta\omega t}e^{-i\omega t}]

(4.155)

Near resonance (
\Delta\omega\ll\omega
), the exponentials
e^{\pm i\Delta\omega t}
vary slowly compared to
e^{\pm i\omega t}
. We can separate the expression into slowly and rapidly oscillating parts by writing
e^{i\Delta\omega t}\approx 1
for the slowly varying terms:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=\frac{\hbar\Delta\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}+\sigma_{-}]+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}e^{2i\omega t}+\sigma_{-}e^{-2i\omega t}]

(4.156)

The Rotating Wave Approximation:
The complete Hamiltonian in the doubly rotating frame is:

\hat{H}_{rot}(t)=\frac{\hbar\Delta\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}+\sigma_{-}]+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{4}[\sigma_{+}e^{2i\omega t}+\sigma_{-}e^{-2i\omega t}]

(4.157)

The last term oscillates rapidly at frequency
2\omega
. The rotating wave approximation (RWA) consists of neglecting these rapidly oscillating "counter-rotating" terms. The physical justification is that these terms oscillate much faster than any other timescale in the problem, so they average to zero and don’t contribute to the long-time dynamics.

Dropping the rapidly oscillating terms and using
\sigma_{+}+\sigma_{-}=2\sigma_{x}
:

\hat{H}_{eff}=\frac{\hbar\Delta\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\Omega}{2}\sigma_{x}

(4.158)

This is the effective Hamiltonian in the rotating wave approximation. It is now time-independent, allowing us to use simple exponential time evolution instead of the Dyson series.

4.8.9  Rabi Oscillations and Bloch Vector Dynamics

In the rotating frame with the rotating wave approximation, the effective Hamiltonian generates rotations about the axis
(\Omega,0,\Delta\omega)
with frequency
\Omega_{eff}=\sqrt{\Omega^{2}+(\Delta\omega)^{2}}
.

For exact resonance (
\Delta\omega=0
), the effective Hamiltonian becomes
\hat{H}_{eff}=\hbar\Omega\sigma_{x}/2
, generating rotations about the x-axis. Starting from the ground state
|+z\rangle
:

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-i\Omega t\sigma_{x}/2}|+z\rangle

(4.159)

\displaystyle=\cos(\Omega t/2)|+z\rangle-i\sin(\Omega t/2)|-z\rangle

(4.160)

The population oscillates between the two levels:

P_{excited}(t)=|\langle-z|\psi(t)\rangle|^{2}=\sin^{2}(\Omega t/2)

(4.161)

These are called Rabi oscillations, and they provide a precise method for controlling quantum states through the duration and amplitude of applied pulses.

4.9  Adiabatic Evolution and Geometric Phases

4.9.1  Slow Parameter Variation and the Adiabatic Theorem

When the parameters in a quantum system change slowly compared to the energy gaps, the system can remain in an instantaneous eigenstate while accumulating both dynamical and geometric phases. This adiabatic evolution provides a powerful tool for robust quantum state manipulation and reveals deep connections between quantum mechanics and geometry.

Consider a time-dependent Hamiltonian
\hat{H}(t)
that changes slowly over time. At each instant
t
, we can define instantaneous eigenstates
|n(t)\rangle
and eigenvalues
E_{n}(t)
:

\hat{H}(t)|n(t)\rangle=E_{n}(t)|n(t)\rangle

(4.162)

The adiabatic theorem states that if the system starts in eigenstate
|n(0)\rangle
and the Hamiltonian changes sufficiently slowly, the system will remain in the instantaneous eigenstate
|n(t)\rangle
but acquire a phase:

|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle=e^{i\phi_{n}(t)}|n(t)\rangle

(4.163)

where
|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle
is the actual state of the system (which follows the
n
-th eigenstate adiabatically) and
|n(t)\rangle
is the instantaneous eigenstate of
\hat{H}(t)
at time
t
.

The total phase has two contributions:

\displaystyle\phi_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\phi_{n}^{(dyn)}(t)+\phi_{n}^{(geom)}(t)

(4.164)

\displaystyle\phi_{n}^{(dyn)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\hbar}\int_{0}^{t}E_{n}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\quad\text{(dynamical)}

(4.165)

\displaystyle\phi_{n}^{(geom)}(t)

\displaystyle=i\int_{0}^{t}\langle n(t^{\prime})|\frac{d}{dt^{\prime}}|n(t^{\prime})\rangle dt^{\prime}\quad\text{(geometric)}

(4.166)

Origin of the Geometric Phase:
The geometric phase arises from the requirement that the state remain normalized during evolution. When we write
|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle=e^{i\phi_{n}(t)}|n(t)\rangle
, taking the time derivative and using the Schrödinger equation gives:

i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle=\hat{H}(t)|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle

(4.167)

Expanding the left side using the product rule:

i\hbar\left[i\frac{d\phi_{n}}{dt}e^{i\phi_{n}}|n(t)\rangle+e^{i\phi_{n}}\frac{d|n(t)\rangle}{dt}\right]=\hat{H}(t)e^{i\phi_{n}}|n(t)\rangle

(4.168)

Since
\hat{H}(t)|n(t)\rangle=E_{n}(t)|n(t)\rangle
, this becomes:

i\hbar\frac{d\phi_{n}}{dt}e^{i\phi_{n}}|n(t)\rangle+i\hbar e^{i\phi_{n}}\frac{d|n(t)\rangle}{dt}=E_{n}(t)e^{i\phi_{n}}|n(t)\rangle

(4.169)

Dividing through by
e^{i\phi_{n}}
(which is just a phase factor):

i\hbar\frac{d\phi_{n}}{dt}|n(t)\rangle+i\hbar\frac{d|n(t)\rangle}{dt}=E_{n}(t)|n(t)\rangle

(4.170)

Taking the inner product with
\langle n(t)|
:

i\hbar\frac{d\phi_{n}}{dt}+i\hbar\langle n(t)|\frac{d}{dt}|n(t)\rangle=E_{n}(t)

(4.171)

Rearranging:

\frac{d\phi_{n}}{dt}=-\frac{E_{n}(t)}{\hbar}-i\langle n(t)|\frac{d}{dt}|n(t)\rangle

(4.172)

Why the second term is purely real:
The inner product
\langle n(t)|\frac{d}{dt}|n(t)\rangle
is purely imaginary, not the phase itself. Since
\langle n(t)|n(t)\rangle=1
for all
t
, taking the time derivative:

\frac{d}{dt}\langle n(t)|n(t)\rangle=\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}n(t)\middle|n(t)\right\rangle+\left\langle n(t)\middle|\frac{d}{dt}n(t)\right\rangle=0

(4.173)

This means:

\left\langle n(t)\middle|\frac{d}{dt}n(t)\right\rangle=-\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}n(t)\middle|n(t)\right\rangle=-\left\langle n(t)\middle|\frac{d}{dt}n(t)\right\rangle^{*}

(4.174)

So
\langle n(t)|\frac{d}{dt}|n(t)\rangle
is purely imaginary. When multiplied by
-i
in the equation for
d\phi_{n}/dt
, the second term becomes purely real. Therefore, both terms on the right side contribute real values to the phase.

Integrating yields the two contributions: the first term gives the dynamical phase (energy-dependent), while the second term gives the geometric phase. This geometric phase depends only on how the instantaneous eigenstate
|n(t)\rangle
evolves along its path in Hilbert space, not on the rate of evolution or the energy. It is this path-dependence that gives the phase its geometric character.

The dynamical phase depends on the energy, while the geometric phase depends only on the path traced by the state vector in Hilbert space.

4.9.2  Berry Phase Equals Half the Solid Angle for Two-Level Systems

For a two-level system undergoing adiabatic evolution around a closed path, the geometric Berry phase is exactly half the solid angle enclosed by the path on the Bloch sphere. We now derive this remarkable result.

Setup:
Consider a two-level Hamiltonian where a magnetic field of constant magnitude traces a path on the unit sphere:

\hat{H}(t)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\vec{B}(t)\cdot\vec{\sigma}

(4.175)

where
\vec{B}(t)
is a unit vector parameterized by spherical coordinates
(\theta(t),\phi(t))
:

\vec{B}(t)=(\sin\theta(t)\cos\phi(t),\sin\theta(t)\sin\phi(t),\cos\theta(t))

(4.176)

The Hamiltonian depends on time through the magnetic field direction. The energy splitting between the two levels is
\hbar\omega_{0}
, with eigenvalues
\pm\hbar\omega_{0}/2
. As time evolves and the field traces a path on the sphere, the parameters
(\theta(t),\phi(t))
change, causing the instantaneous eigenstate to evolve.

Instantaneous Eigenstates:
At each instant, the ground state eigenstate (lower energy) is:

|\psi_{-}(\theta,\phi)\rangle=\cos(\theta/2)|+z\rangle-e^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2)|-z\rangle

(4.177)

This eigenstate points opposite to
\vec{B}(t)
on the Bloch sphere (spin anti-aligned with the field).

The Berry Connection:
From equation (4.174), the geometric phase is:

\phi^{(geom)}=i\int_{0}^{t}\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{d}{dt^{\prime}}|\psi_{-}\rangle dt^{\prime}

(4.178)

Using the chain rule:

\frac{d}{dt}|\psi_{-}\rangle=\frac{\partial|\psi_{-}\rangle}{\partial\theta}\frac{d\theta}{dt}+\frac{\partial|\psi_{-}\rangle}{\partial\phi}\frac{d\phi}{dt}

(4.179)

Substituting and changing variables from time to path parameters:

\phi^{(geom)}=i\int_{\text{path}}\left[\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|\psi_{-}\rangle d\theta+\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}|\psi_{-}\rangle d\phi\right]

(4.180)

We define the
Berry connection
:

\vec{A}(\theta,\phi)=i\langle\psi_{-}|\nabla|\psi_{-}\rangle=\left(A_{\theta},A_{\phi}\right)

(4.181)

where:

\displaystyle A_{\theta}

\displaystyle=i\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|\psi_{-}\rangle

(4.182)

\displaystyle A_{\phi}

\displaystyle=i\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}|\psi_{-}\rangle

(4.183)

The geometric phase for a closed path
\mathcal{C}
is:

\phi^{(geom)}=\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\vec{A}\cdot d\vec{r}=\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{\theta}d\theta+A_{\phi}d\phi)

(4.184)

Computing the Berry Connection:

For
A_{\phi}
, taking the
\phi
-derivative:

\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}|\psi_{-}\rangle=-ie^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2)|-z\rangle

(4.185)

Therefore:

\displaystyle A_{\phi}

\displaystyle=i\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}|\psi_{-}\rangle=i\left[-ie^{-i\phi}\sin(\theta/2)\right]\left[-ie^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2)\right]=-\sin^{2}(\theta/2)=-\frac{1-\cos\theta}{2}

(4.186)

For
A_{\theta}
, taking the
\theta
-derivative:

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|\psi_{-}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin(\theta/2)|+z\rangle-\frac{1}{2}e^{i\phi}\cos(\theta/2)|-z\rangle

(4.187)

Computing the inner product:

A_{\theta}=i\langle\psi_{-}|\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|\psi_{-}\rangle=i(-i\cos(\theta/2)\sin(\theta/2))=\frac{1}{2}\sin\theta

(4.188)

From Line Integral to Solid Angle:
Using Stokes’ theorem, we convert the line integral to a surface integral:

\phi^{(geom)}=\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\vec{A}\cdot d\vec{r}=\iint_{S}(\nabla\times\vec{A})\cdot d\vec{S}

(4.189)

The Berry curvature in spherical coordinates is:

\nabla\times\vec{A}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta A_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial\theta}\right]\hat{r}

(4.190)

Substituting our expressions:

\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(-\frac{1-\cos\theta}{2}\right)\right]\hat{r}

(4.191)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\left[\sin\theta\cos\theta+\frac{\sin\theta}{2}\right]\hat{r}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{r}

(4.192)

With surface element
d\vec{S}=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi\,\hat{r}
:

\phi^{(geom)}=\iint_{S}\frac{1}{2}\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi=\frac{1}{2}\Omega

(4.193)

where
\Omega=\iint_{S}\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi
is the solid angle enclosed by the path.

With the conventional sign:

\boxed{\phi_{geom}=-\frac{1}{2}\Omega}

(4.194)

Physical Interpretation:
Why half the solid angle? This factor arises from the spinorial nature of spin-1/2 wavefunctions. A
2\pi
rotation does not return the wavefunction to itself but picks up a minus sign:

e^{-i(2\pi)\hat{n}\cdot\vec{\sigma}/2}=-\hat{I}

(4.195)

The rotation group SO(3) is represented projectively as SU(2) for spin-1/2 systems. The Berry phase reflects this: when the magnetic field traces a closed path on the sphere, the quantum state acquires a phase that is half the enclosed solid angle.

Geometrically, the Bloch sphere represents quantum states, but physically distinct states correspond to rays in Hilbert space (states differing by a phase are equivalent). The solid angle
\Omega
measures the path geometry on the Bloch sphere, while the factor of 1/2 accounts for the spinorial structure of the quantum state space.

This result unifies geometry (solid angle), topology (Berry phase), and quantum mechanics (spinor nature), providing a direct experimental signature of these fundamental properties.

For a spin-1/2 system in a slowly rotating magnetic field, the geometric phase has a particularly elegant form. Consider a magnetic field of constant magnitude that traces out a closed path on the unit sphere:

\vec{B}(t)=B(\sin\theta(t)\cos\phi(t),\sin\theta(t)\sin\phi(t),\cos\theta(t))

(4.196)

If the system starts in the ground state and follows the field adiabatically, after one complete cycle it acquires a geometric phase:

\phi_{geom}=-\frac{1}{2}\Omega

(4.197)

where
\Omega
is the solid angle enclosed by the path on the unit sphere. This Berry phase depends only on the geometry of the path, not on the rate of traversal.

Example 7

Berry Phase Visualization with the Bloch Cube
The Bloch cube provides an excellent visualization tool for understanding the Berry phase. Consider a quantum state that starts at one face of the cube and is adiabatically transported around a closed loop on the cube’s surface.

Triangular Path on the Bloch Cube:

Start with a state at the north pole of the Bloch sphere (top face of cube,
|+z\rangle
). Follow this path:
1. Move to the equator along the positive x-axis (
|+x\rangle
)
2. Move along the equator to the positive y-axis (
|+y\rangle
)
3. Return to the north pole

This triangular path on the Bloch sphere encloses a solid angle of
\Omega=\pi/2
steradians (one-eighth of the sphere). The Berry phase acquired is:

\phi_{Berry}=-\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{\pi}{2}=-\frac{\pi}{4}

(4.198)

You can visualize this on the Bloch cube: the path connects three adjacent vertices, forming a triangular face of the octahedron inscribed in the cube. The Berry phase is proportional to the area of this triangle projected onto the unit sphere.

Square Path on the Bloch Cube:

For a path that traverses four faces of the cube in sequence (e.g.,
|+z\rangle\to|+x\rangle\to|-z\rangle\to|-x\rangle\to|+z\rangle
), the enclosed solid angle is
\Omega=2\pi
steradians (a hemisphere), giving:

\phi_{Berry}=-\pi

(4.199)

This is precisely a sign flip—the state returns with opposite phase, demonstrating the topological nature of the Berry phase.

Example 8

Berry Phase for a Spin-1/2 Particle
Consider a magnetic field that traces a
closed path
: starting along the z-axis, tilting to make angle
\alpha
with the z-axis, rotating once around the z-axis at this tilt angle, then returning to the z-axis to close the loop.

The Berry phase is defined only for this complete closed path. To calculate the solid angle enclosed by this closed curve on the unit sphere, we can break it into segments:

Phase 1 (Tilting down):
The path from north pole to latitude
\alpha
traces a meridian line (zero width), contributing no area.

Phase 2 (Rotation at fixed latitude):
The circular path at constant
\theta=\alpha
with
\phi:0\to 2\pi
forms the "base" of a spherical cap. Together with the paths to and from the north pole, this encloses a spherical cap of solid angle
\Omega=2\pi(1-\cos\alpha)
.

Phase 3 (Tilting back up):
The return path along the same meridian closes the loop but adds no additional enclosed area.

The key insight is that the
total solid angle enclosed by the entire closed path
is
\Omega=2\pi(1-\cos\alpha)
, which comes entirely from the latitude circle at angle
\alpha
. The meridian segments are necessary to close the path but don’t contribute to the enclosed area (like the sides of a cone that meet at the apex).

The Berry phase for the complete closed path is:

\phi_{geom}=-\frac{1}{2}\Omega=-\pi(1-\cos\alpha)

(4.200)

For
\alpha=\pi/2
(equatorial loop), the closed path encloses a hemisphere:
\phi_{geom}=-\pi
. For
\alpha=\pi
(great circle through south pole), the closed path encloses the entire sphere:
\phi_{geom}=-2\pi
. The phase depends only on the solid angle enclosed by the closed curve, providing a topological characterization of the evolution.

4.10  Many-Body Hamiltonians: From Single Particles to Quantum Fields

4.10.1  The Hopping Hamiltonian and Translational Invariance

Having established how Hamiltonians generate time evolution for single two-level systems, we now extend this formalism to many-particle systems on a lattice. Recall from Chapter 3 that we can represent particles on a lattice using creation and annihilation operators
\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{a}_{i}
. The natural dynamics for such systems involve particles hopping between lattice sites.

The hopping Hamiltonian in second quantization takes the form:

\hat{H}_{\text{hop}}=-\Delta\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}(\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i})

(4.201)

where
\langle i,j\rangle
denotes nearest neighbors and
\Delta>0
is the hopping amplitude.

For a periodic chain with
N
sites (indexed from 0 to
N-1
), this becomes:

\hat{H}=-\Delta\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j+1}+\hat{a}_{j+1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j})

(4.202)

where site indices are understood modulo
N
(so site
N
is identified with site 0). Here and throughout this section, we use indices
j,k,l
for position space (lattice sites) and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
for momentum space.

4.10.2  Momentum Eigenstates as the Natural Basis

The key insight is that the hopping Hamiltonian has translational symmetry. Define the translation operator:

\hat{T}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\hat{a}_{j+1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}

(4.203)

Since
[\hat{H},\hat{T}]=0
, we can find simultaneous eigenstates of both operators. These are the momentum eigenstates, defined in the single-particle sector as:

|k_{\alpha}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ik_{\alpha}j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|\text{vac}\rangle

(4.204)

where the allowed momenta for periodic boundary conditions are:

k_{\alpha}=\frac{2\pi\alpha}{N},\quad\alpha=0,1,2,\ldots,N-1

(4.205)

These states satisfy:

\displaystyle\hat{T}|k_{\alpha}\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{ik_{\alpha}}|k_{\alpha}\rangle

(4.206)

\displaystyle\hat{H}|k_{\alpha}\rangle

\displaystyle=E(k_{\alpha})|k_{\alpha}\rangle

(4.207)

where the dispersion relation is:

E(k_{\alpha})=-2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)

(4.208)

4.10.3  General Time Evolution for N Sites

Following the pattern established in equation (4.148), we can write the complete time evolution for a particle on an N-site ring. In the position basis, we expand the wavefunction as:

|\psi(t)\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\psi_{j}(t)|j\rangle

(4.209)

where
|j\rangle
denotes a particle at site
j
and
\psi_{j}(t)
is the amplitude at site
j
at time
t
.

The transformation from position basis to momentum basis is:

|k_{\alpha}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ik_{\alpha}j}|j\rangle

(4.210)

where
k_{\alpha}=2\pi\alpha/N
for
\alpha=0,1,2,\ldots,N-1
.

The inverse transformation from momentum to position basis is:

|j\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}e^{-ik_{\alpha}j}|k_{\alpha}\rangle

(4.211)

In the momentum basis, time evolution is diagonal:

\hat{U}(t)|k_{\alpha}\rangle=e^{-iE(k_{\alpha})t/\hbar}|k_{\alpha}\rangle=e^{i2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)t/\hbar}|k_{\alpha}\rangle

(4.212)

Now, for an initial state with amplitudes
\psi_{k}(0)
:

|\psi(0)\rangle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\psi_{k}(0)|k\rangle

(4.213)

The time-evolved state is:

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{U}(t)|\psi(0)\rangle

(4.214)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}(t)\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\psi_{k}(0)|k\rangle

(4.215)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\psi_{k}(0)\hat{U}(t)|k\rangle

(4.216)

To find
\psi_{j}(t)=\langle j|\psi(t)\rangle
, we compute:

\displaystyle\psi_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\psi_{k}(0)\langle j|\hat{U}(t)|k\rangle

(4.217)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\psi_{k}(0)[\hat{U}(t)]_{jk}

(4.218)

The matrix element
[\hat{U}(t)]_{jk}
is calculated using the momentum eigenbasis:

\displaystyle[\hat{U}(t)]_{jk}

\displaystyle=\langle j|\hat{U}(t)|k\rangle

(4.219)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}\langle j|\hat{U}(t)|k_{\alpha}\rangle\langle k_{\alpha}|k\rangle

(4.220)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}\langle j|k_{\alpha}\rangle e^{-iE(k_{\alpha})t/\hbar}\langle k_{\alpha}|k\rangle

(4.221)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}e^{-ik_{\alpha}j}\cdot e^{i2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)t/\hbar}\cdot e^{ik_{\alpha}k}

(4.222)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}e^{ik_{\alpha}(k-j)}e^{i2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)t/\hbar}

(4.223)

Therefore, the complete time evolution in the position basis is:

\boxed{\psi_{j}(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}e^{ik_{\alpha}(k-j)}e^{i2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)t/\hbar}\right]\psi_{k}(0)}

(4.224)

This can be written in matrix form as:

\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0}(t)\\
\psi_{1}(t)\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{N-1}(t)\end{pmatrix}=\hat{S}\cdot\hat{U}_{\text{diag}}(t)\cdot\hat{S}^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0}(0)\\
\psi_{1}(0)\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{N-1}(0)\end{pmatrix}

(4.225)

where
\hat{S}
is the matrix transforming to the momentum basis,
\hat{U}_{\text{diag}}(t)
contains the diagonal phase factors
e^{i2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)t/\hbar}
, and
\hat{S}^{\dagger}
transforms back to the position basis.

This general formula demonstrates the triple product structure:

•

First, transform from position to momentum basis via
\hat{S}^{\dagger}

•

Then, apply diagonal time evolution via
\hat{U}_{\text{diag}}(t)

•

Finally, transform back to position basis via
\hat{S}

For a particle initially localized at site
k_{0}
, we have
\psi_{k}(0)=\delta_{k,k_{0}}
, and the formula simplifies to:

\psi_{j}(t)=[\hat{U}(t)]_{j,k_{0}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{N-1}e^{ik_{\alpha}(k_{0}-j)}e^{i2\Delta\cos(k_\alpha)t/\hbar}

(4.226)

This shows how the particle amplitude spreads from site
k_{0}
to all other sites through quantum hopping.

4.10.4  Explicit Calculation for Three Sites

Consider a ring of three sites (indexed 0, 1, 2) with periodic boundaries. The Hamiltonian in the position basis
\{|100\rangle,|010\rangle,|001\rangle\}
is:

\hat{H}=-\Delta\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\
1&0&1\\
1&1&0\end{pmatrix}

(4.227)

The momentum eigenstates are:

\displaystyle|k_{\alpha=0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|100\rangle+|010\rangle+|001\rangle),\quad k_{0}=0

(4.228)

\displaystyle|k_{\alpha=1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|100\rangle+e^{i2\pi/3}|010\rangle+e^{i4\pi/3}|001\rangle),\quad k_{1}=\frac{2\pi}{3}

(4.229)

\displaystyle|k_{\alpha=2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|100\rangle+e^{i4\pi/3}|010\rangle+e^{i2\pi/3}|001\rangle),\quad k_{2}=\frac{4\pi}{3}

(4.230)

The corresponding energies are:

\displaystyle E(k_{0})

\displaystyle=-2\Delta\cos(k_0)=-2\Delta

(4.231)

\displaystyle E(k_{1})

\displaystyle=-2\Delta\cos(k_1)=\Delta

(4.232)

\displaystyle E(k_{2})

\displaystyle=-2\Delta\cos(k_2)=\Delta

(4.233)

4.10.5  Three-Site Example: Explicit Matrix Calculation

In the momentum basis, the Hamiltonian is diagonal:

\hat{H}_{\text{diag}}=\begin{pmatrix}-2\Delta&0&0\\
0&\Delta&0\\
0&0&\Delta\end{pmatrix}

(4.234)

The time evolution operator in the momentum basis is:

\hat{U}_{\text{diag}}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}_{\text{diag}}t/\hbar}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i2\Delta t/\hbar}&0&0\\
0&e^{-i\Delta t/\hbar}&0\\
0&0&e^{-i\Delta t/\hbar}\end{pmatrix}

(4.235)

To find the time evolution operator in the position basis, we need the unitary transformation matrix
\hat{S}
whose columns are the momentum eigenstates:

\hat{S}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
1&e^{i2\pi/3}&e^{i4\pi/3}\\
1&e^{i4\pi/3}&e^{i2\pi/3}\end{pmatrix}

(4.236)

The inverse transformation is
\hat{S}^{\dagger}=\hat{S}^{*}
(complex conjugate):

\hat{S}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
1&e^{-i2\pi/3}&e^{-i4\pi/3}\\
1&e^{-i4\pi/3}&e^{-i2\pi/3}\end{pmatrix}

(4.237)

The full time evolution operator in the position basis is:

\hat{U}(t)=\hat{S}\hat{U}_{\text{diag}}(t)\hat{S}^{\dagger}

(4.238)

Explicitly:

\displaystyle\hat{U}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
1&e^{i2\pi/3}&e^{i4\pi/3}\\
1&e^{i4\pi/3}&e^{i2\pi/3}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}e^{i2\Delta t/\hbar}&0&0\\
0&e^{-i\Delta t/\hbar}&0\\
0&0&e^{-i\Delta t/\hbar}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
1&e^{-i2\pi/3}&e^{-i4\pi/3}\\
1&e^{-i4\pi/3}&e^{-i2\pi/3}\end{pmatrix}

(4.239)

After matrix multiplication:

[\hat{U}(t)]_{jk}=\frac{1}{3}\left[e^{i2\Delta t/\hbar}+2e^{-i\Delta t/\hbar}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi(j-k)}{3}\right)\right]

(4.240)

This gives the complete quantum dynamics for a particle hopping on a three-site ring.

4.10.6  Many-Body Dynamics Under the Hopping Hamiltonian

The beauty of second quantization is that the same Hamiltonian describes dynamics for any number of particles:

Example 9

Two-Particle Hopping Dynamics
For two particles initially at sites 1 and 2:

\displaystyle\hat{H}|110\rangle

\displaystyle=-\Delta(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}+\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{3}+\hat{a}_{3}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2})|110\rangle

(4.241)

Let’s evaluate term by term:

•

\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}|110\rangle=0
(site 1 already occupied)

•

\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}|110\rangle=0
(site 2 already occupied)

•

\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{3}|110\rangle=0
(no particle at site 3)

•

\hat{a}_{3}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}|110\rangle=|101\rangle
(particle hops 2
\to
3)

Result:
\hat{H}|110\rangle=-\Delta|101\rangle

The hard-core constraint naturally emerges—particles cannot hop to occupied sites!

4.10.7  Time Evolution of Many-Body States

The time evolution operator for the hopping Hamiltonian is:

\hat{U}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}_{\text{hop}}t/\hbar}

(4.242)

For states within a fixed particle number sector, this evolution conserves particle number since:

[\hat{H}_{\text{hop}},\hat{N}]=0

(4.243)

where
\hat{N}=\sum_{i}\hat{n}_{i}
is the total particle number operator.

Example 10

Evolution of a Localized Two-Particle State
Consider two particles initially localized at adjacent sites on a 4-site ring:

|\psi(0)\rangle=|1100\rangle

(4.244)

The time evolution involves:
1. Both particles can hop to their respective neighboring sites
2. The hard-core constraint prevents them from occupying the same site
3. The resulting dynamics exhibits both single-particle hopping and two-particle correlations

For short times:

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle

\displaystyle\approx|1100\rangle-\frac{it}{\hbar}\hat{H}|1100\rangle+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})

(4.245)

\displaystyle\approx|1100\rangle-\frac{it}{\hbar}(-\Delta)(|0110\rangle+|1001\rangle)

(4.246)

\displaystyle=|1100\rangle+\frac{i\Delta t}{\hbar}(|0110\rangle+|1001\rangle)

(4.247)

The particles begin to spread while avoiding each other—a purely quantum many-body effect!

4.10.8  From Discrete to Continuous: The Continuum Limit

The lattice hopping Hamiltonian connects to continuum physics in the limit of small lattice spacing. For a particle with wave function
\psi_{j}
at site
j=xa/a
(where
a
is the lattice constant):

\displaystyle-\Delta(\psi_{j+1}+\psi_{j-1}-2\psi_{j})

\displaystyle\approx-\Delta a^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial x^{2}}

(4.248)

This identifies:

\Delta=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2ma^{2}}

(4.249)

The hopping Hamiltonian thus discretizes the kinetic energy operator of continuous quantum mechanics. This connection reveals that:

•

Lattice models capture essential physics of continuous systems

•

Hopping amplitude
\Delta
relates to particle mass and lattice spacing

•

Band structure emerges from discrete translational symmetry

•

Many-body effects persist in the continuum limit

Diagonalization reveals the natural basis for evolution: energy eigenstates acquire only phase factors
e^{-iE_{n}t/\hbar}
while superpositions develop complex interference. The time evolution operator decomposes as a triple matrix product
\hat{U}(t)=\hat{V}\hat{U}_{\text{diag}}(t)\hat{V}^{\dagger}
, transforming to the eigenbasis, applying diagonal phases, then transforming back.

Conservation laws emerge from commutation relations: observables satisfying
[\hat{H},\hat{A}]=0
have constant expectation values, connecting symmetries to conserved quantities through Noether’s theorem. The Heisenberg picture
\hat{A}_{H}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{A}_{S}e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}
transfers time dependence from states to operators.

Time-dependent Hamiltonians require time-ordered exponentials
\mathcal{T}\exp(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int\hat{H}(t')dt')
. The driven two-level system in NMR demonstrates how the rotating frame and rotating wave approximation reduce time-dependent problems to effective time-independent ones, revealing universal Rabi oscillations. Adiabatic evolution accumulates geometric Berry phases that depend only on the path through parameter space, not the rate of traversal.

The hopping Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=-\Delta\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}(\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i})
extends these principles to many-body systems. Momentum eigenstates
|k_{n}\rangle
with dispersion
E(k_{n})=-2\Delta\cos(k_n)
diagonalize the evolution. The general solution
\psi_{j}(t)=\sum_{i}[\hat{U}(t)]_{ji}\psi_{i}(0)
maintains the triple product structure, showing how single-particle and many-body dynamics share the same mathematical framework of unitary evolution generated by Hermitian operators.

4.11  Problems

Problem 4.1

(Verifying Unitarity and the Group Property)

Consider the time evolution operator
\hat{U}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}
for a time-independent Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
.

(a)

Show that if
\hat{H}
is Hermitian (
\hat{H}^{\dagger}=\hat{H}
), then
\hat{U}(t)
is unitary for all real times
t

(b)

Verify the group property:
\hat{U}(t_{1})\hat{U}(t_{2})=\hat{U}(t_{1}+t_{2})

(c)

Show that
\hat{U}(-t)=\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)
. What is the physical meaning of this relationship?

(d)

For the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=\hbar\omega\sigma_{z}/2
, explicitly calculate
\hat{U}(\pi/\omega)
and verify it is unitary

(e)

Starting from the infinitesimal form
\hat{U}(dt)=\hat{I}-i\hat{H}dt/\hbar
, derive the differential equation for
\hat{U}(t)

Problem 4.2

(From Discrete Gates to Continuous Evolution)

The Hadamard gate
\hat{H}
and the

gate are fundamental quantum gates:

1&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\hat{T}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{i\pi/4}\end{pmatrix}

(4.250)

(a)

such that

for some time

(b)

Find a Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{2}
such that

for some time
t_{2}

Are your Hamiltonians unique? If not, find another valid choice for each

(d)

||\hat{H}||\leq E_{max}
?

(e)

Show that any single-qubit unitary can be generated by some Hamiltonian evolution

(Compound Rotations and the General Rotation Formula)

.

(a)

Problem 4.15

(Trotter Error Scaling)

The Trotter formula decomposes time evolution into small steps:

?

for

in the rotating frame

Problem 4.17

(Quantum Zeno Effect)

Chapter 5  Continuous Space and the Quantum Harmonic Oscillator

5.1  Introduction

Key Idea: From Discrete Lattice to Continuous Wave Function

As the lattice spacing approaches zero, the discrete amplitude at each site transforms into a continuous wave function
\psi(x)
. Traditional wave mechanics—particles in boxes, harmonic oscillators, quantum tunneling—emerges from the discrete qubit framework developed in previous chapters.

Chapter 3 showed that a single particle on
n
lattice sites occupies an
n
-dimensional subspace within the full
2^{n}
-dimensional Hilbert space. We now pass to the continuum limit: the lattice spacing
a\to 0
, the number of sites
n\to\infty
, while the total system size
L=na
remains fixed.

The limit transforms:

•

Discrete position states
|x_{i}\rangle
into continuous states
|x\rangle

•

Sums into integrals

•

Finite-difference operators into differential operators

Differential operators come from finite differences, boundary conditions from lattice edges, and wave functions from continuous limits of discrete amplitudes.

5.1.1  Discrete vs Continuous: Two Perspectives

The discrete-continuous connection is important theoretically and practically:

•

Computational:
Numerical solutions require discretization. Understanding the lattice-continuum connection improves numerical accuracy.

•

Physical:
Many systems (quantum dots, optical lattices, qubit arrays) are inherently discrete. The discrete formulation is exact for these systems.

•

Fundamental:
Whether space is discrete at the Planck scale remains an open question in quantum gravity.

The formulations are complementary.

5.2  From Discrete Lattice to Continuous Space

5.2.1  Position States in the Discrete and Continuous Pictures

On a one-dimensional lattice with spacing
a
, the discrete positions are labeled
x_{j}=ja
for
j=1,2,...,n
. A particle localized at site
j
is described by the state
|x_{j}\rangle=|0...010...0\rangle
, where the single 1 appears at position
j
. These
n
states form an orthonormal basis for the single-particle sector:

\langle x_{j}|x_{k}\rangle=\delta_{jk}

(5.1)

The completeness relation for the discrete position basis reads:

\sum_{j=1}^{n}|x_{j}\rangle\langle x_{j}|=\hat{I}_{\text{single-particle}}

(5.2)

In the continuum limit, the discrete index
j
becomes the continuous variable
x
, the lattice spacing
a
approaches zero, and the number of sites
n
approaches infinity such that the system size
L=na
remains fixed.

The transformations are:

•

Kronecker delta
\delta_{jk}
becomes Dirac delta
\delta(x-x^{\prime})

•

Discrete sums
\sum_{i}
become integrals
\int dx

•

Finite-dimensional Hilbert space becomes infinite-dimensional

The orthonormality relation becomes:

\langle x|x^{\prime}\rangle=\delta(x-x^{\prime})

(5.3)

Discrete states are normalizable (
\langle x_{j}|x_{j}\rangle=1
), but continuous position eigenstates are distributions, not functions—reflecting that perfect localization at a point is physically impossible.

Example 1: From Discrete Delta to Dirac Delta

Consider the relationship between localized states on a lattice and in the continuum.

On the lattice:
A particle perfectly localized at site
j_{0}
(where
x_{j_{0}}=j_{0}a
) has the wave function:

\psi_{j}=\delta_{j,j_{0}}

(5.4)

Normalization:
\sum_{j}|\psi_{j}|^{2}=1
.

Continuum correspondence:
To connect to the continuum, we must account for the density of states. The continuum wave function
\psi(x)
relates to the discrete amplitudes via:

\psi(x_{j})=\frac{\psi_{j}}{\sqrt{a}}

(5.5)

This scaling ensures that in the continuum limit:

\sum_{j}|\psi_{j}|^{2}=\sum_{j}a\cdot\left|\frac{\psi_{j}}{\sqrt{a}}\right|^{2}\to\int dx|\psi(x)|^{2}

(5.6)

For perfect localization at
x_{0}
:

•

Discrete:
\psi_{j}=\delta_{j,j_{0}}

•

Continuum:
\psi(x)=\delta(x-x_{0})

The
1/\sqrt{a}
scaling ensures proper normalization in the transition from sums to integrals.

5.2.2  The Wave Function as Continuous Amplitude

A general single-particle state on the lattice takes the form:

|\psi\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\psi_{j}|x_{j}\rangle

(5.7)

where
\psi_{j}
represents the probability amplitude for finding the particle at site
j
.

The transformation requires normalization:

Example 2: Normalization in the Continuum Limit

Define the continuum wave function as
\psi(x)=\psi_{j}/\sqrt{a}
where
x=ja
. Then:

\displaystyle\sum_{j}|\psi_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}a|\psi(x_{j})|^{2}

(5.8)

\displaystyle\to\int dx\,|\psi(x)|^{2}

(5.9)

The factor
1/\sqrt{a}
ensures the integral converges to the sum as
a\to 0
.

In the continuum limit, the state becomes:

|\psi\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,\psi(x)|x\rangle

(5.10)

The coefficient function
\psi(x)=\langle x|\psi\rangle
is the wave function—the continuous probability amplitude for finding the particle at position
x
.

5.2.3  The Mathematical Structure of Wave Functions

Wave functions belong to the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of square-integrable functions:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\psi(x)|^{2}dx<\infty

(5.11)

This requirement ensures that the total probability is finite and can be normalized to unity. The inner product in this space is:

\langle\phi|\psi\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi^{*}(x)\psi(x)dx

(5.12)

Properties of the wave function space:

Example 3: Properties of the Wave Function Space

1. Linearity:
If
\psi_{1}(x)
and
\psi_{2}(x)
are valid wave functions, so is any linear combination:

\psi(x)=c_{1}\psi_{1}(x)+c_{2}\psi_{2}(x)

(5.13)

where
|c_{1}|^{2}+|c_{2}|^{2}=1
for normalization.

2. Completeness:
Any square-integrable function can be expanded in a complete basis:

\psi(x)=\sum_{n}c_{n}\phi_{n}(x)

(5.14)

where
\{\phi_{n}(x)\}
forms an orthonormal basis.

3. Continuity:
Physical wave functions and their derivatives must be continuous except at infinite potential jumps. This ensures finite momentum uncertainty.

4. Boundary behavior:
For bound states,
\psi(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
fast enough to be square-integrable.

5.2.4  Inner Products and Probability

The inner product between states transforms from a discrete sum to a continuous integral:

Example 4: Inner Product Evolution

Discrete lattice:

\langle\phi|\psi\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\phi_{j}^{*}\psi_{j}

(5.15)

Continuum limit (with proper normalization):

\langle\phi|\psi\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,\phi^{*}(x)\psi(x)

(5.16)

For orthogonal states on a three-site lattice:

\displaystyle\psi_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,0,-1)

(5.17)

\displaystyle\psi_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(1,-2,1)

(5.18)

We have
\sum_{j}\psi_{1}^{*}[j]\psi_{2}[j]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(1-0-1)=0
.

The continuum analogs are orthogonal functions like
\sin(x)
and
\cos(x)
.

5.2.5  The Emergence of Differential Operators

The nearest-neighbor hopping Hamiltonian on the lattice was:

\hat{H}_{\text{hop}}=-t\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(|x_{j+1}\rangle\langle x_{j}|+|x_{j}\rangle\langle x_{j+1}|)

(5.19)

The hopping operator becomes a differential operator as follows:

Example 5: Detailed Derivation of the Kinetic Energy Operator

The hopping Hamiltonian acts on a lattice wave function as:

(\hat{H}_{\text{hop}}\psi)_{j}=-t(\psi_{j+1}+\psi_{j-1})

(5.20)

Using Taylor expansion around
x_{j}=ja
:

\displaystyle\psi_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\psi(x_{j}+a)=\psi(x_{j})+a\psi^{\prime}(x_{j})+\frac{a^{2}}{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(x_{j})+\frac{a^{3}}{6}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{j})+O(a^{4})

(5.21)

\displaystyle\psi_{j-1}

\displaystyle=\psi(x_{j}-a)=\psi(x_{j})-a\psi^{\prime}(x_{j})+\frac{a^{2}}{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(x_{j})-\frac{a^{3}}{6}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{j})+O(a^{4})

(5.22)

Adding these:

\psi_{j+1}+\psi_{j-1}=2\psi(x_{j})+a^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(x_{j})+O(a^{4})

(5.23)

Therefore:

(\hat{H}_{\text{hop}}\psi)_{j}=-t[2\psi(x_{j})+a^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(x_{j})]+O(a^{4})

(5.24)

Setting
t=\hbar^{2}/(2ma^{2})
and shifting the energy zero to remove the constant term:

\hat{H}_{\text{hop}}\to-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}=\hat{T}

(5.25)

The parameter
t=\hbar^{2}/(2ma^{2})
where:

•

t
: hopping amplitude (energy scale for tunneling)

•

m
: particle mass (inertia)

•

a
: lattice spacing

•

\hbar
: action scale

5.2.6  Momentum States and Dispersion Relations

The continuum limit also transforms how we understand momentum states and their energy-momentum relationships:

Example 6: Continuum Limit of Momentum States

The discrete momentum states on a lattice with periodic boundary conditions are:

|k_{m}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e^{ik_{m}j}|ja\rangle

(5.26)

where
k_{m}=2\pi m/L
with
m=0,1,...,n-1
.

In the continuum limit, these become plane waves:

\psi_{k}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}e^{ikx}

(5.27)

The dispersion relation transforms from On the lattice:

E_{m}=-2t\cos(k_m a)

(5.28)

For small
k_{m}a
(long wavelengths compared to lattice spacing):

\displaystyle E_{m}

\displaystyle=-2t\cos(k_m a)

(5.29)

\displaystyle\approx-2t\left(1-\frac{(k_{m}a)^{2}}{2}\right)\quad\text{for }k_{m}a\ll 1

(5.30)

\displaystyle=-2t+\frac{\hbar^{2}k_{m}^{2}}{2m}

(5.31)

Dropping the constant energy shift, we recover the free particle dispersion:

E=\frac{\hbar^{2}k^{2}}{2m}=\frac{p^{2}}{2m}

(5.32)

The quadratic free-particle dispersion emerges from the lattice cosine dispersion at long wavelengths.

5.2.7  The Position Representation of Quantum Mechanics

In the position representation, quantum mechanical operators become differential operators acting on wave functions.

Operator Dictionary: Discrete to Continuous

Physical Quantity

Discrete (Lattice)

Continuous (Position)

Position

\hat{x}=\sum_{j}x_{j}|x_{j}\rangle\langle x_{j}|

\hat{x}=x
(multiplication)

Translation by
a

\hat{T}_{a}=\sum_{j}|x_{j+1}\rangle\langle x_{j}|

\hat{T}_{a}=e^{-i\hat{p}a/\hbar}

Momentum

\hat{p}\approx-i\hbar\frac{\hat{T}_{a}-\hat{T}_{-a}}{2a}

\hat{p}=-i\hbar\frac{d}{dx}

Kinetic Energy

\hat{T}=-t(\hat{T}_{a}+\hat{T}_{-a}-2\hat{I})

\hat{T}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}

Number at site

\hat{n}_{j}=|x_{j}\rangle\langle x_{j}|

\delta(x-x_{j})

The momentum operator emerges from considering infinitesimal translations:

Example 7: Deriving the Momentum Operator

For a small translation by distance
\epsilon
:

\psi(x-\epsilon)=\psi(x)-\epsilon\frac{d\psi}{dx}+O(\epsilon^{2})

(5.33)

The translation operator is:

\hat{T}_{\epsilon}=1-\epsilon\frac{d}{dx}=1-\frac{i\epsilon}{\hbar}(-i\hbar\frac{d}{dx})

(5.34)

Identifying the generator of translations as momentum:

\hat{T}_{\epsilon}=1-\frac{i\epsilon}{\hbar}\hat{p}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{p}=-i\hbar\frac{d}{dx}

(5.35)

For finite translations:
\hat{T}_{a}=e^{-i\hat{p}a/\hbar}
.

5.2.8  Commutation Relations

The fundamental commutation relation emerges naturally:

Example 8: Position-Momentum Commutator

\displaystyle[\hat{x},\hat{p}]\psi(x)

\displaystyle=x(-i\hbar\frac{d\psi}{dx})-(-i\hbar\frac{d}{dx})(x\psi)

(5.36)

\displaystyle=-i\hbar x\frac{d\psi}{dx}-(-i\hbar)\left(\psi+x\frac{d\psi}{dx}\right)

(5.37)

\displaystyle=-i\hbar x\frac{d\psi}{dx}+i\hbar\psi+i\hbar x\frac{d\psi}{dx}

(5.38)

\displaystyle=i\hbar\psi

(5.39)

Therefore:
[\hat{x},\hat{p}]=i\hbar
.

This can be verified on the lattice for large
n
:

[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}]_{\text{lattice}}\approx i\hbar\delta_{jk}+O(a)

(5.40)

Verification on the lattice:
On the lattice, position acts as multiplication by
x_{j}=ja
, while momentum implements a finite difference:

\displaystyle(\hat{x}\psi)_{j}

\displaystyle=x_{j}\psi_{j}

(5.41)

\displaystyle(\hat{p}\psi)_{j}

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\frac{\psi_{j+1}-\psi_{j-1}}{2a}

(5.42)

Computing the commutator:

\displaystyle[\hat{x},\hat{p}]\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=x_{j}\cdot\left(-i\hbar\frac{\psi_{j+1}-\psi_{j-1}}{2a}\right)-\hat{p}(x_{j}\psi_{j})

(5.43)

For the second term, noting that
x_{j\pm 1}=x_{j}\pm a
:

\displaystyle\hat{p}(x\psi)_{j}

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\frac{x_{j+1}\psi_{j+1}-x_{j-1}\psi_{j-1}}{2a}

(5.44)

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\left[\frac{x_{j}(\psi_{j+1}-\psi_{j-1})}{2a}+\frac{\psi_{j+1}+\psi_{j-1}}{2}\right]

(5.45)

Therefore:

\displaystyle[\hat{x},\hat{p}]\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=i\hbar\frac{\psi_{j+1}+\psi_{j-1}}{2}

(5.46)

Using Taylor expansion
\psi_{j\pm 1}=\psi_{j}+O(a)
, we get
[\hat{x},\hat{p}]=i\hbar\cdot\mathbb{I}+O(a^{2})
on the lattice, confirming that the canonical commutation relation emerges exactly in the continuum limit.

5.2.9  The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff Formula

The commutation relation
[\hat{x},\hat{p}]=i\hbar
has profound consequences that extend far beyond the uncertainty principle. Before proceeding to multiple representations and specific examples, we introduce a powerful mathematical tool that will be essential throughout our study of continuous quantum systems.

Physical Intuition: Commutators as Derivatives

The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula describes how operators transform under conjugation by exponentials. This is deeply connected to time evolution in quantum mechanics.

Consider the Heisenberg picture, where operators evolve in time while states remain constant. The key equation is:

\frac{d\hat{B}}{dt}=\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\hat{B}]

(5.47)

The commutator
[\hat{H},\cdot]
acts like a derivative operator in the space of operators!

Formal Solution: From Schrödinger to Heisenberg Picture

We know from the Schrödinger picture that states evolve as:

|\psi(t)\rangle=e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}|\psi(0)\rangle

(5.48)

Expectation values must be independent of which picture we use. In the Schrödinger picture:

\langle\hat{B}\rangle=\langle\psi(t)|\hat{B}|\psi(t)\rangle=\langle\psi(0)|e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{B}e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}|\psi(0)\rangle

(5.49)

This suggests defining a time-evolved operator in the Heisenberg picture:

\boxed{\hat{B}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{B}(0)e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}}

(5.50)

This is the formal solution to the operator equation of motion.

Verification:
Let us verify that this exponential form satisfies
\frac{d\hat{B}}{dt}=\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\hat{B}]
.

Taking the time derivative using the product rule:

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{B}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{B}(0)e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hat{H}}{\hbar}e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{B}(0)e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}+e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{B}(0)\left(-\frac{i\hat{H}}{\hbar}\right)e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{H}\hat{B}(t)-\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{B}(t)\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\hat{B}(t)]

(5.51)

The exponential form indeed satisfies the operator equation of motion. This formal solution is the foundation of the BCH formula.

Taylor Series Expansion with Nested Commutators

We can expand the formal solution as a Taylor series. Just as for ordinary functions:

f(t)=f(0)+tf^{\prime}(0)+\frac{t^{2}}{2!}f^{\prime\prime}(0)+\frac{t^{3}}{3!}f^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)+\cdots

(5.52)

we have for operators:

\hat{B}(t)=\hat{B}(0)+t\frac{d\hat{B}}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}+\frac{t^{2}}{2!}\frac{d^{2}\hat{B}}{dt^{2}}\bigg|_{t=0}+\frac{t^{3}}{3!}\frac{d^{3}\hat{B}}{dt^{3}}\bigg|_{t=0}+\cdots

(5.53)

Now substitute the expressions for the time derivatives:

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{B}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\hat{B}]

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\hat{B}}{dt^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\frac{d\hat{B}}{dt}]=\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\right)^{2}[\hat{H},[\hat{H},\hat{B}]]

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}\hat{B}}{dt^{3}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\right)^{3}[\hat{H},[\hat{H},[\hat{H},\hat{B}]]]

(5.54)

This gives:

\hat{B}(t)=\hat{B}+\frac{it}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\hat{B}]+\frac{(it/\hbar)^{2}}{2!}[\hat{H},[\hat{H},\hat{B}]]+\frac{(it/\hbar)^{3}}{3!}[\hat{H},[\hat{H},[\hat{H},\hat{B}]]]+\cdots

(5.55)

Connection to the General BCH Formula

Setting
\hat{A}=i\hat{H}t/\hbar
, the series becomes:

\hat{B}(t)=\hat{B}+[\hat{A},\hat{B}]+\frac{1}{2!}[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]+\frac{1}{3!}[\hat{A},[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]]+\cdots

(5.56)

But we also showed that
\hat{B}(t)=e^{\hat{A}}\hat{B}e^{-\hat{A}}
. Therefore, we have discovered:

e^{\hat{A}}\hat{B}e^{-\hat{A}}=\hat{B}+[\hat{A},\hat{B}]+\frac{1}{2!}[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]+\frac{1}{3!}[\hat{A},[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]]+\cdots

(5.57)

This is the BCH conjugation formula! The operator
e^{\hat{A}}
"flows"
\hat{B}
along the direction generated by repeated commutators with
\hat{A}
. Each term involves one more nested commutator, exactly as each term in a Taylor series involves one more derivative.

The General Conjugation Formula

More generally, for any operators
\hat{A}
and
\hat{B}
:

\boxed{e^{\hat{A}}\hat{B}e^{-\hat{A}}=\hat{B}+[\hat{A},\hat{B}]+\frac{1}{2!}[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]+\frac{1}{3!}[\hat{A},[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]]+\cdots}

(5.58)

Think of this as: the operator
e^{\hat{A}}
"flows"
\hat{B}
along the direction generated by repeated commutators with
\hat{A}
. Each term involves one more nested commutator, just as each term in a Taylor series involves one more derivative. The commutator
[\hat{A},\cdot]
is the "derivative" in operator space.

Example 9: BCH for Position Under Momentum Translation

Consider translating position by momentum:
\hat{A}=-i\epsilon\hat{p}/\hbar
,
\hat{B}=\hat{x}
.

The first commutator:

[\hat{A},\hat{B}]=[-i\epsilon\hat{p}/\hbar,\hat{x}]=-i\epsilon[\hat{p},\hat{x}]/\hbar=-i\epsilon(-i\hbar)/\hbar=-\epsilon

(5.59)

This is a c-number! Therefore all higher commutators vanish:

[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]=[\hat{A},-\epsilon]=0

(5.60)

The BCH series terminates:

e^{-i\epsilon\hat{p}/\hbar}\hat{x}e^{i\epsilon\hat{p}/\hbar}=\hat{x}-\epsilon

(5.61)

Momentum translation shifts position by
\epsilon
, exactly as expected classically!

The Key Simplification: C-Number Commutators

When
[\hat{A},\hat{B}]
is a c-number (a multiple of the identity), all higher commutators automatically vanish:

[\hat{A},[\hat{A},\hat{B}]]=[\hat{A},c\hat{I}]=c[\hat{A},\hat{I}]=0

(5.62)

The infinite series becomes just two terms:

\boxed{e^{\hat{A}}\hat{B}e^{-\hat{A}}=\hat{B}+[\hat{A},\hat{B}]}\quad\text{(when $[\hat{A},\hat{B}]$ is a c-number)}

(5.63)

This dramatic simplification is why position and momentum operators have such simple transformation properties under displacement: their commutator
[\hat{x},\hat{p}]=i\hbar
is a c-number. We will exploit this repeatedly when constructing coherent states.

The Exponential Product Formula

A related BCH result concerns products of exponentials. For two operators
\hat{A}
and
\hat{B}
that don’t commute:

e^{\hat{A}+\hat{B}}\neq e^{\hat{A}}e^{\hat{B}}

(5.64)

The general BCH formula for exponential products is an infinite series involving nested commutators. However, when
[\hat{A},\hat{B}]
commutes with both
\hat{A}
and
\hat{B}
(automatically satisfied if
[\hat{A},\hat{B}]
is a c-number), the formula simplifies to:

\boxed{e^{\hat{A}+\hat{B}}=e^{\hat{A}}e^{\hat{B}}e^{-[\hat{A},\hat{B}]/2}}

(5.65)

This form is particularly useful when constructing coherent states of the harmonic oscillator.

Example 10: BCH for Harmonic Oscillator Displacement Operator

For the harmonic oscillator displacement operator
\hat{D}(\alpha)=e^{\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\alpha^{*}\hat{a}}
, we have
\hat{A}=\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{B}=-\alpha^{*}\hat{a}
.

The commutator is:

[\hat{A},\hat{B}]=[\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger},-\alpha^{*}\hat{a}]=-\alpha\alpha^{*}[\hat{a}^{\dagger},\hat{a}]=|\alpha|^{2}

(5.66)

Since this is a c-number, we can apply the exponential product formula:

e^{\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\alpha^{*}\hat{a}}=e^{\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger}}e^{-\alpha^{*}\hat{a}}e^{-|\alpha|^{2}/2}=e^{-|\alpha|^{2}/2}e^{\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger}}e^{-\alpha^{*}\hat{a}}

(5.67)

This factorization is essential for constructing and analyzing coherent states.

Summary: Why BCH Matters

The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula is not just a mathematical curiosity—it reveals the fundamental algebraic structure of quantum mechanics:

•

Commutators are derivatives:
The commutator
[\hat{A},\cdot]
acts like a directional derivative in operator space

•

Exponentials generate flows:

e^{\hat{A}}
generates a "flow" along the direction defined by
\hat{A}

•

C-numbers simplify everything:
When commutators yield c-numbers, infinite series truncate to simple expressions

•

Classical-quantum connection:
BCH shows how quantum operators reduce to classical transformations in appropriate limits

We will use BCH extensively in constructing coherent states, analyzing displacement operators, and understanding squeezed states. The formula provides both computational power and conceptual insight into the structure of quantum theory.

5.2.10  Connections Between Discrete and Continuous

Finite Differences and Differential Operators

The continuum limit can be reversed to recover discrete approximations:

Example 11: Finite Difference Approximations

Second derivative:

\frac{d^{2}\psi}{dx^{2}}\approx\frac{\psi(x+a)-2\psi(x)+\psi(x-a)}{a^{2}}+O(a^{2})

(5.68)

First derivative (symmetric):

\frac{d\psi}{dx}\approx\frac{\psi(x+a)-\psi(x-a)}{2a}+O(a^{2})

(5.69)

The discrete Schrödinger equation on a lattice:

-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2ma^{2}}(\psi_{j+1}-2\psi_{j}+\psi_{j-1})+V_{j}\psi_{j}=E\psi_{j}

(5.70)

This can be written as a matrix eigenvalue problem:

\begin{pmatrix}V_{1}+2t&-t&0&\cdots\\
-t&V_{2}+2t&-t&\cdots\\
0&-t&V_{3}+2t&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\\
\psi_{3}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}=E\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\\
\psi_{3}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}

(5.71)

where
t=\hbar^{2}/(2ma^{2})
.

The Deep Connection

The discrete-continuous relationship:

Physical Insight

The discrete and continuous formulations represent complementary perspectives, neither more fundamental than the other. Each approach has its natural domain of application. Some quantum gravity theories propose discrete spacetime at the Planck scale, while others maintain continuity down to arbitrarily small distances. Condensed matter systems naturally exhibit both discrete descriptions at the atomic level and continuous effective field theories at larger scales. Mathematical physics moves fluidly between discrete algebraic methods and continuous analytic techniques, choosing the approach best suited to each problem.

The practical implications are profound. Lattice models can capture essential physics with finite computational resources, making them ideal for numerical simulations. Continuous formulations provide analytical insights and exact solutions that reveal general principles. Some phenomena like band structure and Wannier states in crystals are naturally discrete, while others like scattering theory and field propagation are naturally continuous. Modern quantum simulation exploits this duality, using discrete quantum systems to model both discrete and continuous physics.

The conceptual unity between these pictures is striking. Wave functions and discrete amplitudes transform into each other via the continuum limit or discretization. Differential operators and difference operators are mutually derivable through systematic expansion procedures. Boundary conditions in the continuum correspond to edge effects on the lattice. Analytical solutions complement numerical methods, each illuminating aspects the other cannot easily capture. This deep connection between discrete and continuous descriptions lies at the heart of modern quantum theory.

5.3  Examples of Continuous Hilbert Spaces

5.3.1  Multiple Representations

The Power of Multiple Perspectives

Quantum systems can be described in multiple representations. Position and momentum space, related by the Fourier transform, are the most fundamental. Problems intractable in one representation often become simple in the other.

Position and Momentum Space

Different representations offer complementary insights:

Position vs Momentum Representation

The relationship between representations is given by the Fourier transform:

Master Reference: Wave Function Transformations

Fundamental Transformations:

Position to Momentum:

\tilde{\psi}(p)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,e^{-ipx/\hbar}\psi(x)

(5.72)

Momentum to Position:

\psi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\,e^{ipx/\hbar}\tilde{\psi}(p)

(5.73)
