\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{B}=\mu_{0}\vec{J}+\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}\frac{\partial\vec{E}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{B}=\frac{4\pi}{c}\vec{J}+\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\vec{E}}{\partial t}

Table 8.4
:
Maxwell’s equations in Heaviside-Lorentz and atomic units.

Equation

Heaviside-Lorentz

Atomic

Gauss (E)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{E}=\rho

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{E}=4\pi\rho

Gauss (B)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{B}=0

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{B}=0

Faraday

\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{E}=-\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\vec{B}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{E}=-\alpha\frac{\partial\vec{B}}{\partial t}

Ampère-Maxwell

\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{B}=\frac{1}{c}\vec{J}+\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\vec{E}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle\nabla\times\vec{B}=4\pi\alpha\vec{J}+\alpha\frac{\partial\vec{E}}{\partial t}

Heaviside-Lorentz has the cleanest form: no
4\pi
factors in the differential equations. Atomic units are awkward for electrodynamics because
\alpha\approx 1/137
appears where
1/c
would be in Gaussian units—atomic units are optimized for non-relativistic quantum mechanics, where
c
doesn’t naturally appear.

8.2.9  Converting Between Systems

The key conversion from SI to Gaussian is:

\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\quad\text{(SI)}\longleftrightarrow\quad e^{2}\quad\text{(Gaussian)}

(8.13)

To convert any formula, replace every
e^{2}/(4\pi\epsilon_{0})
with
e^{2}
.

To convert from Gaussian to atomic units, set
\hbar=m_{e}=e=1
. Lengths become pure numbers (measured in units of
a_{0}
); energies become pure numbers (measured in units of
E_{h}
).

The fine structure constant
\alpha
is dimensionless and therefore identical in all unit systems:

\alpha=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}\hbar c}=\frac{e^{2}}{\hbar c}=\frac{1}{c}\approx\frac{1}{137}

(8.14)

8.2.10  Which System to Use?

SI units
: Engineering, experimental physics, undergraduate teaching, numerical comparisons with experiment.

Gaussian units
: Classic texts (Jackson, Landau), plasma physics, astrophysics, condensed matter theory, avoiding
4\pi\epsilon_{0}
clutter.

Heaviside-Lorentz units
: Quantum field theory, particle physics, when you want clean Maxwell equations.

Atomic units
: Atomic/molecular/optical physics, quantum chemistry, whenever you want the simplest Schrödinger equation.

Key Idea: The Fine Structure Constant

The fine structure constant
\alpha\approx 1/137
is the one truly dimensionless number characterizing electromagnetism. It measures the strength of the electromagnetic interaction:
\alpha=e^{2}/(\hbar c)
in Gaussian units. Its smallness (
\alpha\ll 1
) is why perturbation theory works for atomic physics and why atoms exist at all. In atomic units,
\alpha=1/c
, making the non-relativistic nature of atomic physics manifest.

For this chapter and most atomic physics applications, we use
atomic units
:
\hbar=m_{e}=e=1
. Lengths are measured in Bohr radii (
a_{0}=1
), energies in Hartrees (
E_{h}=1
), and the Coulomb potential is simply
V(r)=-1/r
.

8.3  The Schrödinger Equation in Spherical Coordinates

For a particle of mass
\mu
(in units of
m_{e}
) in a central potential
V(r)
, the time-independent Schrödinger equation is:

-\frac{1}{2\mu}\nabla^{2}\psi+V(r)\psi=E\psi

(8.15)

For hydrogen with an infinitely heavy nucleus,
\mu=1
. In spherical coordinates
(r,\theta,\phi)
, the Laplacian takes the form:

\nabla^{2}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\sin\theta\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\phi^{2}}

(8.16)

8.3.1  Separation of Variables

We seek solutions of the form:

\psi(r,\theta,\phi)=R(r)Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\phi)

(8.17)

where
Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\phi)
are the spherical harmonics we studied in Chapter
6
.

8.4  The Radial Equation

After separation, the radial equation becomes:

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{dR}{dr}\right)+\left[2\mu(E-V(r))-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}\right]R=0

(8.18)

For the Coulomb potential
V(r)=-1/r
, this becomes the hydrogen radial equation.

8.4.1  The Effective Potential

Physical Insight

The term
\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2\mu r^{2}}
acts as a centrifugal barrier, preventing the electron from approaching the nucleus too closely for
\ell>0
. This is a purely quantum mechanical effect arising from the angular momentum of the electron.

8.5  Energy Levels and Wave Functions

8.5.1  The Hydrogen Hamiltonian

In atomic units, the hydrogen Hamiltonian takes its simplest possible form:

\hat{H}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-\frac{1}{r}

(8.19)

This elegant expression contains no fundamental constants at all—just the kinetic energy operator and the Coulomb attraction.

8.5.2  Energy Eigenvalues

The energy eigenvalues are:

E_{n}=-\frac{1}{2n^{2}}

(8.20)

where
n=1,2,3,\ldots
is the principal quantum number. These energies are measured in Hartrees.

In terms of Rydbergs (1 Ry
=\frac{1}{2}E_{h}=13.6
eV):

E_{n}=-\frac{\text{Ry}}{n^{2}}

(8.21)

The ground state energy is exactly
E_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}E_{h}=-1
Ry
=-13.6
eV.

8.5.3  Radial Wave Functions

The normalized radial wave functions are:

R_{n\ell}(r)=\sqrt{\left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^{3}\frac{(n-\ell-1)!}{2n(n+\ell)!}}e^{-r/n}\left(\frac{2r}{n}\right)^{\ell}L_{n-\ell-1}^{2\ell+1}\left(\frac{2r}{n}\right)

(8.22)

where
r
is measured in Bohr radii and
L_{k}^{p}(x)
are the associated Laguerre polynomials.

Explicit forms:

Ground state (
1s
):

R_{10}(r)=2e^{-r}

(8.23)

2s
state:

R_{20}(r)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left(1-\frac{r}{2}\right)e^{-r/2}

(8.24)

2p
state:

R_{21}(r)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}re^{-r/2}

(8.25)

3s
state:

R_{30}(r)=\frac{2}{81\sqrt{3}}\left(27-18r+2r^{2}\right)e^{-r/3}

(8.26)

8.6  Quantum Numbers and Degeneracy

Key Idea: Quantum Numbers of Hydrogen

The hydrogen atom wave functions are characterized by three quantum numbers: the principal quantum number
n=1,2,3,\ldots
(which determines energy), the orbital angular momentum
\ell=0,1,2,\ldots,n-1
, and the magnetic quantum number
m=-\ell,-\ell+1,\ldots,\ell-1,\ell
. The degeneracy of energy level
n
is
n^{2}
.

The total degeneracy is:

g_{n}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}(2\ell+1)=n^{2}

(8.27)

Including electron spin:
g_{n}=2n^{2}
.

8.7  Probability Densities and Orbitals

8.7.1  Radial Probability Distribution

The probability of finding the electron between
r
and
r+dr
is:

P(r)dr=|R_{n\ell}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr

(8.28)

For the ground state:

P_{1s}(r)=4r^{2}e^{-2r}

(8.29)

The most probable radius (where
dP/dr=0
) is
r^{*}=1
(one Bohr radius).

The expectation value is
\langle r\rangle=3/2
(in units of
a_{0}
).

8.7.2  Angular Distributions and Orbital Shapes

The angular part determines the shape of orbitals. The
s
orbitals (
\ell=0
) are spherically symmetric,
p
orbitals (
\ell=1
) are dumbbell shaped, and
d
orbitals (
\ell=2
) have more complex four-lobed shapes.

8.8  The Effective Potential

The effective potential in atomic units is:

V_{\text{eff}}(r)=-\frac{1}{r}+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2r^{2}}

(8.30)

For
\ell>0
, this has a minimum at:

r_{\min}=\ell(\ell+1)

(8.31)

with value:

V_{\text{eff}}(r_{\min})=-\frac{1}{2\ell(\ell+1)}

(8.32)

8.9  Hydrogen-like Atoms

For ions with nuclear charge
Z
(He
+
, Li
2+
, etc.), the potential becomes
V(r)=-Z/r
. The energy levels and wave functions scale simply:

\displaystyle E_{n}^{(Z)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{Z^{2}}{2n^{2}}

(8.33)

\displaystyle R_{n\ell}^{(Z)}(r)

\displaystyle=Z^{3/2}R_{n\ell}(Zr)

(8.34)

The effective Bohr radius shrinks:
a_{0}^{(Z)}=1/Z
.

Examples:

Ion

Z

E_{1}
(in
E_{h}
)

E_{1}
(in eV)

H

1

-1/2

-13.6

He
+

2

-2

-54.4

Li
2+

3

-9/2

-122.4

8.10  Beyond the Bohr Model: Outlook

The energy levels we have derived represent the leading-order solution to the hydrogen atom. Several small corrections modify these energies.

In atomic units, the speed of light is
c=1/\alpha\approx 137
. This large value reflects the fact that typical atomic velocities (
v\sim 1
in atomic units) are much smaller than
c
, justifying our non-relativistic treatment. Relativistic corrections scale as
(v/c)^{2}\sim\alpha^{2}\sim 10^{-4}
.

Fine structure
(
\sim\alpha^{2}E_{n}
): relativistic kinetic energy, spin-orbit coupling, Darwin term.

Hyperfine structure
(
\sim(m_{e}/m_{p})\alpha^{2}E_{n}
): electron-nuclear spin interaction.

Lamb shift
(
\sim\alpha^{3}\ln\alpha\cdot E_{n}
): quantum electrodynamic vacuum fluctuations.

These corrections will be treated systematically using perturbation theory in Chapter
9
.

8.11  Chapter Summary

In atomic units, the hydrogen atom achieves maximal simplicity:

Quantity

Atomic Units

Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\hat{H}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-\frac{1}{r}

Energy levels

\displaystyle E_{n}=-\frac{1}{2n^{2}}

Ground state energy

E_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}E_{h}=-1
Ry

Ground state wave function

\psi_{100}=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-r}

Most probable radius

r^{*}=1\,a_{0}

Mean radius (ground state)

\langle r\rangle=\frac{3}{2}\,a_{0}

Degeneracy

g_{n}=n^{2}

Hydrogen-like (
Z
)

E_{n}=-Z^{2}/(2n^{2})

The absence of fundamental constants focuses attention on the physics: the competition between kinetic energy (which favors spreading out) and potential energy (which favors localization), resolved by the uncertainty principle into discrete bound states.

In the next chapter, we develop perturbation theory to handle atoms and systems that cannot be solved exactly like hydrogen.

8.12  Problems

Note:
Throughout these problems, we use atomic units (
\hbar=m_{e}=e=1
) unless otherwise specified.

Problem 8.1

(The Schrödinger Equation in Spherical Coordinates)

The time-independent Schrödinger equation for a particle of mass
\mu
in a central potential
V(r)
is (in atomic units):

-\frac{1}{2\mu}\nabla^{2}\psi+V(r)\psi=E\psi

(a)

Starting from the Laplacian in spherical coordinates, show that the angular part can be written as
-\hat{L}^{2}/r^{2}
.

(b)

By substituting
\psi(r,\theta,\phi)=R(r)Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\phi)
into the Schrödinger equation, derive the radial equation:

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{dR}{dr}\right)+\left[2\mu(E-V(r))-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}\right]R=0

(c)

Define
u(r)=rR(r)
and show that the radial equation becomes:

-\frac{1}{2\mu}\frac{d^{2}u}{dr^{2}}+\left[V(r)+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2\mu r^{2}}\right]u=Eu

Problem 8.2

(Atomic Units and the Hydrogen Atom)

In atomic units, the hydrogen atom Hamiltonian is
\hat{H}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-\frac{1}{r}
, and the energy eigenvalues are
E_{n}=-1/(2n^{2})
.

(a)

The Bohr radius is
a_{0}=\hbar^{2}/(m_{e}e^{2})
in Gaussian units. Verify this has dimensions of length and calculate its numerical value.

(b)

The Hartree is
E_{h}=e^{2}/a_{0}
. Express this in SI units and calculate its numerical value in eV.

(c)

Express the hydrogen ground state energy
E_{1}=-1/2
in: (i) Hartrees, (ii) Rydbergs, (iii) electron volts.

(d)

For hydrogen-like ions with nuclear charge
Z
, show that
E_{n}^{(Z)}=-Z^{2}/(2n^{2})
. Calculate the ionization energy of He
+
in eV.

Problem 8.3

(Quantum Numbers and Degeneracy)

The hydrogen atom wave functions are characterized by three quantum numbers:
n
,
\ell
, and
m
.

(a)

For a given principal quantum number
n
, list all allowed values of
\ell
and explain why these are the only allowed values.

(b)

For a given
\ell
, list all allowed values of
m
and explain the physical origin of this constraint.

(c)

Calculate the total degeneracy
g_{n}=n^{2}
by counting all allowed combinations of
\ell
and
m
for a given
n
.

(d)

Including electron spin (
s=1/2
), what is the total degeneracy of energy level
E_{n}
? For
n=3
, enumerate all states using the notation
|n,\ell,m_{\ell},m_{s}\rangle
.

Problem 8.4

(Radial Wave Functions and Probability Distributions)

In atomic units, the normalized radial wave functions for hydrogen are:

R_{n\ell}(r)=\sqrt{\left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^{3}\frac{(n-\ell-1)!}{2n(n+\ell)!}}e^{-r/n}\left(\frac{2r}{n}\right)^{\ell}L_{n-\ell-1}^{2\ell+1}\left(\frac{2r}{n}\right)

(a)

Write out the explicit form of
R_{10}(r)
for the ground state (
n=1
,
\ell=0
).

(b)

Write out the explicit form of
R_{21}(r)
for the
2p
state.

(c)

The radial probability density is
P(r)=|R_{n\ell}(r)|^{2}r^{2}
. For the ground state, find the most probable radius
r^{*}
by solving
dP/dr=0
.

(d)

Calculate the expectation value
\langle r\rangle
for the ground state and compare it to
r^{*}
. Why are these different?

Problem 8.5

(The Effective Potential and Classical Turning Points)

In atomic units, the effective potential for the hydrogen radial equation is:

V_{\text{eff}}(r)=-\frac{1}{r}+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2r^{2}}

(a)

Sketch
V_{\text{eff}}(r)
for
\ell=0,1,2
on the same axes. Describe how the centrifugal barrier affects the behavior near
r=0
.

(b)

For
\ell>0
, find the radius
r_{\min}
where
V_{\text{eff}}
has its minimum.

(c)

Calculate
V_{\text{eff}}(r_{\min})
and show that it equals
-1/[2\ell(\ell+1)]
.

(d)

For the
2p
state (
n=2
,
\ell=1
), find the inner and outer classical turning points where
E_{2}=V_{\text{eff}}(r)
.

Problem 8.6

(Angular Momentum and Orbital Shapes)

The angular part of the hydrogen wave function is given by the spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\phi)
.

(a)

The
s
orbitals (
\ell=0
) have
Y_{0}^{0}=1/\sqrt{4\pi}
. Explain why
s
orbitals are spherically symmetric.

(b)

The
p
orbitals (
\ell=1
) have complex spherical harmonics
Y_{1}^{0}\propto\cos\theta
and
Y_{1}^{\pm 1}\propto\sin\theta e^{\pm i\phi}
. The real combinations are
p_{z}\propto\cos\theta
,
p_{x}\propto\sin\theta\cos\phi
,
p_{y}\propto\sin\theta\sin\phi
. Show that
p_{x}
and
p_{y}
are linear combinations of
Y_{1}^{+1}
and
Y_{1}^{-1}
.

(c)

Calculate
\langle\hat{L}_{z}\rangle
for the state
p_{x}
. Is
p_{x}
an eigenstate of
\hat{L}_{z}
?

(d)

For a
p_{z}
orbital, the probability density is
|\psi|^{2}\propto|R_{n1}(r)|^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
. In which directions is the probability maximum? Where is it zero? Describe the orbital shape.

Problem 8.7

(Hydrogen Expectation Values and Virial Theorem)

(a)

For the hydrogen ground state
|1s\rangle
, calculate
\langle r\rangle
,
\langle r^{2}\rangle
, and
\langle 1/r\rangle
in atomic units. Use the radial wave function
R_{10}(r)=2e^{-r}
.

(b)

The virial theorem for a power-law potential
V\propto r^{k}
states:
2\langle T\rangle=k\langle V\rangle
. For the Coulomb potential (
k=-1
), show that
\langle T\rangle=-E
and
\langle V\rangle=2E
.

(c)

Verify the virial theorem numerically for the
n=2
state using
E_{2}=-1/8
.

(d)

What is the ratio
\langle 1/r\rangle/\langle r\rangle^{-1}
for the ground state? Interpret this result.

Problem 8.8

(Relativistic Corrections and the Darwin Term)

The relativistic correction to the kinetic energy is
T_{\text{rel}}=-p^{4}/(8m^{3}c^{2})
in leading order.

(a)

In atomic units with
c\approx 137
, the relativistic correction to energy is
E_{\text{rel}}=-\langle p^{4}\rangle/(8c^{2})
. For the ground state, use
\langle p^{2}\rangle=1
to estimate
\langle p^{4}\rangle
by assuming
\langle p^{4}\rangle\sim\langle p^{2}\rangle^{2}
.

(b)

The exact result is
E_{\text{rel}}=-\alpha^{2}E_{n}/(2n^{3})[n/\ell+1/2)^{-1}-3/4]
where
\alpha=1/c
. For
n=1
,
\ell=0
, calculate the fractional shift in energy.

(c)

The Darwin term (QED effect) contributes
\Delta E_{\text{Darwin}}=\alpha^{2}\pi\delta^{3}(\vec{r})/2
for
s
states. Using
\psi_{1s}(0)=1/\sqrt{\pi}
, calculate this contribution.

(d)

The total fine structure splitting between
2S_{1/2}
and
2P_{1/2}
(historically the Lamb shift) is about 1058 MHz. Convert to eV and compare to the splitting predicted by Dirac theory alone.

Problem 8.9

(Helium Ground State and Pauli Exclusion)

Two electrons in helium interact via Coulomb repulsion. In the simplest approximation, each electron is in a hydrogen-like orbital with effective nuclear charge
Z_{\text{eff}}
.

(a)

The ground state has both electrons in the
1s
orbital with opposite spins:
\psi(1,2)=|1s\rangle_{1}|1s\rangle_{2}\times\text{singlet spin}
. The total energy is
E_{\text{tot}}=2E_{1}(Z_{\text{eff}})+\langle V_{ee}\rangle
where
V_{ee}=1/r_{12}
is the electron-electron repulsion.

(b)

Using the variational method with trial wave function
\psi_{1s}(r)=(Z^{3}/\pi)^{1/2}e^{-Zr}
for both electrons, show that the total energy is
E(Z)=-2Z^{2}+(5/8)Z
(in atomic units).

(c)

Minimize
E(Z)
to find the optimal
Z_{\text{eff}}
. What is the resulting ground state energy and how does it compare to the experimental value of
-5.81
eV?

(d)

Explain why
Z_{\text{eff}}<2
. What is the physical origin of the screening effect?

Problem 8.10

(Spectroscopic Transitions and Selection Rules)

Selection rules for electric dipole transitions are
\Delta\ell=\pm 1
,
\Delta m=0,\pm 1
(from
z
and
x\pm iy
components of the dipole operator).

(a)

Using the selection rules, list all allowed transitions from the
n=3
level of hydrogen to the
n=2
level. Identify the emitted photon wavelengths using
E=-1/(2n^{2})
.

(b)

Why is the
2s\to 1s
transition forbidden? Why is the
2p\to 1s
transition allowed?

(c)

The transition
1s\to 2p
requires absorption of a photon with energy
\Delta E=E_{2}-E_{1}=3/8
(in atomic units). Calculate the wavelength in nm and identify which spectral line this is (Lyman-alpha).

(d)

For hydrogen-like atoms with nuclear charge
Z
, the transition frequency scales as
\omega\propto Z^{2}
. Estimate the wavelength of the Lyman-alpha line for He
+
.

Problem 8.11

(Fine Structure and Lamb Shift)

The fine structure arises from spin-orbit coupling and relativistic corrections. The Lamb shift is a QED effect that splits the
2S_{1/2}
and
2P_{1/2}
levels.

(a)

The spin-orbit energy is
E_{\text{SO}}=\alpha^{2}/(2n^{3})\times f(j,\ell,s)
where
f
depends on quantum numbers. For the
2P
level (
\ell=1
,
s=1/2
), the two levels are
2P_{1/2}
and
2P_{3/2}
. Calculate the spin-orbit splitting.

(b)

The relativistic mass correction is
\Delta E_{\text{rel}}=-\alpha^{2}/(2n^{3})\times[3/4-n/(j+1/2)]
. Calculate this for
2S_{1/2}
and
2P_{1/2}
.

(c)

The Lamb shift (QED) adds about
+1.6
to the
2S_{1/2}
energy (in units of
10^{-3}
eV relative to
n=2
) but not to
2P_{1/2}
. Estimate the total
2S_{1/2}-2P_{1/2}
splitting and compare to the measured
1058
MHz.

Problem 8.12

(Finite Nuclear Size Effects)

Real nuclei are not point charges. The nuclear charge distribution affects the
1s
orbital significantly.

(a)

For a uniformly charged sphere of radius
R
(nuclear radius), the potential is
V(r)=-Z/r
for
r>R
and
V(r)=-Z[3/(2R)-r^{2}/(2R^{3})]
for
r<R
.

(b)

For hydrogen,
R\sim 10^{-5}
in atomic units. The shift in
1s
energy due to finite size is
\Delta E_{1s}\sim(Z\alpha)^{2}(R/a_{0})^{2}|E_{1}|
. Estimate this numerically.

(c)

The shift in the muonic hydrogen
1s
level (muon mass 207
m_{e}
) is about 200 times larger. Explain why.

(d)

How could atomic spectroscopy of muonic atoms be used to measure the nuclear radius?

, 3rd ed. Cambridge University Press, 2018. Chapter 4 is the standard undergraduate-level derivation; useful as a slower walk through the radial equation if needed.

“On the constitution of atoms and molecules.”
Philosophical Magazine

26
, 1–25 (1913).
doi:10.1080/14786441308634955
. The original quantization argument that produced
E_{n}=-1/(2n^{2})
E
h
.

“Über das Wasserstoffspektrum vom Standpunkt der neuen Quantenmechanik.”
Zeitschrift für Physik

36
, 336–363 (1926).
doi:10.1007/BF01450175
. Pauli’s algebraic solution using the Runge–Lenz vector, derived before Schrödinger’s wave-mechanical treatment; the cleanest way to see the
n^{2}
degeneracy as an
SO(4)
symmetry.
