, Vol. 1. Wiley-VCH, 1977. Chapter VII treats the hydrogen atom in conventional units; useful for cross-checking the atomic-units formulas of this chapter against the SI versions.

Chapter 9  Perturbation Theory

9.1  Introduction

While the hydrogen atom represents a pinnacle of exact solvability in quantum mechanics, most physical systems cannot be solved analytically. Perturbation theory provides a systematic approach to finding approximate solutions when the Hamiltonian can be written as a sum of a solvable part and a small perturbation.

Key Idea: The Perturbative Approach

Perturbation theory treats a complicated problem as a simple, solvable problem plus a small correction. We expand solutions in powers of a small parameter, obtaining increasingly accurate approximations.

9.2  Time-Independent Perturbation Theory

9.2.1  Non-Degenerate Perturbation Theory

Consider a Hamiltonian of the form:

H=H_{0}+\lambda V

(9.1)

where
H_{0}
is the unperturbed Hamiltonian with known eigenstates
|n^{(0)}\rangle
and eigenvalues
E_{n}^{(0)}
, and
\lambda V
is a small perturbation.

We expand the perturbed eigenstates and energies as power series in
\lambda
:

\displaystyle|n\rangle

\displaystyle=|n^{(0)}\rangle+\lambda|n^{(1)}\rangle+\lambda^{2}|n^{(2)}\rangle+\cdots

(9.2)

\displaystyle E_{n}

\displaystyle=E_{n}^{(0)}+\lambda E_{n}^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}E_{n}^{(2)}+\cdots

(9.3)

9.2.2  Derivation of First-Order Corrections

Substituting the expansions (
9.2
) and (
9.3
) into the eigenvalue equation
(H_{0}+\lambda V)|n\rangle=E_{n}|n\rangle
:

(H_{0}+\lambda V)(|n^{(0)}\rangle+\lambda|n^{(1)}\rangle+\cdots)=(E_{n}^{(0)}+\lambda E_{n}^{(1)}+\cdots)(|n^{(0)}\rangle+\lambda|n^{(1)}\rangle+\cdots)

(9.4)

Expanding and collecting terms by powers of
\lambda
:

\displaystyle\lambda^{0}:\quad

\displaystyle H_{0}|n^{(0)}\rangle=E_{n}^{(0)}|n^{(0)}\rangle

(9.5)

\displaystyle\lambda^{1}:\quad

\displaystyle H_{0}|n^{(1)}\rangle+V|n^{(0)}\rangle=E_{n}^{(0)}|n^{(1)}\rangle+E_{n}^{(1)}|n^{(0)}\rangle

(9.6)

Equation (
9.5
) is just the unperturbed eigenvalue equation, which is satisfied by assumption.

First-order energy correction:
To find
E_{n}^{(1)}
, take the inner product of equation (
9.6
) with
\langle n^{(0)}|
:

\langle n^{(0)}|H_{0}|n^{(1)}\rangle+\langle n^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle=E_{n}^{(0)}\langle n^{(0)}|n^{(1)}\rangle+E_{n}^{(1)}\langle n^{(0)}|n^{(0)}\rangle

(9.7)

Since
H_{0}
is Hermitian,
\langle n^{(0)}|H_{0}=E_{n}^{(0)}\langle n^{(0)}|
, so the first term on the left equals
E_{n}^{(0)}\langle n^{(0)}|n^{(1)}\rangle
. This cancels the first term on the right, leaving:

\boxed{E_{n}^{(1)}=\langle n^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle}

(9.8)

First-order state correction:
To find
|n^{(1)}\rangle
, expand it in the complete basis of unperturbed states:

|n^{(1)}\rangle=\sum_{k}c_{k}^{(1)}|k^{(0)}\rangle

(9.9)

Take the inner product of equation (
9.6
) with
\langle m^{(0)}|
for
m\neq n
:

\langle m^{(0)}|H_{0}|n^{(1)}\rangle+\langle m^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle=E_{n}^{(0)}\langle m^{(0)}|n^{(1)}\rangle+E_{n}^{(1)}\underbrace{\langle m^{(0)}|n^{(0)}\rangle}_{=0}

(9.10)

Using
\langle m^{(0)}|H_{0}=E_{m}^{(0)}\langle m^{(0)}|
and
\langle m^{(0)}|n^{(1)}\rangle=c_{m}^{(1)}
:

E_{m}^{(0)}c_{m}^{(1)}+\langle m^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle=E_{n}^{(0)}c_{m}^{(1)}

(9.11)

Solving for
c_{m}^{(1)}
:

c_{m}^{(1)}=\frac{\langle m^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle}{E_{n}^{(0)}-E_{m}^{(0)}}\quad(m\neq n)

(9.12)

Therefore:

\boxed{|n^{(1)}\rangle=\sum_{m\neq n}\frac{\langle m^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle}{E_{n}^{(0)}-E_{m}^{(0)}}|m^{(0)}\rangle}

(9.13)

Physical Insight

The first-order energy shift is just the expectation value of the perturbation in the unperturbed state. States mix in proportion to their coupling strength divided by their energy separation.

Example 1

Spin-1/2 in a Tilted Magnetic Field
Consider a spin-1/2 particle in a strong magnetic field along
z
with a weak transverse component along
x
. The Hamiltonian is:

H=-\gamma B_{z}S_{z}-\gamma B_{x}S_{x}=\underbrace{-\gamma B_{z}S_{z}}_{H_{0}}+\underbrace{(-\gamma B_{x}S_{x})}_{V}

(9.14)

where
B_{x}\ll B_{z}
.

The unperturbed eigenstates are
|\uparrow\rangle
and
|\downarrow\rangle
with energies
E_{\uparrow}^{(0)}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma B_{z}
and
E_{\downarrow}^{(0)}=+\frac{1}{2}\gamma B_{z}
.

For the first-order energy correction to the spin-up state:

E_{\uparrow}^{(1)}=\langle\uparrow|(-\gamma B_{x}S_{x})|\uparrow\rangle=-\gamma B_{x}\langle\uparrow|S_{x}|\uparrow\rangle=0

(9.15)

since
S_{x}
has no diagonal matrix elements in the
S_{z}
basis. Similarly,
E_{\downarrow}^{(1)}=0
.

The first-order state correction is:

|\uparrow^{(1)}\rangle=\frac{\langle\downarrow|(-\gamma B_{x}S_{x})|\uparrow\rangle}{E_{\uparrow}^{(0)}-E_{\downarrow}^{(0)}}|\downarrow\rangle=\frac{-\gamma B_{x}(1/2)}{-\gamma B_{z}}|\downarrow\rangle=\frac{B_{x}}{2B_{z}}|\downarrow\rangle

(9.16)

The perturbed spin-up state tilts toward the
x
-direction:

|\uparrow^{\prime}\rangle\approx|\uparrow\rangle+\frac{B_{x}}{2B_{z}}|\downarrow\rangle

(9.17)

This makes physical sense: the spin aligns with the total field direction.

Example 2

Zeeman Effect on Hydrogen Ground State
Consider a hydrogen atom in its ground state (
1s
, with
\ell=0
) placed in a uniform magnetic field
\vec{B}=B\hat{z}
. The perturbation is:

V=-\vec{\mu}\cdot\vec{B}=\frac{e}{2m_{e}}(\vec{L}+2\vec{S})\cdot\vec{B}=\frac{eB}{2m_{e}}(L_{z}+2S_{z})

(9.18)

For the
1s
state,
\ell=0
so
L_{z}|1s\rangle=0
. The unperturbed ground state is two-fold degenerate due to spin:
|1s,\uparrow\rangle
and
|1s,\downarrow\rangle
.

However, these states are eigenstates of
S_{z}
, so the perturbation is already diagonal:

\displaystyle E_{1s,\uparrow}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{eB}{2m_{e}}\langle 1s,\uparrow|2S_{z}|1s,\uparrow\rangle=\frac{eB}{2m_{e}}=\mu_{B}B

(9.19)

\displaystyle E_{1s,\downarrow}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{eB}{2m_{e}}\langle 1s,\downarrow|2S_{z}|1s,\downarrow\rangle=-\mu_{B}B

(9.20)

where
\mu_{B}=\frac{e}{2m_{e}}=\frac{1}{2}
in atomic units is the Bohr magneton.

The magnetic field splits the ground state by
\Delta E=2\mu_{B}B
. For a 1 Tesla field, this is about
1.16\times 10^{-4}
eV.

Context:
Magnetic field control of atomic states is foundational to atomic clocks, MRI, and quantum sensing. Trapped ion qubits often use Zeeman sublevels as computational basis states, where precise control of this splitting enables gate operations.

9.2.3  Second-Order Corrections

The second-order energy correction involves virtual transitions:

E_{n}^{(2)}=\sum_{m\neq n}\frac{|\langle m^{(0)}|V|n^{(0)}\rangle|^{2}}{E_{n}^{(0)}-E_{m}^{(0)}}

(9.21)

Physical Insight

Second-order corrections arise from “virtual transitions” to intermediate states. The ground state always shifts down in energy (since all denominators are negative), reflecting the system’s ability to lower its energy by mixing with excited states.

Example 3

Anharmonic Oscillator: Quartic Perturbation
Consider a harmonic oscillator with a quartic perturbation:

H=\frac{p^{2}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2}+\lambda x^{4}

(9.22)

The unperturbed energies are
E_{n}^{(0)}=\omega(n+\frac{1}{2})
. For the ground state (
n=0
), we need
\langle 0|x^{4}|0\rangle
.

Using the ladder operator representation
x=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\omega}}(a+a^{\dagger})
(with
m=1
in atomic units):

x^{4}=\left(\frac{1}{2\omega}\right)^{2}(a+a^{\dagger})^{4}

(9.23)

The first-order correction requires terms with equal numbers of
a
and
a^{\dagger}
:

E_{0}^{(1)}=\lambda\langle 0|x^{4}|0\rangle=\lambda\left(\frac{1}{2\omega}\right)^{2}\langle 0|(a+a^{\dagger})^{4}|0\rangle

(9.24)

Expanding and keeping terms that return to
|0\rangle
:

\langle 0|(a+a^{\dagger})^{4}|0\rangle=\langle 0|(aa^{\dagger}aa^{\dagger}+aa^{\dagger}a^{\dagger}a+a^{\dagger}aaa^{\dagger}+\text{2 more})|0\rangle=3

(9.25)

Therefore:

E_{0}^{(1)}=\frac{3\lambda}{4\omega^{2}}

(9.26)

For the second-order correction,
x^{4}
connects
|0\rangle
to
|2\rangle
and
|4\rangle
:

\displaystyle\langle 2|x^{4}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\omega}\right)^{2}\sqrt{2}\cdot 3=\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4\omega^{2}}

(9.27)

\displaystyle\langle 4|x^{4}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\omega}\right)^{2}\sqrt{24}=\frac{\sqrt{24}}{4\omega^{2}}

(9.28)

The second-order correction is:

E_{0}^{(2)}=\frac{|\langle 2|x^{4}|0\rangle|^{2}}{E_{0}^{(0)}-E_{2}^{(0)}}+\frac{|\langle 4|x^{4}|0\rangle|^{2}}{E_{0}^{(0)}-E_{4}^{(0)}}=-\frac{21\lambda^{2}}{8\omega^{5}}

(9.29)

The negative second-order shift reflects the general principle that the ground state energy is pushed down by coupling to excited states.

Example 4

Quadratic Stark Effect in Hydrogen
Working in atomic units, for a hydrogen atom in a weak electric field
\mathcal{E}
along the
z
-axis, the perturbation is:

V=\mathcal{E}z=\mathcal{E}r\cos\theta

(9.30)

For the ground state
|1s\rangle
, which has definite parity (even under
\vec{r}\to-\vec{r}
), the first-order shift vanishes:

E_{1}^{(1)}=\mathcal{E}\langle 1s|z|1s\rangle=0

(9.31)

since
z
is odd under parity. Physically, the spherically symmetric
1s
state has no permanent electric dipole moment.

The second-order correction requires summing over all intermediate states:

E_{1}^{(2)}=\sum_{n\ell m\neq 1s}\frac{|\mathcal{E}\langle n\ell m|z|1s\rangle|^{2}}{E_{1}-E_{n}}

(9.32)

The selection rule
\Delta\ell=\pm 1
from the angular integration means only
p
-states contribute. After detailed calculation:

E_{1}^{(2)}=-\frac{9}{4}\mathcal{E}^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{H}\mathcal{E}^{2}

(9.33)

where
\alpha_{H}=9/2
is the ground state polarizability of hydrogen in atomic units.

The quadratic dependence on field strength is characteristic of induced (rather than permanent) dipole moments. The polarizability
\alpha_{H}=4.5
a.u. is quite small, but for Rydberg states with
n\gg 1
, the polarizability scales as
n^{7}
, making electric field control extremely effective.

Context:
Electric field control of atoms underlies optical tweezers, atom traps, and Rydberg atom quantum simulators. The enormous polarizability of Rydberg atoms makes Stark shifts a primary tool for control and readout in Rydberg-based quantum computing. In precision atomic clocks, understanding and compensating for Stark shifts from blackbody radiation is essential for reaching
10^{-18}
fractional accuracy.

9.3  Degenerate Perturbation Theory

When unperturbed states are degenerate, standard perturbation theory fails because the denominators
E_{n}^{(0)}-E_{m}^{(0)}
vanish for states within the degenerate subspace. We must use degenerate perturbation theory.

9.3.1  The Degenerate Subspace

For a
g
-fold degenerate level with energy
E^{(0)}
, we form the perturbation matrix:

W_{ij}=\langle\psi_{i}^{(0)}|V|\psi_{j}^{(0)}\rangle

(9.34)

within the degenerate subspace. The first-order energy corrections are the eigenvalues of this
g\times g
matrix.

9.3.2  Good Quantum Numbers

The eigenvectors of
W
give the correct zeroth-order states:

|n,\alpha\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{g}c_{i}^{(\alpha)}|\psi_{i}^{(0)}\rangle

(9.35)

where
\alpha
labels the eigenstates within the formerly degenerate subspace. These eigenvectors correspond to “good quantum numbers” that remain well-defined in the presence of the perturbation.

Key Idea: Lifting Degeneracy

Perturbations often break symmetries, lifting degeneracies. The perturbation selects a preferred basis within the degenerate subspace, namely the basis that diagonalizes the perturbation.

Example 5

Two Coupled Spins with Symmetry-Breaking Perturbation
Consider two spin-1/2 particles with exchange coupling and a perturbation that treats them asymmetrically:

H=J\vec{S}_{1}\cdot\vec{S}_{2}+\epsilon(S_{1z}-S_{2z})

(9.36)

The unperturbed Hamiltonian
H_{0}=J\vec{S}_{1}\cdot\vec{S}_{2}
has eigenstates in the coupled basis: the singlet
|0,0\rangle
with
E=-\frac{3}{4}J\hbar^{2}
and the triplet
|1,m\rangle
with
E=\frac{1}{4}J\hbar^{2}
.

The triplet is three-fold degenerate. The perturbation
V=\epsilon(S_{1z}-S_{2z})
breaks the exchange symmetry. In the triplet subspace:

\displaystyle|1,1\rangle

\displaystyle=|\uparrow\uparrow\rangle

(9.37)

\displaystyle|1,0\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle+|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle)

(9.38)

\displaystyle|1,-1\rangle

\displaystyle=|\downarrow\downarrow\rangle

(9.39)

Computing the perturbation matrix elements:

\displaystyle\langle 1,1|V|1,1\rangle

\displaystyle=\epsilon\langle\uparrow\uparrow|(S_{1z}-S_{2z})|\uparrow\uparrow\rangle=\epsilon(\tfrac{\hbar}{2}-\tfrac{\hbar}{2})=0

(9.40)

\displaystyle\langle 1,0|V|1,0\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{2}[\langle\uparrow\downarrow|+\langle\downarrow\uparrow|](S_{1z}-S_{2z})[|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle+|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle]=0

(9.41)

\displaystyle\langle 1,-1|V|1,-1\rangle

\displaystyle=0

(9.42)

All diagonal elements vanish! The off-diagonal elements:

\langle 1,1|V|1,0\rangle=\langle 1,0|V|1,-1\rangle=0

(9.43)

but

\langle\uparrow\downarrow|(S_{1z}-S_{2z})|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle=\hbar,\quad\langle\downarrow\uparrow|(S_{1z}-S_{2z})|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle=-\hbar

(9.44)

The perturbation matrix in the
\{|1,1\rangle,|1,0\rangle,|1,-1\rangle\}
basis is zero, but this is because these are already the good states. The perturbation does not mix states with different
m
, and within each
m
subspace the matrix is diagonal.

However, if we had worked in the uncoupled basis
\{|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle,|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle\}
for
m=0
:

W=\epsilon\hbar\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}

(9.45)

The eigenstates are
|\uparrow\downarrow\rangle
and
|\downarrow\uparrow\rangle
with energies
\pm\epsilon\hbar
. The perturbation breaks the triplet-singlet structure and makes the uncoupled basis the “good” basis.

Example 6

Linear Stark Effect in Hydrogen (
n=2
)
Working in atomic units, the
n=2
level of hydrogen is four-fold degenerate (ignoring spin): one
2s
state and three
2p
states. An electric field
\mathcal{E}
along
z
introduces the perturbation
V=\mathcal{E}z
.

The states are
|2,0,0\rangle
(2s),
|2,1,0\rangle
(2p
z
),
|2,1,1\rangle
(2p
+
), and
|2,1,-1\rangle
(2p
-
).

Since
[L_{z},z]=0
, the perturbation conserves
m
. The
m=\pm 1
states cannot mix with
m=0
states, so the
4\times 4
problem separates:

•

m=+1
:
\langle 2,1,1|V|2,1,1\rangle=0
(no shift)

•

m=-1
:
\langle 2,1,-1|V|2,1,-1\rangle=0
(no shift)

•

m=0
:
2\times 2
subspace of
|2s\rangle
and
|2p_{z}\rangle

For the
m=0
subspace, diagonal elements vanish by parity:

\langle 2s|z|2s\rangle=\langle 2p_{z}|z|2p_{z}\rangle=0

(9.46)

The crucial off-diagonal element:

\langle 2s|z|2p_{z}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{2}dr\int d\Omega\,R_{20}(r)Y_{0}^{0}\cdot r\cos\theta\cdot R_{21}(r)Y_{1}^{0}

(9.47)

Using explicit wave functions (in atomic units where
a_{0}=1
):

\displaystyle R_{20}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\left(2-r\right)e^{-r/2}

(9.48)

\displaystyle R_{21}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{6}}r\,e^{-r/2}

(9.49)

After integration:
\langle 2s|z|2p_{z}\rangle=-3
.

The perturbation matrix in the
\{|2s\rangle,|2p_{z}\rangle\}
basis:

W=\mathcal{E}\begin{pmatrix}0&-3\\
-3&0\end{pmatrix}

(9.50)

Eigenvalues:
E^{(1)}=\pm 3\mathcal{E}
. Eigenstates:

|\pm\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|2s\rangle\mp|2p_{z}\rangle)

(9.51)

The linear Stark effect gives energy shifts proportional to
\mathcal{E}
, not
\mathcal{E}^{2}
! This is because the degenerate
2s
and
2p
states can hybridize to form states with permanent electric dipole moments.

Context:
The linear Stark effect demonstrates how degeneracy enables qualitatively different physics. Rydberg atoms, with their near-degenerate high-
n
manifolds, exhibit enormous electric dipole moments (
\sim n^{2}
a.u.), making them exquisitely sensitive to electric fields. This sensitivity is exploited in Rydberg-atom-based electric field sensors and in creating strong dipole-dipole interactions for quantum simulation.

9.3.3  Choice of Basis: Coupled vs. Uncoupled vs. Full Diagonalization

A recurring theme in perturbation theory is the choice of basis. When multiple quantum numbers could label states, choosing the right basis can dramatically simplify calculations.

Key Idea: Choosing the Right Basis

The “right” basis for perturbation theory is one in which the perturbation is diagonal or block-diagonal. This basis corresponds to the quantum numbers that remain good (conserved) in the presence of the perturbation.

Consider a system with two angular momenta
\vec{L}
and
\vec{S}
. We can work in:

•

Uncoupled basis:

|\ell,m_{\ell},s,m_{s}\rangle
, eigenstates of
L^{2},L_{z},S^{2},S_{z}

•

Coupled basis:

|\ell,s,j,m_{j}\rangle
, eigenstates of
L^{2},S^{2},J^{2},J_{z}

The choice depends on which operators commute with the perturbation:

•

If
[V,J_{z}]=0
but
[V,L_{z}]\neq 0
: use coupled basis

•

If
[V,L_{z}]=0
and
[V,S_{z}]=0
: use uncoupled basis

•

If neither is diagonal: must diagonalize explicitly

Example 7

Hydrogen Fine Structure: Comparing Basis Choices
The fine structure Hamiltonian for hydrogen includes the spin-orbit term:

H_{SO}=\frac{1}{2m_{e}^{2}c^{2}}\frac{1}{r}\frac{dV}{dr}\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}=\xi(r)\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}

(9.52)

Attempt 1: Uncoupled basis

|n,\ell,m_{\ell},m_{s}\rangle

In this basis,
\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}=L_{x}S_{x}+L_{y}S_{y}+L_{z}S_{z}
has off-diagonal elements. For example,
L_{+}S_{-}
connects
|m_{\ell},m_{s}\rangle
to
|m_{\ell}+1,m_{s}-1\rangle
. The perturbation matrix is not diagonal, and we would need to diagonalize a
(2\ell+1)\times 2
dimensional matrix for each
\ell
.

Attempt 2: Coupled basis

|n,\ell,j,m_{j}\rangle

Using
\vec{J}=\vec{L}+\vec{S}
:

\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}=\frac{1}{2}(J^{2}-L^{2}-S^{2})

(9.53)

In the coupled basis, this is diagonal:

\langle n,\ell,j,m_{j}|\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}|n,\ell,j,m_{j}\rangle=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}[j(j+1)-\ell(\ell+1)-s(s+1)]

(9.54)

No diagonalization needed! The first-order energy shift is:

E_{SO}^{(1)}=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}[j(j+1)-\ell(\ell+1)-\tfrac{3}{4}]\langle\xi(r)\rangle_{n\ell}

(9.55)

For
\ell>0
,
j=\ell\pm\frac{1}{2}
:

\displaystyle j=\ell+\tfrac{1}{2}:

\displaystyle\quad E_{SO}^{(1)}=\frac{\ell\hbar^{2}}{2}\langle\xi\rangle

(9.56)

\displaystyle j=\ell-\tfrac{1}{2}:

\displaystyle\quad E_{SO}^{(1)}=-\frac{(\ell+1)\hbar^{2}}{2}\langle\xi\rangle

(9.57)

The coupled basis is the natural choice because
J^{2}
and
J_{z}
commute with
\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}
, making
j
and
m_{j}
good quantum numbers.

Context:
Basis choice is not just mathematical convenience; it reflects physical insight about which quantities are conserved. The same principle governs LS-coupling versus jj-coupling in multi-electron atoms, molecular orbital versus valence bond descriptions in chemistry, and product versus entangled bases in quantum information. In quantum computing, gate design requires choosing bases adapted to the available control Hamiltonians.

9.4  Time-Dependent Perturbation Theory

For time-dependent Hamiltonians
H=H_{0}+V(t)
, we need different techniques. In Chapter
4
, we developed the general framework for time-dependent evolution, including the Dyson series (Section 4.8) and the interaction picture (Section 4.9). Here we apply these tools in the perturbative regime, where the time-dependent part
V(t)
is small compared to
H_{0}
.

9.4.1  The Interaction Picture

Recall from Section 4.9 that the interaction picture provides a hybrid between the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures, transferring the “trivial” evolution under
H_{0}
to the operators while states evolve only due to the perturbation. In the interaction picture, states evolve according to:

i\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle=V_{I}(t)|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle

(9.58)

with
V_{I}(t)=e^{iH_{0}t}V(t)e^{-iH_{0}t}
.

As we showed in Chapter
4
, the formal solution involves the time-ordered exponential (Dyson series):

|\psi_{I}(t)\rangle=\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-i\int_{0}^{t}V_{I}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\right)|\psi_{I}(0)\rangle

(9.59)

Time ordering is essential when
[V_{I}(t_{1}),V_{I}(t_{2})]\neq 0
for different times, reflecting the causal structure of quantum evolution. In perturbation theory, we truncate this series at low order.

9.4.2  Transition Probabilities

To first order, the amplitude for transition from state
|i\rangle
to state
|f\rangle
is:

c_{fi}(t)=-i\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\langle f|V_{I}(t^{\prime})|i\rangle=-i\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega_{fi}t^{\prime}}\langle f|V(t^{\prime})|i\rangle

(9.60)

where
\omega_{fi}=E_{f}-E_{i}
.

Example 8

Rabi Oscillations in a Driven Two-Level System

Consider a spin-1/2 in a static field
B_{0}\hat{z}
driven by an oscillating transverse field:

H=-\gamma B_{0}S_{z}-\gamma B_{1}\cos(\omega t)S_{x}

(9.61)

This is precisely the NMR system we analyzed exactly in Section 4.9 of Chapter
4
. There, we transformed to the rotating frame and applied the rotating wave approximation to obtain the effective time-independent Hamiltonian. Here we take a complementary approach: we treat the oscillating field perturbatively, valid when
\Omega_{1}\ll|\omega_{0}-\omega|
(far from resonance) or for short times.

Let
\omega_{0}=\gamma B_{0}
(Larmor frequency) and
\Omega_{1}=\gamma B_{1}
(Rabi frequency). The unperturbed states are
|\uparrow\rangle
and
|\downarrow\rangle
with energy splitting
\omega_{0}
.

Starting in
|\downarrow\rangle
, the transition amplitude to
|\uparrow\rangle
is:

c_{\uparrow}(t)=-i\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega_{0}t^{\prime}}(-\gamma B_{1}\cos\omega t^{\prime})\langle\uparrow|S_{x}|\downarrow\rangle

(9.62)

Using
\langle\uparrow|S_{x}|\downarrow\rangle=1/2
and
\cos\omega t^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(e^{i\omega t^{\prime}}+e^{-i\omega t^{\prime}})
:

c_{\uparrow}(t)=\frac{i\Omega_{1}}{4}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\left(e^{i(\omega_{0}+\omega)t^{\prime}}+e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t^{\prime}}\right)

(9.63)

Near resonance (
\omega\approx\omega_{0}
), the first term oscillates rapidly and averages to zero (rotating wave approximation). The second term gives:

c_{\uparrow}(t)\approx\frac{i\Omega_{1}}{4}\frac{e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}-1}{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)}=\frac{\Omega_{1}}{2(\omega_{0}-\omega)}\sin\left(\frac{(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}{2}\right)e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)t/2}

(9.64)

The transition probability:

P_{\uparrow}(t)=|c_{\uparrow}(t)|^{2}=\frac{\Omega_{1}^{2}}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{(\omega_{0}-\omega)t}{2}\right)

(9.65)

At exact resonance (
\omega=\omega_{0}
), this formula breaks down, but the exact solution gives:

P_{\uparrow}(t)=\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\Omega_{1}t}{2}\right)

(9.66)

The system oscillates coherently between
|\downarrow\rangle
and
|\uparrow\rangle
at the Rabi frequency
\Omega_{1}
. Perturbation theory is valid when
\Omega_{1}\ll|\omega_{0}-\omega|
or for short times
t\ll 2\pi/\Omega_{1}
.

Compare this perturbative result with the exact rotating-frame solution from Section 4.9. The exact on-resonance result
P_{\uparrow}(t)=\sin^{2}(\Omega_{1}t/2)
agrees with what we would obtain by summing the perturbation series to all orders. The perturbative approach is particularly valuable for understanding the off-resonant response and for systems where the driving is genuinely weak.

Example 9

Electric Dipole Transitions in Hydrogen
Consider a hydrogen atom interacting with an oscillating electric field
\vec{\mathcal{E}}(t)=\mathcal{E}_{0}\hat{z}\cos(\omega t)
. The perturbation is:

V(t)=e\mathcal{E}_{0}z\cos(\omega t)

(9.67)

The transition amplitude from state
|i\rangle=|n_{i},\ell_{i},m_{i}\rangle
to
|f\rangle=|n_{f},\ell_{f},m_{f}\rangle
is:

c_{fi}(t)\propto\langle f|z|i\rangle\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega_{fi}t^{\prime}}\cos(\omega t')

(9.68)

The matrix element
\langle f|z|i\rangle
imposes
selection rules
:

z=r\cos\theta=r\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{3}}Y_{1}^{0}(\theta,\phi)

(9.69)

The angular integration:

\int d\Omega\,Y_{\ell_{f}}^{m_{f}*}Y_{1}^{0}Y_{\ell_{i}}^{m_{i}}

(9.70)

vanishes unless:

\displaystyle\Delta m

\displaystyle=m_{f}-m_{i}=0

(9.71)

\displaystyle\Delta\ell

\displaystyle=\ell_{f}-\ell_{i}=\pm 1

(9.72)

These are the
electric dipole selection rules
. Additionally, parity must change since
z
is odd.

For the Lyman-
\alpha
transition
2p\to 1s
:

\langle 1s|z|2p_{z}\rangle=\langle 1,0,0|r\cos\theta|2,1,0\rangle=\frac{2^{7}}{3^{5}}a_{0}\approx 0.74\,a_{0}

(9.73)

Context:
Selection rules determine which transitions can be driven by lasers, the foundation of laser cooling, optical pumping, and quantum state preparation. “Forbidden” transitions (electric quadrupole, magnetic dipole) have much smaller matrix elements but are used for ultra-narrow clock transitions because their slower decay rates give longer coherence times. The matrix elements directly determine oscillator strengths used throughout astrophysical spectroscopy.

9.5  Fermi’s Golden Rule

9.5.1  Transition Rates to a Continuum

When transitions occur to a continuum of final states, we compute the transition rate rather than the transition probability. Starting from time-dependent perturbation theory with a periodic perturbation
V(t)=Ve^{-i\omega t}+V^{\dagger}e^{i\omega t}
, the transition rate to a continuum of final states is:

\Gamma_{i\to f}=2\pi|\langle f|V|i\rangle|^{2}\rho(E_{f})

(9.74)

where
\rho(E_{f})
is the density of final states at the energy determined by energy conservation:
E_{f}=E_{i}+\omega
(absorption) or
E_{f}=E_{i}-\omega
(emission).

Mathematical Aside: Derivation of Fermi’s Golden Rule

The transition amplitude at time
t
is
c_{f}(t)=-i\langle f|V|i\rangle\frac{e^{i(\omega_{fi}-\omega)t}-1}{i(\omega_{fi}-\omega)}
. The probability
|c_{f}(t)|^{2}\propto\frac{\sin^{2}[(\omega_{fi}-\omega)t/2]}{(\omega_{fi}-\omega)^{2}}
becomes sharply peaked at
\omega_{fi}=\omega
as
t
increases. In the limit
t\to\infty
, this approaches
\pi t\,\delta(\omega_{fi}-\omega)
, giving a constant transition rate.

Example 10

Ionization from a Bound State
Consider a particle initially bound in a one-dimensional well, perturbed by an oscillating electric field that can ionize it into continuum states.

For a particle in the ground state of an infinite square well of width
L
:

\psi_{1}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{L}}\sin\left(\frac{\pi x}{L}\right),\quad E_{1}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2L^{2}}

(9.75)

An oscillating field
V(t)=E_{0}x\cos(\omega t)
can eject the particle into continuum states. For final states with energy
E_{f}=k_{f}^{2}/2
, energy conservation requires:

\omega=E_{f}-E_{1}=\frac{k_{f}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2L^{2}}

(9.76)

The matrix element
\langle k_{f}|x|1\rangle
couples the bound state to the continuum. The density of states for a free particle is
\rho(E)=\frac{L}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{E}}
, and Fermi’s golden rule gives:

\Gamma=2\pi|E_{0}|^{2}|\langle k_{f}|x|1\rangle|^{2}\rho(E_{f})

(9.77)

This ionization rate increases with field strength as
E_{0}^{2}
and depends on the overlap between bound and continuum wave functions.

Example 11

Spontaneous Emission: Hydrogen Lyman-
\alpha

For spontaneous emission, an excited atom decays by emitting a photon into the electromagnetic vacuum. The transition rate for electric dipole radiation is:

A_{fi}=\frac{\omega_{fi}^{3}}{3\pi\epsilon_{0}\hbar c^{3}}|\langle f|\vec{d}|i\rangle|^{2}

(9.78)

where
\vec{d}=e\vec{r}
is the electric dipole operator.

For the Lyman-
\alpha
transition (
2p\to 1s
) in hydrogen:

•

Transition energy:
\hbar\omega=10.2
eV (wavelength 121.6 nm)

•

Dipole matrix element:
|\langle 1s|er|2p\rangle|^{2}=e^{2}\left(\frac{2^{7}}{3^{5}}a_{0}\right)^{2}

The spontaneous emission rate:

A_{2p\to 1s}=\frac{\omega^{3}}{3\pi\epsilon_{0}\hbar c^{3}}\cdot\frac{e^{2}2^{15}a_{0}^{2}}{3^{10}}=6.27\times 10^{8}\text{ s}^{-1}

(9.79)

The lifetime of the
2p
state is
\tau=1/A=1.6
ns.

For comparison, Rydberg states with principal quantum number
n
have:

A_{n}\propto\frac{1}{n^{3}}

(9.80)

A Rydberg state with
n=50
has a lifetime of order milliseconds, a factor of
10^{6}
longer than low-lying states. This suppressed spontaneous emission enables long coherence times in Rydberg-atom quantum computing.

Context:
Spontaneous emission rates determine atomic lifetimes, laser threshold conditions, and quantum efficiency of light sources. The Lyman-
\alpha
line is the most important spectral line in astrophysics, used for mapping cosmic hydrogen distribution and studying the epoch of reionization. In cavity QED, the Purcell effect modifies spontaneous emission rates, enabling single-photon sources for quantum communication.

9.6  The Sudden Approximation

When a perturbation is applied suddenly (faster than characteristic system timescales), the wave function has no time to adjust. We use the sudden approximation.

9.6.1  Overlap Probabilities

If the Hamiltonian suddenly changes from
H_{0}
to
H^{\prime}
at
t=0
, the system’s state
|\psi\rangle
is unchanged, but it is no longer an eigenstate of the new Hamiltonian. The probability of finding the system in eigenstate
|\phi_{n}^{\prime}\rangle
of
H^{\prime}
is:

P_{n}=|\langle\phi_{n}^{\prime}|\psi\rangle|^{2}

(9.81)

Example 12

Sudden Change of Harmonic Oscillator Frequency
Consider a particle in the ground state of a harmonic oscillator with frequency
\omega_{1}
. At
t=0
, the frequency suddenly changes to
\omega_{2}
.

The initial state is:

\psi_{0}^{(1)}(x)=\left(\frac{m\omega_{1}}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\exp\left(-\frac{m\omega_{1}x^{2}}{2\hbar}\right)

(9.82)

The new ground state is:

\psi_{0}^{(2)}(x)=\left(\frac{m\omega_{2}}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\exp\left(-\frac{m\omega_{2}x^{2}}{2\hbar}\right)

(9.83)

The probability of remaining in the ground state is:

P_{0}=|\langle\psi_{0}^{(2)}|\psi_{0}^{(1)}\rangle|^{2}=\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,\psi_{0}^{(2)*}(x)\psi_{0}^{(1)}(x)\right|^{2}

(9.84)

The integral of two Gaussians:

\langle\psi_{0}^{(2)}|\psi_{0}^{(1)}\rangle=\left(\frac{m\omega_{1}}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\left(\frac{m\omega_{2}}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\hbar}{m(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})}}=\frac{2\sqrt{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}

(9.85)

Therefore:

P_{0}=\frac{4\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}{(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})^{2}}

(9.86)

For a small change
\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}(1+\epsilon)
:

P_{0}\approx 1-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4}+O(\epsilon^{3})

(9.87)

For a doubling of frequency (
\omega_{2}=2\omega_{1}
):
P_{0}=8/9\approx 0.89
.

The particle is most likely to remain in the ground state, but there is a finite probability of excitation to higher states (only even states, by parity).

Example 13

Sudden Nuclear Charge Change: Beta Decay
In nuclear
\beta^{-}
decay, a neutron converts to a proton, suddenly increasing the nuclear charge from
Z
to
Z+1
. Consider an electron initially in the ground state of a hydrogen-like atom.

The initial wave function is:

\psi_{1s}^{(Z)}(r)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{Z}{a_{0}}\right)^{3/2}e^{-Zr/a_{0}}

(9.88)

After the decay, the electron finds itself in the Coulomb potential of charge
Z+1
. The probability of remaining in the
1s
state of the new atom:

P_{1s}=|\langle\psi_{1s}^{(Z+1)}|\psi_{1s}^{(Z)}\rangle|^{2}

(9.89)

The overlap integral:

\langle\psi_{1s}^{(Z+1)}|\psi_{1s}^{(Z)}\rangle=\frac{1}{\pi}\left(\frac{Z(Z+1)}{a_{0}^{2}}\right)^{3/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}4\pi r^{2}dr\,e^{-(2Z+1)r/a_{0}}

(9.90)

=4\left(\frac{Z(Z+1)}{a_{0}^{2}}\right)^{3/2}\frac{2a_{0}^{3}}{(2Z+1)^{3}}=\frac{8[Z(Z+1)]^{3/2}}{(2Z+1)^{3}}

(9.91)

For tritium decay (
Z=1\to Z=2
):

P_{1s}=\left(\frac{8(1)(2)^{3/2}}{27}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{16\sqrt{2}}{27}\right)^{2}\approx 0.70

(9.92)

There is a 30% probability that the electron is shaken into an excited state or ionized by the sudden change in nuclear charge.

Context:
The sudden approximation applies to fast quenches in ultracold atomic gases, where trap parameters can be changed faster than atomic motion timescales. In condensed matter, sudden quenches are used to study non-equilibrium dynamics and thermalization. The beta decay calculation was an early confirmation of quantum mechanical overlap predictions.

9.7  The Adiabatic Approximation

When changes occur slowly compared to system dynamics, the system remains in an instantaneous eigenstate. We developed the adiabatic theorem in detail in Section 4.10 of Chapter
4
, including the derivation of the Berry phase for two-level systems. Here we summarize the key results and place them in the context of perturbation theory.

9.7.1  The Adiabatic Theorem

Recall from Section 4.10 that if a system starts in the
n
th eigenstate of
H(0)
and
H(t)
changes slowly enough that transitions to other states are negligible, the system remains in the
n
th instantaneous eigenstate of
H(t)
:

|\psi(t)\rangle=e^{i\gamma_{n}(t)}e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{0}^{t}E_{n}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}|n(t)\rangle

(9.93)

The adiabatic condition requires that the rate of change be slow compared to level spacings:

\hbar\left|\frac{\langle m|\dot{H}|n\rangle}{(E_{n}-E_{m})^{2}}\right|\ll 1\quad\text{for all }m\neq n

(9.94)

This condition can be understood through time-dependent perturbation theory: the transition amplitude to state
|m\rangle
involves the matrix element
\langle m|\dot{H}|n\rangle
divided by the energy denominator
(E_{n}-E_{m})
. When the adiabatic condition is satisfied, these transition amplitudes remain small for all times.

9.7.2  Berry Phase

As derived in Section 4.10.2, the geometric phase
\gamma_{n}(t)
acquired during adiabatic evolution depends only on the path taken through parameter space, not on the rate of traversal. The key result for two-level systems is that the Berry phase equals half the solid angle subtended by the path on the Bloch sphere:

\gamma_{\text{geom}}=-\frac{1}{2}\Omega

(9.95)

where
\Omega
is the solid angle enclosed by the closed path traced by the Hamiltonian’s parameters.

For a general cyclic evolution in parameter space:

\gamma_{n}=i\oint\langle n(R)|\nabla_{R}|n(R)\rangle\cdot dR

(9.96)

This Berry connection
\vec{A}_{n}(R)=i\langle n(R)|\nabla_{R}|n(R)\rangle
plays a role analogous to the vector potential in electromagnetism, with the Berry curvature acting like a magnetic field in parameter space.

Physical Insight

The Berry phase is topological in nature: it depends on the geometry of the parameter space and the structure of eigenstate degeneracies. As we showed in Chapter
4
through the Bloch cube visualization, the factor of
1/2
arises from the spinorial nature of spin-1/2 wavefunctions. The Berry phase underlies phenomena from the Aharonov-Bohm effect to topological insulators.

9.8  Variational Methods

The variational principle provides an alternative approach to approximation that does not require a small parameter.

9.8.1  The Variational Principle

For any normalized trial wave function
|\psi_{\text{trial}}\rangle
:

E_{0}\leq\frac{\langle\psi_{\text{trial}}|H|\psi_{\text{trial}}\rangle}{\langle\psi_{\text{trial}}|\psi_{\text{trial}}\rangle}

(9.97)

where
E_{0}
is the true ground state energy. The equality holds only when
|\psi_{\text{trial}}\rangle
is the exact ground state.

9.8.2  The Rayleigh-Ritz Method

We parameterize trial functions and minimize the energy expectation value with respect to the parameters.

Example 14

Gaussian Trial Function for Harmonic Oscillator
Consider the harmonic oscillator
H=\frac{p^{2}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2}
with a Gaussian trial function:

\psi_{\alpha}(x)=\left(\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{1/4}e^{-\alpha x^{2}}

(9.98)

where
\alpha>0
is the variational parameter.

The kinetic energy:

\langle T\rangle=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,\psi_{\alpha}^{*}\frac{d^{2}\psi_{\alpha}}{dx^{2}}

(9.99)

Using
\frac{d^{2}\psi_{\alpha}}{dx^{2}}=(4\alpha^{2}x^{2}-2\alpha)\psi_{\alpha}
:

\langle T\rangle=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}(2\alpha-4\alpha^{2}\langle x^{2}\rangle)=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\left(2\alpha-4\alpha^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{4\alpha}\right)=\frac{\hbar^{2}\alpha}{2m}

(9.100)

The potential energy:

\langle V\rangle=\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}\langle x^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{4\alpha}=\frac{m\omega^{2}}{8\alpha}

(9.101)

Total energy:

E(\alpha)=\frac{\hbar^{2}\alpha}{2m}+\frac{m\omega^{2}}{8\alpha}

(9.102)

Minimizing:
\frac{dE}{d\alpha}=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}-\frac{m\omega^{2}}{8\alpha^{2}}=0
, giving:

\alpha_{\min}=\frac{m\omega}{2\hbar}

(9.103)

The minimum energy:

E_{\min}=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\cdot\frac{m\omega}{2\hbar}+\frac{m\omega^{2}}{8}\cdot\frac{2\hbar}{m\omega}=\frac{\hbar\omega}{4}+\frac{\hbar\omega}{4}=\frac{\hbar\omega}{2}

(9.104)

This is exactly the ground state energy! The Gaussian trial function with optimal
\alpha
is the exact ground state wave function. This is a special case where the variational method gives the exact answer.

Example 15

Helium Ground State
The helium atom has two electrons in the Coulomb field of a
Z=2
nucleus:

H=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}(\nabla_{1}^{2}+\nabla_{2}^{2})-\frac{2e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{r_{1}}+\frac{1}{r_{2}}\right)+\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{r_{12}}

(9.105)

The electron-electron repulsion makes this problem unsolvable analytically. We use a trial function where each electron occupies a hydrogen-like
1s
orbital with effective charge
Z_{\text{eff}}
:

\psi(r_{1},r_{2})=\frac{Z_{\text{eff}}^{3}}{\pi a_{0}^{3}}e^{-Z_{\text{eff}}(r_{1}+r_{2})/a_{0}}

(9.106)

The effective charge accounts for screening: each electron partially shields the other from the nucleus.

The energy expectation value can be computed analytically:

E(Z_{\text{eff}})=\left(Z_{\text{eff}}^{2}-2Z\cdot Z_{\text{eff}}+\frac{5}{8}Z_{\text{eff}}\right)\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}a_{0}}

(9.107)

For helium (
Z=2
):

E(Z_{\text{eff}})=\left(Z_{\text{eff}}^{2}-4Z_{\text{eff}}+\frac{5}{8}Z_{\text{eff}}\right)\text{Ry}=\left(Z_{\text{eff}}^{2}-\frac{27}{8}Z_{\text{eff}}\right)\text{Ry}

(9.108)

Minimizing:
\frac{dE}{dZ_{\text{eff}}}=2Z_{\text{eff}}-\frac{27}{8}=0
, giving
Z_{\text{eff}}=\frac{27}{16}=1.6875
.

The variational energy:

E_{\min}=-\left(\frac{27}{16}\right)^{2}\text{Ry}=-2.848\text{ Ry}=-77.5\text{ eV}

(9.109)

The experimental value is
-78.975
eV, so the simple one-parameter variational calculation gives 98% of the correct binding energy.

Context:
Variational methods are the foundation of computational quantum chemistry. Hartree-Fock, density functional theory (DFT), and coupled-cluster methods all build on variational principles. Variational quantum eigensolvers (VQE) are leading algorithms for near-term quantum computers, using parameterized quantum circuits as trial wave functions. Tensor network methods in condensed matter physics also rely on variational optimization.

9.9  WKB Approximation

The WKB (Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin) approximation provides semiclassical wave functions valid when the potential varies slowly on the scale of the de Broglie wavelength.

9.9.1  The WKB Wave Function

In classically allowed regions where
E>V(x)
, the local momentum is
p(x)=\sqrt{2m(E-V(x))}
. The WKB wave function is:

\psi(x)\approx\frac{C}{\sqrt{p(x)}}\exp\left(\pm\frac{i}{\hbar}\int^{x}p(x^{\prime})dx^{\prime}\right)

(9.110)

In classically forbidden regions where
E<V(x)
,
p(x)
becomes imaginary:

\psi(x)\approx\frac{C}{\sqrt{|p(x)|}}\exp\left(\pm\frac{1}{\hbar}\int^{x}|p(x^{\prime})|dx^{\prime}\right)

(9.111)

9.9.2  Bohr-Sommerfeld Quantization

For bound states between classical turning points
a
and
b
, the quantization condition is:

\oint p\,dx=2\int_{a}^{b}p(x)\,dx=2\pi\hbar\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(9.112)

The
\frac{1}{2}
arises from connection formulas at the turning points.

Example 16

WKB for the Harmonic Oscillator
For
V(x)=\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2}
, the classical turning points at energy
E
are
x=\pm\sqrt{2E/m\omega^{2}}
.

The local momentum:
p(x)=\sqrt{2m(E-\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2})}=\sqrt{2mE}\sqrt{1-\frac{m\omega^{2}x^{2}}{2E}}
.

The quantization integral:

\int_{-a}^{a}p(x)\,dx=\sqrt{2mE}\int_{-a}^{a}\sqrt{1-x^{2}/a^{2}}\,dx=\sqrt{2mE}\cdot\frac{\pi a}{2}

(9.113)

where
a=\sqrt{2E/m\omega^{2}}
.

Substituting:

\sqrt{2mE}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2E}{m\omega^{2}}}=\frac{\pi E}{\omega}

(9.114)

The quantization condition
\frac{2\pi E}{\omega}=2\pi\hbar(n+\frac{1}{2})
gives:

E_{n}=\hbar\omega\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(9.115)

WKB gives the exact harmonic oscillator spectrum! This is another special case where the semiclassical approximation is exact.

Example 17

WKB for Hydrogen: Radial Quantization
Working in atomic units, the radial Schrödinger equation for hydrogen can be written as a one-dimensional problem:

-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{2}u}{dr^{2}}+V_{\text{eff}}(r)u=Eu

(9.116)

where
u=rR(r)
and

V_{\text{eff}}(r)=-\frac{1}{r}+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2r^{2}}

(9.117)

The classical turning points
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
satisfy
E=V_{\text{eff}}(r)
. The WKB quantization:

\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\sqrt{2(E-V_{\text{eff}}(r))}\,dr=\pi\left(n_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(9.118)

where
n_{r}=0,1,2,\ldots
is the radial quantum number.

This integral can be evaluated using contour integration. The result is:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2E}}-\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right)=\left(n_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(9.119)

Defining
n=n_{r}+\ell+1
:

E_{n}=-\frac{1}{2n^{2}}

(9.120)

which is the exact hydrogen spectrum in atomic units (where the ground state energy is
-1/2
Hartree
=-1
Rydberg).

WKB reproduces the exact hydrogen spectrum.

Context:
Semiclassical methods bridge quantum and classical descriptions, essential for understanding the quantum-classical correspondence. WKB tunneling rates govern alpha decay, scanning tunneling microscopy, and Josephson junction dynamics. The method extends to classically chaotic systems through trace formulas connecting periodic orbits to quantum spectra.

9.10  The Complete Hydrogen Atom

Having developed perturbation theory techniques, we now apply them systematically to understand the real hydrogen atom beyond the idealized Coulomb solution. This synthesis demonstrates how perturbation theory connects textbook quantum mechanics to precision atomic physics.

9.10.1  The Hierarchy of Energy Scales

The hydrogen atom exhibits a beautiful hierarchy of energy scales, each probing different physics:

Effect

Energy Scale

Origin

Gross structure

\text{Ry}\approx 13.6
eV

Coulomb interaction

Fine structure

\alpha^{2}\text{Ry}\sim 10^{-4}
eV

Relativity + spin-orbit

Lamb shift

\alpha^{3}\text{Ry}\sim 10^{-6}
eV

QED vacuum fluctuations

Hyperfine structure

(m_{e}/m_{p})\alpha^{2}\text{Ry}\sim 10^{-7}
eV

Nuclear magnetic moment

Here
\alpha=e^{2}/(4\pi\epsilon_{0}\hbar c)\approx 1/137
is the fine structure constant.

Physical Insight

The hierarchy arises because different interactions involve different combinations of fundamental constants. The fine structure constant
\alpha
controls relativistic corrections, the electron-to-proton mass ratio governs nuclear effects, and higher powers of
\alpha
indicate quantum electrodynamic corrections. This pattern of hierarchical scales, where each level of structure involves smaller energy scales, recurs throughout physics and underlies the effective field theory approach to modern particle physics.

9.10.2  Fine Structure in Detail

The fine structure Hamiltonian consists of three terms:

H_{\text{fine}}=H_{\text{rel}}+H_{SO}+H_{\text{Darwin}}

(9.121)

1. Relativistic kinetic energy correction:

The relativistic kinetic energy
T=\sqrt{p^{2}c^{2}+m^{2}c^{4}}-mc^{2}
expands as:

T=\frac{p^{2}}{2m}-\frac{p^{4}}{8m^{3}c^{2}}+\cdots

(9.122)

The first-order perturbation:

E_{\text{rel}}^{(1)}=-\frac{1}{2mc^{2}}\langle\psi_{n\ell m}|\frac{p^{4}}{4m^{2}}|\psi_{n\ell m}\rangle

(9.123)

Using
p^{2}/2m=E_{n}-V(r)
and the known expectation values
\langle r^{-1}\rangle
and
\langle r^{-2}\rangle
:

E_{\text{rel}}^{(1)}=-\frac{E_{n}^{2}}{2mc^{2}}\left(\frac{4n}{\ell+1/2}-3\right)

(9.124)

2. Spin-orbit coupling:

From the electron’s rest frame, the nucleus appears to orbit, creating a magnetic field that couples to the electron spin:

H_{SO}=\frac{1}{2m_{e}^{2}c^{2}}\frac{1}{r}\frac{dV}{dr}\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}=\frac{e^{2}}{8\pi\epsilon_{0}m_{e}^{2}c^{2}r^{3}}\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}

(9.125)

Using the coupled basis
|n,\ell,j,m_{j}\rangle
where
\vec{L}\cdot\vec{S}=\frac{1}{2}(J^{2}-L^{2}-S^{2})
:

E_{SO}^{(1)}=\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi\epsilon_{0}m_{e}^{2}c^{2}}\langle r^{-3}\rangle_{n\ell}[j(j+1)-\ell(\ell+1)-\tfrac{3}{4}]\hbar^{2}

(9.126)

For
\ell>0
, this gives different energies for
j=\ell+\frac{1}{2}
and
j=\ell-\frac{1}{2}
.

3. Darwin term:

The Darwin term arises from the “zitterbewegung” of the electron (rapid oscillation at the Compton wavelength scale) and affects only
s
-states:

H_{\text{Darwin}}=\frac{\pi\hbar^{2}}{2m_{e}^{2}c^{2}}\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\delta^{3}(\vec{r})

(9.127)

For
s
-states:
E_{\text{Darwin}}^{(1)}=\frac{\pi\hbar^{2}e^{2}}{8\pi\epsilon_{0}m_{e}^{2}c^{2}}|\psi_{n00}(0)|^{2}
.

Combined fine structure:

Remarkably, all three contributions combine to give a simple formula that depends only on
n
and
j
:

E_{\text{fine}}=-\frac{\alpha^{2}\,\text{Ry}}{n^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{j+1/2}-\frac{3}{4n}\right)

(9.128)

where
\text{Ry}\approx 13.6
eV is the Rydberg energy. This is the Sommerfeld fine structure formula. Using
E_{n}=-\text{Ry}/n^{2}
, it can equivalently be written
E_{\text{fine}}=(\alpha^{2}E_{n}/n)\left[1/(j+1/2)-3/(4n)\right]
, which is negative because
E_{n}<0
.

For
n=2
:

•

2s_{1/2}
(
\ell=0
,
j=1/2
):
E_{\text{fine}}=-\frac{5\alpha^{2}}{64}\,\text{Ry}

•

2p_{1/2}
(
\ell=1
,
j=1/2
):
E_{\text{fine}}=-\frac{5\alpha^{2}}{64}\,\text{Ry}

•

2p_{3/2}
(
\ell=1
,
j=3/2
):
E_{\text{fine}}=-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{64}\,\text{Ry}

The
2s_{1/2}
and
2p_{1/2}
states are predicted to be degenerate, a degeneracy broken only by the Lamb shift, which requires quantum electrodynamics.

Context:
The fine structure constant
\alpha
governs the strength of electromagnetic interactions and appears throughout atomic physics. Spin-orbit coupling is the mechanism underlying spintronic devices and topological insulators. In artificial atoms (quantum dots), the “fine structure” splitting is tunable, enabling engineered spin-photon interfaces for quantum networks.

9.10.3  The Zeeman Effect: Weak, Strong, and Intermediate Fields

When hydrogen is placed in a magnetic field, the choice of perturbative approach depends on the field strength relative to fine structure splitting.

Weak-field Zeeman effect (Anomalous Zeeman effect):

When
\mu_{B}B\ll E_{\text{fine}}
, the fine structure dominates. We treat
H_{\text{Zeeman}}=\frac{\mu_{B}}{\hbar}(L_{z}+2S_{z})B
as a perturbation on states labeled by
|n,\ell,j,m_{j}\rangle
.

Since
J_{z}=L_{z}+S_{z}
but not
L_{z}
and
S_{z}
individually are good quantum numbers, we use:

\langle L_{z}+2S_{z}\rangle=\langle J_{z}\rangle+\langle S_{z}\rangle=\hbar m_{j}+\langle S_{z}\rangle

(9.129)

The projection theorem gives
\langle S_{z}\rangle=m_{j}\hbar\cdot\frac{j(j+1)+s(s+1)-\ell(\ell+1)}{2j(j+1)}
.

The energy shift:

E_{\text{Zeeman}}^{(1)}=\mu_{B}Bm_{j}g_{j}

(9.130)

where the Land’e
g
-factor is:

g_{j}=1+\frac{j(j+1)+s(s+1)-\ell(\ell+1)}{2j(j+1)}

(9.131)

For
j=\ell+\frac{1}{2}
:
g_{j}=1+\frac{1}{2\ell+1}
.
For
j=\ell-\frac{1}{2}
:
g_{j}=1-\frac{1}{2\ell+1}
.

Strong-field Zeeman effect (Paschen-Back effect):

When
\mu_{B}B\gg E_{\text{fine}}
, the magnetic field dominates. The good quantum numbers are
m_{\ell}
and
m_{s}
(uncoupled basis), and fine structure becomes a perturbation:

E=E_{n}+\mu_{B}B(m_{\ell}+2m_{s})+E_{\text{fine}}^{(1)}(m_{\ell},m_{s})

(9.132)

Intermediate fields:

When
\mu_{B}B\sim E_{\text{fine}}
, neither basis diagonalizes the total perturbation. We must diagonalize the combined
H_{\text{fine}}+H_{\text{Zeeman}}
within the degenerate subspace.

Example 18

n=2
Hydrogen in an Intermediate Magnetic Field
Consider the
n=2
states (ignoring the
2p_{3/2}
for simplicity). The
2s_{1/2}
and
2p_{1/2}
states each have
m_{j}=\pm 1/2
.

In the basis
\{|2s_{1/2},+\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle,|2s_{1/2},-\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle,|2p_{1/2},+\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle,|2p_{1/2},-\tfrac{1}{2}\rangle\}
, the total perturbation matrix is:

H^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}E_{s}+\mu_{B}Bg_{s}\cdot\frac{1}{2}&0&0&0\\
0&E_{s}-\mu_{B}Bg_{s}\cdot\frac{1}{2}&0&0\\
0&0&E_{p}+\mu_{B}Bg_{p}\cdot\frac{1}{2}&0\\
0&0&0&E_{p}-\mu_{B}Bg_{p}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}

(9.133)

where
g_{s}=2
(pure spin) and
g_{p}=2/3
for
2p_{1/2}
.

Without the Lamb shift,
E_{s}=E_{p}
, and states with the same
m_{j}
would cross. The Lamb shift (
E_{s}>E_{p}
by about 1058 MHz) creates an avoided crossing, and at intermediate fields, states are superpositions of
s
and
p
character.

Context:
Magneto-optical traps (MOTs) exploit Zeeman shifts combined with laser detuning to create position-dependent forces that cool and trap atoms. This technique launched the ultracold atom revolution. Zeeman slowers decelerate atomic beams for loading MOTs. In astrophysics, the Paschen-Back regime is relevant for white dwarf and neutron star atmospheres, where magnetic fields reach
10^{8}
–
10^{11}
T.

9.10.4  Hyperfine Structure

The proton has a magnetic moment
\vec{\mu}_{p}=g_{p}\frac{e}{2m_{p}}\vec{I}
where
\vec{I}
is the nuclear spin (
I=1/2
for hydrogen) and
g_{p}\approx 5.59
. This creates a magnetic field that couples to the electron spin.

The hyperfine Hamiltonian is:

H_{\text{hf}}=A\vec{I}\cdot\vec{S}

(9.134)

where
A
depends on the electron wave function at the nucleus.

For the ground state:

A_{1s}=\frac{2}{3}\mu_{0}g_{p}\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi m_{e}m_{p}}|\psi_{1s}(0)|^{2}=\frac{8}{3}g_{p}\frac{m_{e}}{m_{p}}\alpha^{2}E_{1}

(9.135)

Coupling
\vec{I}
and
\vec{S}
gives total spin
\vec{F}=\vec{I}+\vec{S}
:

\vec{I}\cdot\vec{S}=\frac{1}{2}(F^{2}-I^{2}-S^{2})=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}[F(F+1)-I(I+1)-S(S+1)]

(9.136)

For hydrogen (
I=S=1/2
),
F=0
or
F=1
:

\displaystyle F=1

\displaystyle:\quad E_{\text{hf}}=+\frac{A\hbar^{2}}{4}

(9.137)

\displaystyle F=0

\displaystyle:\quad E_{\text{hf}}=-\frac{3A\hbar^{2}}{4}

(9.138)

The hyperfine splitting of the ground state:

\Delta E_{\text{hf}}=A\hbar^{2}=5.87\times 10^{-6}\text{ eV}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nu=1420.4\text{ MHz}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda=21\text{ cm}

(9.139)

Context:
The 21 cm hydrogen line is radio astronomy’s most important spectral line. It maps the distribution of neutral hydrogen throughout the universe, probes the cosmic “dark ages” before the first stars, and tests cosmological models. Closer to home, the hyperfine transition in cesium-133 defines the SI second, and hyperfine states in trapped ions (such as
171
Yb
+
and
133
Ba
+
) serve as qubit states in leading quantum computing platforms. The hydrogen maser, based on stimulated emission of the 21 cm transition, remains one of the most stable frequency references available.

9.10.5  Summary: When Different Methods Apply

The hydrogen atom illustrates how to choose among perturbative approaches:

Situation

Method

Non-degenerate level, small perturbation

Standard perturbation theory

Degenerate level, perturbation breaks degeneracy

Degenerate perturbation theory

Competing perturbations of similar size

Full matrix diagonalization

Perturbation commutes with some
H_{0}
eigenbasis

Choose that basis

Oscillating perturbation, transitions

Time-dependent perturbation theory

Transition to continuum

Fermi’s golden rule

Fast change

Sudden approximation

Slow change

Adiabatic theorem

No small parameter

Variational method

The art of perturbation theory lies in recognizing which regime applies and choosing the appropriate basis to simplify calculations.

9.11  Chapter Summary

Perturbation theory provides powerful tools for approximate solutions throughout quantum mechanics and beyond. Time-independent perturbation theory handles energy shifts and state mixing in static systems, with first-order shifts determined by expectation values and second-order shifts by virtual transitions to intermediate states. Degenerate perturbation theory addresses situations when symmetries are broken, requiring diagonalization within the degenerate subspace to find the appropriate zeroth-order states and good quantum numbers. Time-dependent perturbation theory describes transitions between states driven by external fields. Fermi’s golden rule gives transition rates to continua of final states, underlying phenomena from spontaneous emission to scattering. The sudden and adiabatic approximations handle the limiting cases of fast and slow parameter changes. Variational methods provide upper bounds on ground state energies without requiring a small expansion parameter. The WKB approximation treats semiclassical systems with slowly varying potentials, connecting quantum mechanics to classical trajectories.

The hydrogen atom, with its hierarchy of corrections from gross structure through fine structure to hyperfine structure, exemplifies how these methods work together. Choice of basis (coupled versus uncoupled, weak-field versus strong-field) reflects physical insight about which quantum numbers remain good under the perturbation at hand. These same principles extend to multi-electron atoms, molecules, solids, and nuclei, making perturbation theory one of the most widely used tools in theoretical physics.

9.12  Problems

Note:
Throughout these problems, we use atomic units (
\hbar=m_{e}=e=1
) unless otherwise specified.

Problem 9.1

(Non-Degenerate Perturbation Theory: Infinite Square Well)

A particle in the ground state of an infinite square well of width
L
(walls at
x=0
and
x=L
) experiences a perturbation
V(x)=V_{0}\sin(\pi x/L)
.

(a)

Calculate the first-order energy correction
E_{1}^{(1)}
for the ground state.

(b)

Calculate the first-order energy correction
E_{2}^{(1)}
for the first excited state. Why is this qualitatively different from part (a)?

(c)

Calculate the second-order energy correction to the ground state energy. Which excited state contributes most?

(d)

For what value of
V_{0}
relative to
E_{1}^{(0)}
does perturbation theory break down? Give a quantitative criterion.

Problem 9.2

(Non-Degenerate Perturbation Theory: Anharmonic Oscillator)

A harmonic oscillator has a cubic perturbation:
H=p^{2}/(2m)+m\omega^{2}x^{2}/2+\lambda x^{3}
. Use
x=(x_{0}/\sqrt{2})(a+a^{\dagger})
with
x_{0}=\sqrt{\hbar/(m\omega)}
.

(a)

Show that
E_{n}^{(1)}=\lambda\langle n|x^{3}|n\rangle
vanishes for every
n
.

(b)

Calculate
(a+a^{\dagger})^{3}|0\rangle
iteratively.

(c)

Compute the matrix elements
\langle 1|x^{3}|0\rangle
and
\langle 3|x^{3}|0\rangle
.

(d)

Calculate the second-order ground state energy correction
E_{0}^{(2)}
and express it in terms of
\lambda
,
x_{0}
, and
\hbar\omega
.

(e)

Although the potential is unbounded below for large
|x|
, the perturbation series gives useful results for small
\lambda
. Explain why physically.

Problem 9.3

(Quadratic Stark Effect in Hydrogen)

The hydrogen ground state
|1s\rangle
(energy
E_{1}=-1/2
in atomic units) is placed in a uniform electric field
\mathcal{E}
along
z
, giving perturbation
V=\mathcal{E}z
.

(a)

Show that
E_{1}^{(1)}=\mathcal{E}\langle 1s|z|1s\rangle=0
. What symmetry is responsible?

(b)

The second-order correction is
E_{1}^{(2)}=\sum_{n\ell m}|\mathcal{E}\langle n,\ell,m|z|1s\rangle|^{2}/(E_{1}-E_{n})
. Explain why only
p
-states (
\ell=1
,
m=0
) contribute. Cite two selection rules.

(c)

The exact result is
E_{1}^{(2)}=-\frac{9}{4}\mathcal{E}^{2}
, giving polarizability
\alpha_{H}=9/2
in atomic units. Express
\alpha_{H}
in SI units and calculate its numerical value.

(d)

Show that replacing all
E_{n}
in the denominator with
E_{2}
gives an upper bound on
|E_{1}^{(2)}|
. What polarizability does this yield, and how does it compare to the exact value?

Problem 9.4

(Degenerate Perturbation Theory: Four-State System)

A system with four states and unperturbed energies
E_{1}^{(0)}=E_{3}^{(0)}=0
and
E_{2}^{(0)}=E_{4}^{(0)}=10
has perturbation matrix:

V\;\approx\;\begin{pmatrix}2&0.3&1&0\\
0.3&-1&0&2\\
1&0&-2&0.5\\
0&2&0.5&3\end{pmatrix}

(a)

Identify the two degenerate subspaces and extract the
2\times 2
perturbation submatrices.

(b)

Diagonalize the
E^{(0)}=0
subspace to find
E_{\pm}^{(1)}
and the correct zeroth-order eigenstates.

(c)

Diagonalize the
E^{(0)}=10
subspace to find its first-order corrections.

(d)

Sketch the energy level diagram showing how the two degenerate pairs split. Are any levels still degenerate?

(e)

Estimate whether the second-order corrections from off-diagonal elements between subspaces are negligible.

Problem 9.5

(Two Coupled Spins with Perturbation)

Two spin-1/2 particles interact via
H=J\vec{S}_{1}\cdot\vec{S}_{2}+\epsilon S_{1z}
where
\epsilon\ll J
is a perturbation.

(a)

Show that the unperturbed energies are
E_{\text{singlet}}=-3J/4
and
E_{\text{triplet}}=J/4
(three-fold degenerate).

(b)

Construct the
3\times 3
perturbation matrix for the triplet subspace using the Clebsch-Gordan decomposition of triplet states.

(c)

Diagonalize this matrix to find the first-order splittings of the three triplet states.

(d)

Does the perturbation mix singlet and triplet? Calculate
\langle 0,0|S_{1z}|1,0\rangle
to check. At what order in perturbation theory does this mixing enter?

Problem 9.6

(Linear Stark Effect in Hydrogen
n=2
)

The
n=2
level of hydrogen (ignoring spin) is 4-fold degenerate:
|2s\rangle
,
|2p_{z}\rangle
,
|2p_{+}\rangle
,
|2p_{-}\rangle
. An electric field
\mathcal{E}
along
z
gives perturbation
V=\mathcal{E}z
.

(a)

Using selection rules
\Delta m=0
and
\Delta\ell=\pm 1
, show that the only nonzero matrix element within
n=2
is
\langle 2s|z|2p_{z}\rangle
.

(b)

Verify that
\langle 2s|z|2p_{z}\rangle=-3
(atomic units) using the given radial wave functions
R_{20}(r)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}(2-r)e^{-r/2}
and
R_{21}(r)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}re^{-r/2}
.

(c)

Write out the full
4\times 4
perturbation matrix. Show that it is block-diagonal in
m
.

(d)

Find the four first-order energy corrections and the correct zeroth-order eigenstates.

(e)

The states
|\pm\rangle=(|2s\rangle\mp|2p_{z}\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
are
sp
-hybrid orbitals with permanent dipole moments. Calculate
\langle\pm|z|\pm\rangle
. How is this possible for parity eigenstates?

Problem 9.7

(Deriving Fermi’s Golden Rule)

For a constant perturbation
V
suddenly switched on at
t=0
, the first-order transition probability is:

P_{i\to f}(t)=\frac{|\langle f|V|i\rangle|^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\frac{\sin^{2}(\omega_{fi}t/2)}{(\omega_{fi}/2)^{2}}

where
\omega_{fi}=(E_{f}-E_{i})/\hbar
.

(a)

Sketch
P_{i\to f}
as a function of
\omega_{fi}
for fixed
t
. What is the peak height and width? How do these scale with
t
?

(b)

For a continuum of final states with density
\rho(E_{f})
, show that the total probability is
P(t)=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}|\langle f|V|i\rangle|^{2}\rho(E_{i})t
by using
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}(xt/2)}{(x/2)^{2}}dx=2\pi t
.

(c)

Derive the transition rate
\Gamma=dP/dt
(Fermi’s golden rule):
\Gamma=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}|\langle f|V|i\rangle|^{2}\rho(E_{i})
. Explain why a constant rate requires a continuum.

(d)

Show that Fermi’s golden rule requires both
t
large (sinc-squared acts like delta function) and
t
small (
P\ll 1
), but these conditions are compatible when
\Gamma\ll\Delta E/\hbar
.

Problem 9.8

(Sudden and Adiabatic: Harmonic Oscillator Frequency Change)

A harmonic oscillator in its ground state with frequency
\omega_{1}
suddenly changes to frequency
\omega_{2}=4\omega_{1}
.

(a)

Sudden limit:
Calculate the probability of remaining in the ground state and the probability of reaching the first excited state. Use parity to argue that only even-
n
final states are accessible.

(b)

Adiabatic limit:
What is the final-state probability and energy? What is the adiabatic phase?

(c)

The energy expectation immediately after the change is
\langle H^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\psi_{0}^{(1)}|H^{\prime}|\psi_{0}^{(1)}\rangle
where
H^{\prime}=p^{2}/(2m)+m\omega_{2}^{2}x^{2}/2
. Calculate
\langle H^{\prime}\rangle
in both limits and interpret.

(d)

Prove that in the sudden case, the particle can only be found in even-
n
states of the new oscillator (parity conservation).

Problem 9.9

(Variational Method: Helium Ground State)

Two electrons in helium interact via
H=-\nabla_{1}^{2}/2-\nabla_{2}^{2}/2-2/r_{1}-2/r_{2}+1/r_{12}
(atomic units).

(a)

Use a trial wave function
\psi(r_{1},r_{2})=(Z^{3}/\pi)^{1/2}e^{-Zr_{1}}(Z^{3}/\pi)^{1/2}e^{-Zr_{2}}
where
Z
is the variational parameter. Calculate
\langle H\rangle
as a function of
Z
.

(b)

Show that the energy is
E(Z)=-2Z^{2}+(5/8)Z
(the electron-electron repulsion integral yields
5Z/8
).

(c)

Minimize to find the optimal
Z_{\text{eff}}
and the resulting ground state energy. Compare to the experimental value of
-5.81
eV.

(d)

Explain physically why
Z_{\text{eff}}<2
. What does the second term in
E(Z)
represent?

Problem 9.10

(WKB Approximation: Tunneling and Quantization)

(a)

For a potential
V(x)=|x|
(linear potential), find the WKB quantization condition
\oint p(x)dx=2\pi n\hbar
for bound states. What are the allowed energies?

(b)

For potential
V(x)=|x|^{\alpha}
with
\alpha>0
, calculate the leading-order WKB spectrum and identify which potentials give equally spaced levels.

(c)

A particle with energy
E
incident on a step potential
V(x)=V_{0}
(for
x>0
) has WKB transmission coefficient
T\approx e^{-2\gamma}
where
\gamma=\int_{0}^{d}\sqrt{2m(V_{0}-E)}/\hbar\,dx
is the tunneling exponent. For a rectangular barrier of height
V_{0}=5
eV and width
d=1
Å, calculate the transmission for
E=4
eV.

Problem 9.11

(Born Approximation and Scattering Amplitude)

The scattering amplitude in the Born approximation is
f(\vec{q})=-\frac{m}{4\pi}(2\pi)^{3}\tilde{V}(\vec{q})
where
\tilde{V}
is the Fourier transform of the potential and
\vec{q}=\vec{k}_{i}-\vec{k}_{f}
is the momentum transfer.

(a)

For a Yukawa potential
V(r)=-\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi}e^{-\mu r}/r
, calculate the Fourier transform
\tilde{V}(\vec{q})
.

(b)

Find the scattering amplitude
f(\theta)
and the differential cross section
d\sigma/d\Omega=|f(\theta)|^{2}
in terms of the scattering angle
\theta
.

(c)

For low-energy scattering (
\mu\ll k
), show that the
s
-wave phase shift is
\delta_{0}\approx-ka_{s}
where
a_{s}
is the scattering length. Relate
a_{s}
to the potential.

Problem 9.12

(Rotating Wave Approximation and Rabi Oscillations)

A two-level atom with transition frequency
\omega_{0}
is driven by an oscillating field
\vec{E}(t)=E_{0}\cos(\omega t)\hat{z}
, producing perturbation
V(t)=-dE_{0}\cos(\omega t)
where
d
is the dipole moment.

(a)

Decompose
V(t)
into rotating and counter-rotating terms:
V=V^{\text{rot}}e^{-i\omega t}+\text{c.c.}
plus counter-rotating terms oscillating at
2\omega
.

(b)

In the rotating frame with
U=e^{-i\omega_{0}t\sigma_{z}/2}
, show that the counter-rotating terms oscillate at
2\omega
(far off-resonance) and can be dropped if
|\omega-\omega_{0}|\ll\omega
. This is the rotating wave approximation.

(c)

In the RWA, the Hamiltonian becomes
H_{\text{RWA}}=\Delta\sigma_{z}/2+\Omega\sigma_{x}
where
\Delta=\omega-\omega_{0}
is the detuning and
\Omega=dE_{0}
is the Rabi frequency.

(d)

For resonant driving (
\Delta=0
), show that the population oscillates between states:
P_{e}(t)=\sin^{2}(\Omega t/2)
. At what time does the system achieve maximum transition probability (a
\pi
pulse)?

, 3rd ed. Cambridge University Press, 2017. Chapter 5 covers time-independent and time-dependent perturbation theory at the level of this chapter; the standard graduate reference for both the formalism and the hydrogen fine-structure application.

Quantum Mechanics
, Vol. 2. Wiley-VCH, 1977. Chapters XI–XIII work through stationary, degenerate, and time-dependent perturbation theory with worked examples; the most thorough textbook treatment, particularly clear on degenerate perturbation theory and good quantum numbers.

Born, M., and Fock, V.

Zeitschrift für Physik

51
, 165–180 (1928).
doi:10.1007/BF01343193
. The original proof of the adiabatic theorem; the pre-Berry foundation for slow parameter changes.

“Quantal phase factors accompanying adiabatic changes.”
Proceedings of the Royal Society A
